# Executive Summaries: Key Questions in Congenital Cardiac Surgery

## Chapter 1: Congenital Cardiac Anatomy

### Embryological Origins

*   **Primary heart field:** Forms the linear heart tube within the pericardial cavity; responsible for forming the left ventricle and part of the ventricular septum.
*   **Secondary heart field:** Invades the venous and arterial poles; forms the atrioventricular canal, primary atrial chamber, right ventricle primordium, and outflow tract.
*   **Ballooning:** Atrial appendages balloon in parallel; ventricular pouches balloon in series (inlet → left ventricle, outlet → right ventricle).
*   **Primary vs chamber myocardium:** Primary tube walls are primary myocardium; definitive chambers form from secondary (chamber) myocardium.

### Heterotaxy and Isomerism

*   **Heterotaxy:** Abnormal arrangement of thoracic/abdominal organs across the left-right axis.
*   **Isomerism:** Right- and left-sided structures are mirror images within the same individual (e.g., bilateral right atrial appendages).
*   **Only atrial appendages** show differential response to laterality genes (Cited2, Pitx2 on left; Lefty2, Sonic hedgehog prevent right-sided expression).
*   **Atrioventricular connections are always mixed** when atrial appendages are isomeric.

### Ventricular Looping and Topology

*   **Rightward looping** (normal) → right-handed topology: palmar surface of right hand on septum (thumb in inlet, fingers in outlet).
*   **Leftward looping** → left-handed topology: palmar surface of left hand on septum.
*   **Right-handed topology** with usual atrial arrangement and concordant AV connections.
*   **Left-handed topology** with mirror-imaged atrial arrangement and concordant AV connections.
*   **Discordant AV connections** → opposite topology.

### Atrial Septum Development

*   **Septum primum:** Grows from atrial roof with mesenchymal cap; primary foramen closes as cap fuses with AV cushions.
*   **Secondary foramen:** Forms as upper edge of primary septum breaks away.
*   **Vestibular spine:** Develops from dorsal mesocardium; muscularises with mesenchymal cap to form inferoanterior buttress of oval fossa.
*   **Oval fossa floor** = primary septum; **superior rim** = infolding of atrial roof (superior interatrial fold).

### Atrial Septal Defects vs Interatrial Communications

| True ASD | Interatrial Communication (Extraseptal) |
|----------|----------------------------------------|
| Oval fossa (secundum) defects | Sinus venosus defects (with anomalous pulmonary veins) |
| Vestibular defects (rare) | Coronary sinus defects |
| — | Ostium primum defect (AVSD, not true ASD) |

### Atrioventricular Septal Defect (AVSD)

*   **Fundamental features:** Common atrioventricular junction + trifoliate left atrioventricular valve.
*   **Left valve is NOT a "cleft mitral valve"** — it is trifoliate, not bifoliate.
*   **Bridging leaflets** derived from superior and inferior AV cushions.
*   Shunting can be atrial, ventricular, or both depending on leaflet fusion.

### Ventricular Septation

*   **Membranous septum:** Formed by fusion of tubercles from AV cushions to close the tertiary interventricular communication.
*   **No muscular outlet septum** in normal heart — subpulmonary infundibulum is a freestanding sleeve.
*   **Supracristal VSD** = doubly committed juxta-arterial defect; fibrous continuity between aortic and pulmonary valves.

### Tetralogy of Fallot

*   **Four features:** Interventricular communication, biventricular aortic connection, subpulmonary stenosis, right ventricular hypertrophy.
*   **Key developmental abnormality:** Combination of anterocephalad malalignment of muscular outlet septum + anomalous (hypertrophied) septoparietal trabeculations.

### Double-Outlet Right Ventricle (DORV)

*   Defined when **larger part of both arterial roots** is supported by morphologically right ventricle.
*   Surgical determinant: Whether the right ventricular border of the overriding root can be closed without tunnelling.

### Functionally Univentricular Heart

*   **Univentricular AV connections:** Double-inlet left ventricle (DILV) or absence of right/left AV connection (e.g., tricuspid atresia).
*   **Biventricular AV connections with small ventricle:** HLHS, PA-IVS with hypoplastic RV, unbalanced AVSD.

### Tricuspid Atresia

*   Two phenotypes: **Imperforate valve** (hypoplastic RV with inlet) vs **absence of right AV connection** (incomplete RV without inlet).
*   Commonest variant: Absence of right AV connection with left atrium connected to dominant left ventricle.

### Common Arterial Trunk

*   **Essential feature:** Commonality of the ventriculo-arterial junction (persistence of undivided embryonic conus).
*   **Simplified classification:** Aortic dominance vs pulmonary dominance (latter associated with arch interruption).

### Transposition

*   **Complete transposition:** Discordant VA connections (aorta from RV, pulmonary trunk from LV) with concordant AV connections.
*   **Congenitally corrected transposition:** Discordant VA + discordant AV connections (double discordance); circulations in series.

### Coronary Artery Classification (Leiden)

*   Observer stands in **non-adjacent sinus** looking towards adjacent sinuses.
*   **Sinus #1:** Right hand of observer; **Sinus #2:** Left hand.
*   Normal: 1R, 2LCx; Transposition commonest: 1LCx, 2R.

### Aortopulmonary Septum

*   Derived from **dorsal wall of aortic sac**, protrudes into distal outflow tract.
*   Fuses with distal ends of major outflow cushions to close aortopulmonary foramen.
*   Failure of fusion → aortopulmonary window; failure of major cushion fusion → common arterial trunk.

### Vascular Rings

*   Explained by **Edwards hypothetical double aortic arch** encircling tracheo-oesophageal pedicle.
*   Derived from arteries of 3rd, 4th, and pulmonary arches (bilaterally symmetrical in early development).
*   Right-sided components normally attenuate.

### Arterial Duct

*   Derived from **left pulmonary arch artery** (previously called "6th arch").
*   Right pulmonary arch artery normally regresses.
*   **Isthmus:** Segment between left subclavian artery and duct insertion (cannot be defined until subclavian migrates cranially).

### Conduction System

*   **Sinus node:** Epicardial aspect of terminal groove at superior cavoatrial junction, inferior to right atrial appendage crest. No specialised internodal tracts — ordinary working myocardium with parallel alignment conducts preferentially.
*   **AV node:** Apex of triangle of Koch (borders: Eustachian valve extension, tricuspid septal leaflet attachment, membranous septum).
*   **AV bundle (His):** Passes through membranous septum to crest of muscular septum; branches into right and left bundle branches.
*   **Landmark:** Line from apex of triangle of Koch to medial papillary muscle (muscle of Lancisi) shows course of conduction axis.

## Chapter 2: Congenital Cardiac Physiology

### Foetal Circulation

*   **Key difference from adult:** Gas exchange occurs in the placenta, not the lungs.
*   **Oxygenated blood (70%):** Umbilical vein → ductus venosus (bypasses liver) → IVC → RA → foramen ovale → LA → LV → ascending aorta (coronaries, head vessels).
*   **Deoxygenated blood (40%):** SVC → RA → RV → PA → ductus arteriosus → descending aorta → placenta via umbilical arteries.
*   **Four shunts:** Placenta (low resistance), ductus venosus, foramen ovale, ductus arteriosus.

### Transition at Birth

*   **Lung expansion + high alveolar PO₂** → sharp decline in pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR).
*   **Ductus arteriosus closure:** Smooth muscle contraction (O₂, ↓PGE₂) → permanent after 3 weeks (endothelial/subintimal changes).
*   **Foramen ovale closure:** LA pressure rises as pulmonary blood flow increases → passive closure; PFO in ~20% (no significant L→R shunt).
*   **Ductus venosus closure:** Sphincter closes, forcing blood through liver.

### Ductus Arteriosus

*   **Duct-dependent circulations:**
    *   **Left-sided obstruction (coarctation):** R→L ductal flow maintains lower body perfusion (differential cyanosis).
    *   **Right-sided obstruction (pulmonary atresia):** Retrograde ductal flow fills pulmonary arteries.
    *   **Transposition:** Ductal patency aids mixing between parallel circulations.
*   **Regulation:**
    *   **Closure:** Indomethacin/ibuprofen (cyclooxygenase inhibitors) promote closure in preterms.
    *   **Patency:** Prostaglandin E₁ infusion (0.005–0.1 μg/kg/min) maintains ductal patency.

### Pulmonary Vascular Resistance

*   **Normal development:**
    *   Immediately after birth: PVR ≈ systemic vascular resistance (SVR).
    *   First 6–8 weeks: Gradual decline in PVR.
    *   After 2 years: PVR reaches adult levels (0.25–1.5 Wood units).
*   **Vasodilators:** Oxygen, hypocapnia, alkalosis, nitric oxide (NO), milrinone (PDE3 inhibitor), sildenafil (PDE5 inhibitor), prostacyclin analogues.
*   **Vasoconstrictors:** Hypoxia, hypercapnia, acidosis, endothelin-1.

### Normal Values (Paediatric)

| Parameter | Neonate | Infant | Toddler | School-age | Adolescent |
|-----------|---------|--------|---------|------------|------------|
| Heart rate (bpm) | 100–205 | 100–190 | 98–140 | 75–118 | 60–100 |
| Respiratory rate | 30–53 | 30–53 | 22–37 | 18–25 | 12–20 |
| Systolic BP (mmHg) | 67–84 | 72–104 | 86–106 | 97–115 | 110–131 |

*   **Circulating blood volume:** Premature 95 mL/kg, term 85 mL/kg, infant 80 mL/kg, adult male 75 mL/kg, adult female 65 mL/kg.

### Haemodynamics

*   **Flow-resistance relationship:** $Q = \Delta P / R$
*   **Poiseuille equation:** $R = 8L\eta / \pi r^4$ (resistance ∝ 1/r⁴)
*   **Systemic vascular resistance (SVR):** $SVR = (MAP - CVP) \times 80 / CO$ (normal: 770–1500 dyne·sec/cm⁵)
*   **Pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR):** $PVR = (MPAP - LAP) \times 80 / CO$ (normal: 20–120 dyne·sec/cm⁵)
*   **Compliance:** $C = \Delta V / \Delta P$ (venous compliance 10–20× greater than arterial)

### Cardiac Shunts

*   **Left-to-right shunt:** Oxygenated blood recirculates to lungs; patient pink (acyanotic).
*   **Right-to-left shunt:** Deoxygenated blood enters systemic circulation; patient cyanotic.
*   **Qp/Qs ratio:**
    *   Qp/Qs = 1: No shunt or balanced circulations
    *   Qp/Qs > 1: Left-to-right shunt
    *   Qp/Qs < 1: Right-to-left shunt
    *   Qp/Qs > 1.5: Haemodynamically significant

### Chamber Dilatation in Left-to-Right Shunts

| Defect | Dilated Chambers |
|--------|------------------|
| ASD | RA, RV, PA |
| VSD | PA, pulmonary veins, LA, LV |
| PDA | PA, pulmonary veins, LA, LV, aorta (proximal) |

### Streaming vs Mixing

*   **Streaming:** Preferential flow without complete mixing; oxygen saturation differs in great arteries (e.g., DORV with subaortic vs subpulmonic VSD).
*   **Mixing:** Complete blending; oxygen saturation equal in both great arteries (e.g., tricuspid atresia).

### Ventricular Circulations

*   **Biventricular:** Two adequate ventricles in series; each supports one circulation.
*   **Single-ventricle (Fontan):** Systemic venous return connected directly to PA without ventricular interposition; Qp/Qs ≈ 1; systemic veins at high pressure.
*   **One-and-a-half-ventricle:** SVC to PA (Glenn shunt) + small RV pumps IVC return; for patients with small RV (e.g., PA-IVS, Ebstein anomaly).

### Qp/Qs Calculation (Fick Principle)

$$\frac{Qp}{Qs} = \frac{Ao\ O_2\ sats - RA\ O_2\ sats}{PV\ O_2\ sats - PA\ O_2\ sats}$$

*   RA saturation = (3×SVC sats + 1×IVC sats)/4
*   PV saturation assumed 100%

### Eisenmenger Syndrome

*   **Definition:** Obstructive pulmonary vascular disease from longstanding large L→R shunt; PVR approaches or exceeds SVR.
*   **Shunt reversal:** R→L shunt → cyanosis.
*   **Early development:** Truncus arteriosus, common AVSD, VSD, PDA, TGA (ASD less established).
*   **CXR:** Decreased cardiac shadow, oligaemic lung fields, enlarged PA shadow.
*   **Median survival:** 20–50 years.

### Oxygen-Haemoglobin Dissociation Curve

*   **Sigmoid shape:** Flat above 60 mm Hg (8 kPa); steep below.
*   **Left shift (↑ affinity):** Foetal haemoglobin, alkalosis, hypothermia, ↓2,3-DPG.
*   **Right shift (↓ affinity):** Acidosis, hyperthermia, ↑CO₂, ↑2,3-DPG (tissue oxygen delivery).

### Cyanosis

*   **Manifest:** ≥5 g/dL deoxyhaemoglobin.
*   **Central:** Arterial desaturation (cardiac or pulmonary).
*   **Peripheral:** Normal arterial saturation; increased tissue extraction (shock, hypovolaemia, cold).
*   **Hyperoxia test:** Pulmonary causes → PaO₂ >100 mm Hg; cardiac causes → rise of 10–30 mm Hg, not >100 mm Hg.

### Oxygen Content

$$\text{O}_2\ \text{content} = (1.34 \times \text{Hb} \times \text{SaO}_2) + (0.003 \times \text{PaO}_2)$$

## Chapter 3: Congenital Cardiac Pharmacology

### Antithrombotic Agents

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Key Indications | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Aspirin** | Cyclo-oxygenase inhibitor | Irreversibly inhibits thromboxane A₂ production → ↓platelet adhesion | Post-op prophylaxis, Kawasaki syndrome | Neonate: 1–5 mg/kg OD; Child: 1–5 mg/kg (max 75 mg) OD |
| **Clopidogrel** | ADP receptor antagonist | Irreversibly modifies platelet ADP receptor → inhibits GP IIb/IIIa activation | Dual therapy with aspirin for acute coronary syndrome | 0–24 mo: 0.2 mg/kg/day; 2–17 y: 0.2–1 mg/kg/day (max 75 mg) |
| **Heparin** | Mucopolysaccharide | Inactivates factor Xa, inhibits prothrombin → thrombin conversion | CPB anticoagulation, DVT/PE prophylaxis | CPB: 300 U/kg; Therapeutic: 20–25 U/kg/hr IV |
| **LMWH** (dalteparin, enoxaparin) | Low-molecular-weight heparin | Greater anti-Xa activity, longer half-life, no APTT monitoring | Acute coronary syndrome, DVT prophylaxis | Enoxaparin therapeutic: 1–1.5 mg/kg BD |
| **Warfarin** | Coumarin derivative | Interferes with vitamin K metabolism → ↓factors II, VII, IX, X | Mechanical valves, AF, LV thrombus | Induction: 200 μg/kg day 1, then 100 μg/kg daily (target INR 2.0–3.5) |
| **Vitamin K** | Cofactor | Restores vitamin K-dependent coagulation factors | Reversal of warfarin | Reversal (no bleeding): 15–30 μg/kg; With bleeding: 250–300 μg/kg |
| **Tranexamic acid** | Lysine analogue | Binds plasminogen → inhibits fibrinolysis | Reduce bleeding post-cardiac surgery | 30 mg/kg loading, then 5–10 mg/kg/hr |

### Inotropes and Vasopressors

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Key Indications | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Dopamine** | Catecholamine | α, β, and dopaminergic receptor agonist | Cardiogenic shock, septic shock | 3–20 μg/kg/min |
| **Dobutamine** | Catecholamine | β₁ receptor agonist (inotropic, chronotropic) | Low CO after cardiac surgery | 2–20 μg/kg/min |
| **Adrenaline** (epinephrine) | Catecholamine | β₁ (inotropic), β₂ (vasodilator), α (vasoconstrictor at high dose) | Low CO, CPR, anaphylaxis | 0.1–1.5 μg/kg/min |
| **Noradrenaline** (norepinephrine) | Catecholamine | Potent α₁ agonist (vasoconstriction), mild β₁ | Shock with low SVR (sepsis) | 20–100 ng/kg/min (max 1 μg/kg/min) |
| **Vasopressin** | Peptide hormone | V₁ receptor (vasoconstriction), V₂ receptor (ADH) | Vasodilatory shock, oesophageal varices | 0.3 units/kg over 20–30 min |
| **Phenylephrine** | α₁ agonist | Selective α₁-adrenergic receptor agonist | Acute hypotension, maintain BP on CPB | 500 μg–1 mg IV bolus |
| **Levosimendan** | Calcium sensitizer | Increases myocyte sensitivity to calcium | Severe heart failure | 0.05–0.2 μg/kg/min |
| **Enoximone** | PDE3 inhibitor | Inodilator: ↓SVR, ↑contractility (↑cAMP) | Low CO with pulmonary hypertension | 500 μg/kg load, then 5–20 μg/kg/min |
| **Nitric oxide** | Endogenous gas | Selective pulmonary vasodilator (↑cGMP) | Persistent neonatal pulmonary hypertension | 20 ppm |

### Pulmonary Vasodilators

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------|-----------------|
| **Sildenafil** | PDE5 inhibitor | Inhibits cGMP degradation → pulmonary vasodilation | <20 kg: 10 mg TDS; >20 kg: 20 mg TDS |
| **Bosentan** | Endothelin-1 antagonist | Competitively blocks ET-1 receptors | 10–20 kg: 31.25 mg OD → BD; 20–40 kg: 31.25 mg OD → 62.5 mg BD |

### Antiarrhythmics

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Key Indications | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Amiodarone** | III | K⁺ channel blockade → ↑AP duration, refractory period | SVT, VT | PO: 5–10 mg/kg BD; IV: 5–10 mg/kg load |
| **Sotalol** | III + non-selective β | K⁺ channel blockade + β-blockade | Arrhythmias | 1–4 mg/kg BD |
| **Flecainide** | Ic | Na⁺ channel blockade → slows conduction | SVT, VT, WPW | 2 mg/kg BD/TDS |
| **Lidocaine** | Ib | Na⁺ channel blockade | Ventricular arrhythmias, digoxin toxicity | 0.5–1 mg load, then 0.6–3 mg/kg/hr |
| **Procainamide** | Ia | Na⁺ channel blockade | SVT, VT, WPW | IV: 3–6 mg/kg load; PO: 20 mg/kg/day divided |
| **Adenosine** | Endogenous nucleoside | AV node conduction block | Acute SVT termination | 100–150 μg/kg rapid IV, titrate up to 500 μg/kg |
| **Atropine** | Muscarinic antagonist | Blocks M₂ receptors → ↑HR | Bradycardia | 10–20 μg/kg IV |
| **Calcium gluconate** | — | ↑threshold potential, stabilises membrane | Hyperkalaemia | 0.11 mmol/kg (0.5 mL/kg of 10%) |

### Beta-Blockers and ACE Inhibitors

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------|-----------------|
| **Atenolol** | β₁-selective | Negative inotropic, chronotropic; reduces myocardial O₂ demand | 0.5–2 mg/kg OD |
| **Carvedilol** | Non-selective β + α₁ | Heart failure | 50 μg/kg BD, titrate to 350 μg/kg BD |
| **Esmolol** | Ultra-short-acting β₁ | Tetralogy spells | 600 μg/kg load, then 300–900 μg/kg/min |
| **Enalapril** | ACE inhibitor | ↓angiotensin II, ↑bradykinin → vasodilation | Neonate: 10 μg/kg OD; Child: 100 μg/kg OD |

### Diuretics

| Drug | Mechanism | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-----------|-----------------|
| **Furosemide** | Loop diuretic: inhibits Na⁺/K⁺/2Cl⁻ cotransporter | 0.5–2 mg/kg (max 12 mg/kg/day) |
| **Spironolactone** | Aldosterone antagonist: K⁺-sparing | 1–3 mg/kg/day (up to 9 mg/kg/day in resistant ascites) |

### Ductus Arteriosus Modulators

| Drug | Mechanism | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-----------|-----------------|
| **Alprostadil** (PGE₁) | Vasodilates ductus arteriosus | 5–100 ng/kg/min IV infusion |
| **Indomethacin** | Cyclo-oxygenase inhibitor → closes PDA | Neonate: 100–200 μg/kg ×1, then 100 μg/kg ×2 at 12–24 hr intervals |

### Anaesthetic and Sedative Agents

| Drug | Class | Key Indications | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Propofol** | IV anaesthetic | Induction/maintenance of anaesthesia | Induction: 2.5–4 mg/kg; Maintenance: 9–15 mg/kg/hr |
| **Midazolam** | Benzodiazepine | Sedation, ventilation | Sedation: 50–400 μg/kg/hr; Ventilation: 200 μg/kg bolus, then 60 μg/kg/hr |
| **Sevoflurane** | Volatile anaesthetic | Induction/maintenance of anaesthesia | Induction: up to 4–8%; Maintenance: 0.5–3% |
| **Morphine** | Opioid (µ-agonist) | Severe pain | 200 μg/kg PO q4–6h |
| **Remifentanil** | Opioid (µ-agonist) | Ultra-short-acting analgesia | 0.1–1 μg/kg load, then 3–120 μg/kg/hr |
| **Suxamethonium** | Depolarising muscle relaxant | Rapid tracheal intubation | 1–2 mg/kg IV |
| **Rocuronium** | Non-depolarising muscle relaxant | Muscle relaxation | 600 μg/kg load, then 300–600 μg/kg/hr |

### Antibiotics

| Drug | Class | Key Indications | Paediatric Dose |
|------|-------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Cefuroxime** | 2nd-gen cephalosporin | Surgical prophylaxis | 50 mg/kg pre-op, then 30 mg/kg TDS |
| **Gentamicin** | Aminoglycoside | Gram-negative infections | 7 mg/kg once daily, or 2.5 mg/kg TDS |
| **Vancomycin** | Glycopeptide | MRSA, Clostridium difficile | 10–15 mg/kg QDS (IV) or 10 mg/kg QDS (PO for C. diff) |

## Chapter 4: Congenital Echocardiography

### Principles and Modalities

*   **Sequential segmental analysis:** Systematic approach to describe congenitally malformed hearts based on three morphological segments (atria, ventricles, arterial trunks) and their connections.
*   **Transthoracic echocardiography (TTE):** Handheld transducer on chest/abdomen; commonest modality.
*   **Transoesophageal echocardiography (TOE):** Probe passed into oesophagus; detailed valve assessment, intra-operative imaging, guidance for transcatheter procedures.
*   **Epicardial echocardiography:** Probe placed directly on epicardium with chest open; intra-operative or on PICU.
*   **B-mode:** Live moving 2D image.
*   **M-mode:** Time-motion display; assesses LV function, fractional shortening, myocardial thickness.
*   **Doppler:** Assesses direction and velocity of blood flow; colour flow uses BART (Blue Away, Red Towards).
*   **3D echocardiography:** Demonstrates cardiac morphology, valves, septal structures; guides interventions.
*   **Z-score:** Number of standard deviations from the mean; used to normalise measurements for body surface area.

### Key Echocardiographic Views

| View | Structures Visualised |
|------|----------------------|
| **Abdominal short-axis (situs)** | Situs, IVC, aorta, hepatic veins |
| **Subcostal** | Atrial septum, AV valves, ventricular septum |
| **Apical four-chamber** | All four chambers, AV valves |
| **Apical five-chamber** | Additional aortic root |
| **Parasternal long-axis** | LVOT, aortic valve, mitral valve, aorta |
| **Parasternal short-axis** | RVOT, pulmonary valve, pulmonary arteries, aortic valve (en face) |
| **Suprasternal** | Aortic arch, PDA |
| **Left subclavicular ("crab view")** | Pulmonary veins |

### Atrial Septal Defects (ASD)

| Type | Characteristics |
|------|-----------------|
| **PFO** | Small communication (<4 mm) in central septum; bubble contrast may be needed |
| **Secundum** | Central septal communication; assess rims for device closure |
| **Primum** | Defect at junction of primum septum and AV valves; no offsetting of tricuspid/mitral valves |
| **Sinus venosus** | Proximity to SVC/pulmonary veins; high right parasternal view |
| **Coronary sinus** | Unroofing of coronary sinus |

### Ventricular Septal Defects (VSD)

| Type | Echocardiographic Features |
|------|---------------------------|
| **Perimembranous** | Fibrous continuity between aortic and tricuspid valves; parasternal short-axis |
| **Muscular** | Multiple; sweep entire septum with low Nyquist limit |
| **Doubly-committed subarterial** | Fibrous continuity between aortic and pulmonary valves; subcostal short-axis |

### Left-to-Right Shunt Assessment

| Defect | Dilated Chambers | Key Features |
|--------|------------------|--------------|
| **ASD** | RA, RV | Assess rims for device closure |
| **VSD** | LA, LV | Assess aortic valve regurgitation |
| **PDA** | LA, LV | Continuous L→R flow; estimate PA pressure |

### Obstructive Lesions

*   **Aortic stenosis:** Assess valve morphology, number of cusps, peak velocity (Doppler), gradient; best alignment: high right parasternal, apical five-chamber, subcostal short-axis.
*   **Coarctation:** Suprasternal view shows juxtaductal narrowing with shelf; CW Doppler shows high peak systolic velocity with diastolic tail; abdominal aortic PW shows low-velocity continuous flow with prolonged time to peak.
*   **Tetralogy of Fallot:** Dual-profile CW Doppler (fixed parabolic + dynamic dagger-shaped); assess coronary artery crossing RVOT (LAD from RCA in 3%).

### Cyanotic Congenital Heart Disease

| Condition | Key Echocardiographic Features |
|-----------|-------------------------------|
| **TAPVD** | Supracardiac: vertical vein to innominate vein; Cardiac: coronary sinus dilation ("tail of whale"); Infracardiac: descending vein (most prone to obstruction) |
| **TGA** | Parallel great vessels ("gun-barrel"); aorta from RV, PA from LV |
| **ccTGA** | AV discordance + VA discordance; reverse offsetting of AV valves (tricuspid more apical on left) |
| **Truncus arteriosus** | Solitary vessel from base; classify by pulmonary artery origin |
| **DORV** | Aorta overrides septum >50%; aorto-mitral discontinuity |
| **PA-IVS** | Assess tricuspid valve Z-score, RV size, RV sinusoids (50%), coronary fistulae |
| **PA-VSD** | Hypoplastic branch PAs; MAPCAs; high parasternal views |
| **Tricuspid atresia** | Thick band at AV groove; absent right AV connection |
| **Double-inlet ventricle** | Dominant LV with rudimentary RV (DILV commonest) |
| **HLHS** | Hypoplastic LV, aortic/mitral atresia/stenosis, restrictive ASD, RV function (TAPSE, FAC) |
| **Ebstein's anomaly** | Apical displacement of septal/posterior tricuspid leaflets; atrialised RV; assess anterior leaflet (may cause RVOT obstruction) |
| **ALCAPA** | LCA from pulmonary artery; retrograde flow (steal), dilated RCA, endocardial fibroelastosis |

### Atrioventricular Septal Defect (AVSD)

*   **Complete AVSD:** Common AV valve at single level; no offsetting; assess Rastelli classification (subcostal short-axis en face).
*   **Associated lesions:** PDA, coarctation, secundum ASD, additional VSDs, tetralogy of Fallot.

### Isomerism

| Type | Echocardiographic Features |
|------|---------------------------|
| **Right isomerism** | Absent coronary sinus, TAPVD, AVSD, DORV with PS/PA, bilateral SVC, left-handed topology |
| **Left isomerism** | Interrupted IVC with azygos continuation, AVSD, bilateral SVC |

---

## Chapter 5: Congenital Cardiac Imaging

### Principles of Imaging Modalities

| Modality | Principles | Risks |
|----------|------------|-------|
| **CT** | X-ray attenuation; iodinated contrast enhances blood pool | Ionising radiation (IRMER: ALARP); allergic reaction (24/1000); renal impairment (<2%) |
| **MRI** | Magnetic field + radiofrequency; protons generate signal; gadolinium contrast | Metal implant risks; NSF (renal failure); anaphylaxis rare (1:10,000) |

### Coronary Artery Anomalies

*   **Modality of choice:** ECG-gated CT angiography (origin, course, termination, fistulae, myocardial bridges)
*   **MRI role:** Ventricular function, myocardial viability, valvular function, stress ischaemia detection, post-repair baffle assessment
*   **Key anomalies:** Anomalous origin from opposite sinus (ALCAPA, retro-aortic course), interarterial course, myocardial bridging

### Atrial Septal Defect (ASD)

*   **CXR:** Cardiomegaly, RA dilatation, prominent central pulmonary arteries, increased vascular markings
*   **MRI:** Poor echo windows, anatomical assessment, shunt quantification (phase-contrast Qp/Qs), ventricular volumes/function
*   **CT:** Associated anomalies (anomalous pulmonary veins), pre-op planning (vascular access, defect size/shape for device), coronary assessment

### Ventricular Septal Defect (VSD)

*   **CXR:** Dilated central pulmonary arteries, increased vascularity, increased cardiothoracic ratio
*   **MRI:** Gold standard for LV function/size, shunt quantification, scar localisation (late gadolinium) for arrhythmia ablation
*   **CT:** Complementary anatomy (type, location, size, number), outflow obstruction, coronary assessment, surgical planning

### Atrioventricular Septal Defect (AVSD)

*   **CXR:** Cardiomegaly, large proximal/small peripheral PAs ("peripheral pruning"), prominent pulmonary veins (if AV valve regurgitation)
*   **CT/MRI:** Anatomical evaluation (when echo inconclusive), outflow obstruction, coarctation, heterotaxy; MRI for post-op quantification (MR, LV function, residual shunt)

### Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)

*   **CT:** RVOT morphology, pulmonary arteries, aorta, collaterals (MAPCAs), re-do surgery planning, coronary patency, sternal proximity
*   **MRI:** Ventricular size/function, pulmonary regurgitation quantification (phase-contrast), residual VSD, fibrosis (late gadolinium) — guides timing of pulmonary valve replacement

### Double-Outlet Right Ventricle (DORV)

*   **MRI:** Intracardiac anatomy, biventricular function, surgical feasibility, pulmonary arteries, aortic arch
*   **CT:** Great artery relationship, VSD location, outflow obstruction, coronary anomalies (RCA from LMCA, duplicated LAD), 3D reconstruction for surgical planning

### Transposition of the Great Arteries (TGA)

*   **CXR:** Narrow mediastinum (parallel great arteries)
*   **Atrial switch (Mustard/Senning):** MRI for RV function, baffle anatomy, leaks; CT preferred if pacemaker (baffle obstruction, leaks, vena cava obstruction)
*   **Rastelli:** MRI for ventricular function, conduit function; CT for conduit calcification/stenosis, LVOT obstruction
*   **Arterial switch (Jatene):** MRI for ventricular volumes, aortic regurgitation, branch PA stenosis, coronary anatomy, myocardial perfusion/viability; CT for anatomical delineation, coronary stenosis

### Congenitally Corrected TGA (ccTGA)

*   **CXR:** Dextrocardia or mesocardia
*   **MRI:** Anatomy, ventricular function, ischaemia/scarring, RV size/function, tricuspid regurgitation severity
*   **CT:** Anatomical assessment, great vessels, coronary arteries, cardiac function

### Cavopulmonary Circulation (Fontan)

*   **Pre-op:** CT for branch PA stenosis, pulmonary venous return; MRI for ventricular function, valvular regurgitation, collateral flow quantification
*   **Post-op:** CT for connection stenosis/thrombosis, pulmonary vein compression, AV fistulae; MRI for ventricular function, connection evaluation, flow through fenestrations/leaks, collaterals, thrombosis, fibrosis

### Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Drainage (TAPVD)

*   **Modality of choice:** CT and MRI (echo difficult unless vein drains close to cavoatrial junction)
*   **CXR:** Scimitar sign, pulmonary artery dilatation, cardiomegaly
*   **MRI:** Phase-contrast Qp/Qs quantifies shunt significance

### Coarctation of the Aorta

*   **CXR:** Rib notching (Roesler sign), figure of 3 sign
*   **MRI:** Preferred (no radiation) — severity, collateral vessels, associated anomalies, recoarctation, aneurysm
*   **CT:** Stented coarctation (metal artefact limits MRI), site/morphology, arch hypoplasia, collaterals, post-stenting complications (dissection, leak, thrombus, migration, fracture), coronary evaluation

### Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome (HLHS)

*   **Prenatal:** Ultrasound (>22 weeks)
*   **Post-op:** CMR for Glenn/Fontan suitability (ventricular size/function, pulmonary arteries, neo-aorta); CT for associated anomalies, sizing

### Proximal Aortic Pathology

*   **Indications:** Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos, Loeys-Dietz, bicuspid aortic valve
*   **CTA:** Most precise for aortic diameter measurement
*   **MRI:** Preferred for follow-up (young patients) — 3D SSFP, cine images, flow mapping for valve disease (mitral prolapse, aortic regurgitation)

### Ebstein's Anomaly

*   **CXR:** Cardiomegaly, narrow aortic pedicle, decreased pulmonary vascular markings
*   **CT/MRI:** Apical displacement of septal/posterior tricuspid leaflet, atrialised RV, severe RA enlargement, associated anomalies (ASD, PS, subaortic stenosis, coarctation)
*   **MRI:** RV size/function quantification, tricuspid regurgitation severity, Qp/Qs, fibrosis, LV non-compaction assessment; post-op surveillance

### Transcatheter Procedure Planning

*   **CT/Echo:** Suitability, device size, vascular access, coronary proximity, complications
*   **PET:** Device infection

### Vascular Slings and Rings

*   **CXR:** Tracheal narrowing, unilateral aeration
*   **Upper GI:** Oesophageal indentation
*   **CTA/MRA:** Vascular anatomy, tracheal compression, intracardiac anomalies

### Aortopulmonary Window

*   **CT:** Abnormal connection between aorta and pulmonary trunk, associated pulmonary hypertension (mosaic perfusion, PA dilatation, hepatic vein dilatation)

---

## Chapter 6: Congenital Angiography and Catheter Interventions

### Radiation Safety

*   **Factors increasing exposure:** Age, body size, distance, study length, frame rate
*   **ALARP tactics:** Iso-centring, lowest frame rate, collimation, minimise beam-on time, remove anti-scatter grids for <20 kg
*   **Hazards:**
    *   Skin: erythema at 2 Gy, hair loss at >6 Gy, telangiectasis at 10–15 Gy
    *   Eye: cataracts (operators wear protective goggles)
    *   Malignancy: stochastic effect, brain/skin/thyroid highest risk (wear thyroid shield)

### Angiographic Projections (Table 1)

| Lesion | A Plane | B Plane |
|--------|---------|---------|
| Pulmonary stenosis | 0° + 30° cranial | 90° |
| RVOT/MPA | 10° LAO + 40° cranial | 90° |
| LPA (long axial) | 30° RAO | 60° LAO + 30° cranial |
| ASD | 30° LAO + 30° cranial | — |
| PA bifurcation | 30° caudal + 10° RAO | 20° caudal |
| LVOT obstruction | RAO | 60° LAO + 20–30° cranial |
| Coarctation | 0° | 90° or 60° LAO + 20–30° cranial |
| VSD | RAO 45–70° | LAO + 30° cranial |
| PDA | 30° RAO | 90° |

### Three-Dimensional Rotational Angiography (3DRA)

*   C-arm rotation over 3–5 seconds with timed contrast injection
*   Reconstruction <30 seconds, can be rotated in 3D
*   Fusion imaging: pre-registered CT/MRI overlaid on live fluoroscopy → reduced contrast/radiation

### Haemodynamic Waveforms

| Waveform | Normal Values | Abnormalities |
|----------|---------------|---------------|
| **RA** | Mean 2–8 mmHg | Elevated a wave (tricuspid stenosis/atresia, ↓RV compliance); elevated v wave (RV failure, severe TR); prominent y descent (constriction, restriction); blunted y descent (tamponade) |
| **RV** | Systolic 15–30 mmHg, EDP 2–8 mmHg | — |
| **PCWP** | 4–12 mmHg | Reflects left atrial pressure; a wave (atrial contraction), v wave (atrial filling) |
| **Aortic** | — | Widened pulse pressure (AR, PDA, ruptured sinus, AV malformations); reduced pulse pressure (<50% of diastolic: tamponade, heart failure, AS) |

### Pressure Gradients

*   Peak-to-peak systolic gradient (catheter) vs maximum instantaneous gradient (Doppler) — different timing; mean gradients are better for comparison
*   Doppler gradients are not "overestimates" — measured at different point in cardiac cycle

### Valvuloplasty

| Procedure | Indications | Technique | Success Criteria |
|-----------|-------------|----------|------------------|
| **Aortic** | Neonatal critical AS; AS with depressed LV function; peak gradient ≥50 mmHg (or ≥40 mmHg with symptoms) | Femoral/carotid access; balloon:annulus ratio 0.8–0.9; Tyshak II balloons | >50% ↓gradient; >25% ↑valve area; ≤mild AR |
| **Pulmonary** | Critical PS; peak gradient >40 mmHg (catheter) or >60 mmHg (echo); RV dysfunction | Femoral vein; balloon:annulus ratio 1.2–1.4; longer balloons for stability | — |

### Stenting and Angioplasty

| Vessel | Indications | Considerations |
|--------|-------------|----------------|
| **Pulmonary artery** | Gradient 20–30 mmHg; RV pressure >⅔ systemic; flow discrepancy 35/65% | Balloon angioplasty first in small/complex anatomy; stents should have adult-size potential; MDT discussion |
| **Pulmonary vein** | Congenital, post-operative, post-ablation | Stents better than balloons; rarely curative in congenital disease; pulmonary haemorrhage risk |
| **Ductus arteriosus** | Cyanotic heart disease (TOF, PA), HLHS hybrid, non-HLHS with single-stage repair contraindicated | Straight ductus (≤2 bends); stent diameter 3.5–4 mm (term), 3 mm (<3 kg); femoral vessel injury risk |
| **Coarctation** | Recoarctation with gradient >20 mmHg; native coarctation in unstable patients (palliation) | Primary stenting preferred >20 kg; covered CP Stent™ reduces rupture risk; open cell stents for arch hypoplasia |

### Defect Closure

| Defect | Indications | Devices | Complications |
|--------|-------------|--------|---------------|
| **PDA** | Premature <2.4 kg with low PVR; large PDA with reactive PVR (after testing); contraindicated in Eisenmenger | Coils (<2 mm); Amplatzer Duct Occluder, Vascular Plug II, MVP | Embolisation, aortic/pulmonary obstruction, haemolysis |
| **Secundum ASD** | RV volume overload; paradoxical emboli; platypnoea-orthodeoxia | Self-centring (Amplatzer Septal Occluder, Figulla, Cera, GORE CARDIOFORM); non-self-centring (GORE Septal Occluder) | — |
| **VSD** | Haemodynamically significant (LV volume overload, heart failure, elevated RV pressures); contraindicated if PVR >7 WU/m², sepsis, need for antiplatelet contraindication | Muscular VSD Occluder (waist + 8 mm discs), Membranous VSD Occluder (unequal discs), Duct Occluder II | Heart block, aortic/tricuspid valve damage, embolisation |
| **Fontan fenestration** | Favourable haemodynamics: balloon occlusion → ↑SaO₂ with modest ↑Fontan pressure or <20% ↓CO | ASD occluder, PFO occluder, VSD occluder, vascular plug, covered stent | — |

### Hybrid VSD Closure

*   Median sternotomy, beating heart, no CPB
*   RV free wall puncture → wire across VSD → sheath into LV → deploy LV disc → pull back → deploy RV disc
*   For patients where CPB contraindicated or challenging anatomy

### Percutaneous Pulmonary Valve Replacement (PPVR)

**Indications:**
*   PR >25% with RVEDVi >150 mL/m², RVESVi >80 mL/m², RVEF <47%, or LVEF <55%
*   RVOT obstruction with RV systolic pressure ≥70% systemic + at least moderate TR
*   Heart failure symptoms, exercise intolerance (≤70% predicted VO₂), syncope, QRS >160 ms

**Anatomical criteria:** Previously implanted conduit (16–29 mm) or RVOT/PA of similar diameter

**Valves:**
*   **Edwards SAPIEN 3/Ultra:** Balloon-expandable, cobalt-chromium frame, bovine pericardial leaflets; 23–29 mm; 16 Fr sheath
*   **Edwards Alterra Adaptive Prestent:** Self-expanding stent for native RVOT → landing zone for SAPIEN 3
*   **Medtronic Melody:** Bovine jugular vein valve in platinum-iridium frame; 18–22 mm; 22 Fr sheath

**Pre-procedural:** CMR/CTA to define anatomy; coronary compression assessment with balloon inflation in landing zone (especially post-arterial switch/Ross)

### Atrial Septostomy

*   Rashkind procedure (1966): balloon passed across interatrial septum, inflated, pulled back to tear fossa ovalis
*   Performed at bedside with echo guidance when possible
*   Indications: TGA with restrictive/intact atrial communication; mitral atresia/HLHS requiring L→R shunt; PA-IVS, tricuspid atresia, TAPVD requiring R→L shunt; left atrial hypertension on ECMO

---

## Chapter 7: Anaesthesia and Congenital Heart Disease

### Role of Anaesthetists

Anaesthetists are involved in:
*   Pre-operative planning and peri-operative care for cardiac surgery
*   Anaesthesia/sedation for percutaneous interventions in catheter laboratory
*   Intensive care support for adult and paediatric patients
*   Non-cardiac interventions: pain management, vascular access, general surgery, obstetrics (pregnancy increases physiological demands)

### Neonatal vs Older Children — Key Differences

| System | Neonate Characteristics |
|--------|-------------------------|
| **Airway** | Prominent occiput (neck flexion), large tongue, floppy U-shaped epiglottis, cephalad larynx |
| **Breathing** | Rapid hypoxia (immature respiratory centres, easily fatigued muscles, horizontal ribs ↓mechanical advantage, low FRC, ↑airway resistance, foetal ACh receptors → delayed recovery from depolarising relaxants) |
| **Circulation** | Rate-dependent CO (stroke volume fixed); stiff myocardium; CO: 400 mL/kg/min at birth → 150 at 8 weeks → 70 at puberty |
| **Pharmacology** | ↑gastric pH, slower emptying, ↑transit; blood-brain barrier immature; slower metabolism; impaired renal excretion (preterm) |
| **Temperature** | ↑surface area:volume → rapid heat loss; forced air warmers essential |
| **Communication** | Developmental stage impacts interaction |

### Pre-operative Assessment

**History:** Cardiac diagnosis, comorbidities, previous anaesthesia (patient and family)

**Examination:** Airway assessment, vascular access sites

**Investigations:**
*   FBC: polycythaemia (cyanotic patients require higher Hb) vs anaemia
*   Haemoglobinopathy screen: if cooling planned (sickle crisis risk)
*   Coagulation: cyanotic patients prone to bleeding even with normal labs
*   U&Es: arrhythmia risk
*   Pregnancy test: all post-menarche females
*   ECG: conduction abnormalities, arrhythmias
*   CXR: lung size, anatomical abnormalities, respiratory disease

**Starvation guidelines:**
*   Clear fluids: 1 hour
*   Breast milk: 4 hours
*   Other milk/food: 6 hours

### Classification of CHD for Anaesthetic Management

| Type | Physiology | Examples | Key Anaesthetic Concerns |
|------|------------|----------|-------------------------|
| **↑PBF (L→R shunt)** | Volume overload, initially acyanotic | ASD, VSD, AVSD, PDA | Avoid ↓PVR, manage heart failure, pulmonary hypertension may develop |
| **↓PBF (R→L shunt)** | Cyanotic | TOF, PA ± VSD, DORV (TOF type) | Maintain balance between PVR and SVR; avoid ↓SVR or ↑PVR |
| **↓SBF** | Systemic hypoperfusion | Coarctation, HLHS, severe AS, obstructed TAPVC, TGA with restrictive ASD/PDA | Support systemic perfusion, maintain ductal patency (PGE₁) |
| **Balanced parallel** | Total mixing, single ventricle | HLHS (pre-Norwood), truncus arteriosus, unbalanced AVSD | Balance PVR/SVR; avoid disruption of equilibrium |

### Principles of Anaesthesia for CHD

**Key destabilising factors:**
*   Reduced venous return (hypovolaemia from starvation + anaesthetic vasodilation)
*   Reduced myocardial function (balanced technique: opioids + sedatives + muscle relaxant minimises suppression)
*   Increased PVR (hypoventilation, hypercapnia, PEEP)
*   Decreased SVR (impairs coronary perfusion)
*   Temperature and glucose abnormalities

**High-risk periods:** Induction and extubation (hypoventilation, hypercapnia → ↑PVR)

**Special considerations:**
*   Endocarditis prophylaxis
*   Paradoxical emboli risk (intravenous injections)

### Cyanotic Lesions — Management

Cyanosis worsens with: ↓pulmonary vein saturation (V/Q mismatch) or ↑R→L shunting

**R→L shunting increased by:** ↑PVR (hypoxia, hypercarbia, acidosis, PEEP) or ↓SVR (anaesthetic agents)

**Strategies:**
*   Avoid hypoxia, hypercarbia, acidosis
*   May require systemic vasopressors to maintain PVR/SVR balance
*   Extubation challenging: early extubation avoids coughing (↑PVR) but risks hypoxia/hypercarbia

### Specific Lesions

| Lesion | Key Anaesthetic Risks | Management Principles |
|--------|----------------------|----------------------|
| **Ebstein's anomaly** | Poor CO, R→L shunt (interatrial), arrhythmias | Minimise PVR, optimise preload, maintain contractility, sinus rhythm; small dose vasopressor (avoid fluid overload) |
| **HLHS (pre-Norwood)** | Balance PVR/SVR critical; PDA-dependent systemic circulation | PGE₁, maintain SVR low, PVR high; modify ventilation; NIRS monitoring |
| **Single ventricle (post-Glenn/Fontan)** | Passive pulmonary flow; hypovolaemia poorly tolerated; arrhythmias | Avoid ↑PVR (hypoxia, hypercarbia, acidosis, ketamine); maintain sinus rhythm; systemic RV may have poor function |
| **Coarctation with poor LV function** | Upper body hypertension; cross-clamp may require vasodilators; avoid hyperventilation | Monitor BP above and below coarctation (right radial and femoral); pre-op inotropes; heparin; intercostal blocks for post-op analgesia |
| **PA-IVS** | RV hypertrophy, supra-systemic pressures; coronary sinusoids (myocardial perfusion dependent on intracavitary pressure) | Avoid inotropes (↑subpulmonary gradient); PGE₁ for PDA; radiofrequency perforation risk (tamponade) |
| **PDA ligation (preterm)** | Respiratory disease; lung retraction impairs ventilation; bleeding risk | Full cardiac assessment (associated lesions); adequate vascular access; post-op inotropic support; recurrent laryngeal nerve injury |
| **Obstructed TAPVC** | Low CO, pulmonary oedema, right heart failure | IPPV pre-op; monitor RAP and LAP post-bypass; pulmonary vasodilators (milrinone, NO); atrial fenestration if needed; delayed sternal closure |

### Eisenmenger Syndrome — Anaesthetic Risk

**High mortality** — avoid unless essential

**Precautions:**
*   Strict starvation guidelines
*   Avoid ↓SVR (anaesthesia) or ↑PVR (hypoxia, hypercarbia, inadequate analgesia)
*   Avoid ↑SVR (pain, anxiety) → ↑L→R shunt, RV dilatation, arrhythmias
*   Convert oral anticoagulants to short-acting IV agents

### Haemostasis and Transfusion

**Coagulation abnormalities in cyanotic CHD:**
*   Thrombocytopenia, reduced fibrinogen, ↑fibrinolysis, ↓factors V and VIII

**CPB effects:** Haemodilution, synthetic surface exposure → platelet dysfunction, coagulation abnormalities

**Transfusion considerations:**
*   Previous transfusions → antibody formation
*   Point-of-care testing (TEG®, SpHb) guides product administration
*   Cyanotic patients may require higher haematocrit; over-transfusion causes hyperviscosity and fluid overload

### Weaning from Cardiopulmonary Bypass

**Critical factors:**
*   Adequate repair — residual lesions poorly tolerated
*   **Pressure overload:** residual stenosis (RVOT, mitral, LVOT) → ventricular failure
*   **Volume overload:** residual VSD, significant AV valve regurgitation, large PDA → pulmonary overflow, cerebral/myocardial hypoperfusion

**Early signs of myocardial dysfunction:**
*   Poor BP with normal afterload/adequate preload
*   High venous pressures, inadequate systemic pressure
*   Low end-tidal CO₂
*   Direct observation (poor contraction, mottled epicardium, distended ventricles)
*   TOE evidence

**Management:**
*   Optimise filling pressures (LAP may be needed — PVR varies in children)
*   Ionised calcium (neonatal myocardium immature)
*   Inotropes (milrinone common in infants — RV hypertrophy, reactive pulmonary vasculature)
*   Rewarming (coagulation, vascular tone)
*   Mechanical assist (ECMO, VAD) if pharmacological measures fail

### Monitoring

**Minimum standards (italics):**
*   **Airway:** Airway pressure, disconnection alarms; expired CO₂
*   **Breathing:** *Pulse oximetry*, tidal volumes, airway pressure, respiratory rate, inspired O₂, volatile agent concentration
*   **Circulation:** *ECG*, blood pressure (arterial line for beat-to-beat), filling pressure (right internal jugular ± left atrial line post-bypass), TOE if indicated
*   **Neurological:** NIRS (cerebral oximetry), BIS, nerve stimulators
*   **Renal:** Urinary catheter, NIRS (flank)
*   **Metabolism:** Temperature (central/peripheral), blood gases

**Cerebral oximetry (NIRS):**
*   Uses near-infrared light (730 nm and 810 nm) — oxyhaemoglobin and deoxyhaemoglobin absorb differently
*   Does not require pulsatile flow (usable on CPB)
*   Reflects cerebral perfusion; guides manipulation of systemic blood flow, cerebral vascular tone, haematocrit

### Central Venous Access Risks

| Timing | Risks |
|--------|-------|
| **Immediate** | Vessel trauma (aberrant anatomy), arterial cannulation, arrhythmias, air embolus (paradoxical in shunts), catheter/guidewire embolisation |
| **Early** | Blood loss (concealed), pneumothorax, haemothorax, hydrothorax |
| **Medium-term** | Infection, venous thrombosis (especially subclavian) |
| **Long-term** | Vessel occlusion, stenosis (critical in passive circulations — Fontan) |

**Risk reduction:** Ultrasound, Trendelenburg position, sterile precautions, remove lines promptly

### Transoesophageal Echocardiography (TOE)

**Indications:**
*   Anatomy when TTE non-diagnostic or impossible
*   Diagnosing pathology (endocarditis, thrombus)
*   Immediate evaluation of surgical results
*   Guiding percutaneous device placement (ASD occlusion, pulmonary valve replacement, transseptal puncture)

**Risks:**
*   Airway: trauma, ETT displacement
*   Breathing: airway compression
*   Circulation: arrhythmias (vagal/sympathetic stimulation)
*   Trauma: teeth, pharynx, oesophagus, stomach
*   Ischaemic injury (pressure/heat)
*   Bacteraemia

**Risk reduction:** Dedicated, trained TOE operator

### Thromboelastogram (TEG®)

| Parameter | What It Measures | Clinical Action |
|-----------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **R time (reaction time)** | Clot initiation | Prolonged → hypofibrinogenaemia, factor deficiency → FFP, cryoprecipitate, fibrinogen concentrate |
| **K time, alpha angle** | Clot propagation speed | Deranged → hypofibrinogenaemia, factor deficiency → cryoprecipitate, FFP |
| **MA (maximum amplitude)** | Clot strength (platelet function) | Reduced → platelet transfusion |
| **LY30, LY60** | Fibrinolysis | >7.5% → antifibrinolytics (tranexamic acid, aprotinin) |

Heparinase samples distinguish heparin effect; temperature-adjusted samples differentiate hypothermia-associated coagulopathy.

### Early vs Prolonged Ventilation

| Early Extubation | Prolonged Ventilation |
|------------------|----------------------|
| Shorter hospital stay | Ventilator-associated pneumonia |
| Increased comfort, parental satisfaction | Subglottic stenosis |
| Early recovery of communication, feeding, mobility | Accidental extubation, kinking, obstruction |
| — | Sedation side effects |

**Contraindications to early extubation:** Prolonged CPB time, significant inotrope requirement, significant comorbidities (PHT, heart failure, chronic lung disease, liver failure), high risk of TRALI/ARDS/MOF

### Handover to PICU — Key Information

*   Patient identifiers (hospital number, DOB, age, weight)
*   Diagnostic issues (underlying condition, pre-op status, previous procedures, allergies)
*   Procedure performed (repair/palliation, residual lesions, CPB details, blood products, arrhythmias, pacing, drains)
*   Airway (mask ventilation ease, laryngoscopy, ETT size/depth)
*   Ventilator (mode, pressures, O₂, nitric oxide)
*   Circulatory (lines, CPB duration, cross-clamp, DHCA, inotropes, pacing, blood products, results, ultrafiltration)
*   Antibiotics, analgesia, fluids administered
*   Plan (ideal haemodynamics)
*   Contact details

### Postoperative Pain — Negative Effects

| System | Effects |
|--------|---------|
| **Cardiovascular** | ↑HR, ↑SVR, ↑PVR, ↑O₂ demand; crying ↑intrathoracic pressure → ↑PVR → altered shunting |
| **Respiratory** | Atelectasis → pneumonia; hypocapnia with prolonged crying |
| **CNS** | Aversion to future procedures, impaired communication |
| **General** | Impaired wound healing (↓subcutaneous O₂), poor mobilisation → delayed discharge |

### Quality Control

**Quality improvement cycle:** Identify problem → define standard → collect data → implement change → evaluate impact

**Challenges:** Complexity of healthcare makes meaningful metrics difficult; QIP may identify resource deficiencies or educational needs but frequent changes risk staff disengagement

---

## Chapter 8: Paediatric Cardiac Intensive Care

### Principles of Practice

Cardiac intensive care units manage children with cardiac conditions admitted for:
*   Acute medical problems (stabilisation prior to cardiotomy)
*   Post-operative care (cardiac operations, catheter-based procedures, electrophysiology)
*   Post-non-cardiac surgery care

### Monitoring Tools (Table 1)

| Tool | Key Uses |
|------|----------|
| **ECG** | Heart rate, rhythm, ST changes |
| **Invasive arterial pressure** | Beat-to-beat BP, pulse pressure (SVR), pulse pressure variation (volume), contractility (upstroke), CO calculation, sampling |
| **CVP** | Right atrial pressure, RVEDP, preload; waveform for arrhythmias/tricuspid disease |
| **LAP** | LVEDP (surrogate), preload/contractility/afterload; risk of air embolus |
| **SpO₂** | Oxygen saturation (<80% less accurate), tissue perfusion (waveform) |
| **CO monitors** | CO, SV, SVR, contractility; trends more useful than absolute values |
| **ETCO₂** | Gas exchange, ventilation adequacy, ETT position, pulmonary blood flow |
| **NIRS** | Regional tissue oxygenation (cerebral, renal); marker of perfusion |
| **aEEG** | Electrocortical activity (seizures, encephalopathy) |
| **BIS** | Depth of sedation/anaesthesia |

### Duct-Dependent Circulations

| Type | Lesions | Presentation | Management |
|------|---------|--------------|------------|
| **Systemic** | Critical AS, interrupted aortic arch, coarctation, HLHS | Cardiogenic shock, acidosis, multiorgan failure | Prostaglandin (5–100 ng/kg/min), intubation, inotropes, correct electrolytes, organ support |
| **Pulmonary** | Critical PS, PA ± VSD, TOF with severe RVOTO | Cyanosis, hyperoxia test negative | Prostaglandin, intubation if shock/acidosis/profound cyanosis/apnoea |

**Prostaglandin effects:** Dose-dependent (low: maintain, high: reopen); side effects: apnoea, pyrexia, vasodilation (hypotension)

### Persistent Pulmonary Hypertension of Newborn (PPHN) vs Cyanotic CHD

| Feature | PPHN | Cyanotic CHD |
|---------|------|--------------|
| Response to 100% O₂ | Minor response | No response |
| PaO₂ | Occasionally normal/high | Never normal |
| Response to iNO | Usually good | No/minimal response |
| Response to ↓PaCO₂ | Improved oxygenation | No response |

**Gold standard:** Echocardiography (exclude CHD, especially obstructed TAPVC)

### Single-Ventricle Pre-operative Management

**Goal:** Balanced Qp:Qs (0.7–1:1), target SpO₂ 75–80%

**Requirements:**
*   Unrestrictive interatrial communication
*   Patent duct of adequate size
*   Balanced PVR/SVR (Figure 2)

**High Qp:Qs (excessive pulmonary flow):**
*   ↑PVR: ↑PaCO₂ (pulmonary vasoconstrictor), avoid O₂, ↓prostaglandin
*   ↓SVR: sedation/analgesia, normothermia, afterload reduction (milrinone)

**Qp:Qs calculation:**
$$\frac{Qp}{Qs} = \frac{\text{Aortic sats} - \text{Mixed venous sats}}{\text{Pulmonary venous sats} - \text{Pulmonary artery sats}}$$

(Assumptions: mixed venous = CVP sats, pulmonary venous ≈100%, pulmonary artery = aortic sats in true single ventricle)

### Left-to-Right Shunt Pre-operative Management

**Causes:** Large VSD, AVSD, PDA, AP window

**Deterioration triggers:** Falling PVR with age, intercurrent infections, pulmonary hypertensive episodes

**Management:**
*   Treat intercurrent illness
*   Reduce pulmonary oedema: diuretics, PEEP
*   Reduce pulmonary blood flow (↑PVR): ↑PaCO₂, minimise O₂
*   Afterload reduction: ACE inhibitors, milrinone, positive pressure ventilation

### Right-to-Left Shunt Pre-operative Management (Cyanotic)

**Causes:** Tetralogy of Fallot, pulmonary atresia ± VSD

**Aim:** Improve pulmonary blood flow

**Strategies:**
*   Reduce R→L shunt: ↓PVR (O₂, sedation, ventilation, iNO); ↑SVR (phenylephrine, noradrenaline, vasopressin, cooling); ↓RVOTO (avoid acidosis, β-blockers, sedation, fluids)
*   Interventional cardiology: RVOT/PDA stenting, balloon valvuloplasty
*   Surgery: BT shunt, transannular patch, primary repair, ECMO

### Transposition of Great Arteries (TGA) Pre-operative Management

**Mixing required at:** Atrial (PFO/ASD), ventricular (VSD), or arterial (PDA) level

**Causes of profound cyanosis:** Associated PPHN, restrictive/intact atrial septum, delayed diagnosis, associated lesions

**Strategies to improve mixing:**
*   Maintain ductal patency (prostaglandin)
*   Unrestrict atrial shunting: balloon atrial septostomy (BAS), ↑LA pressure (prostaglandin)
*   ↓PVR: O₂, sedation, ventilation, iNO
*   ↑SVR: noradrenaline, vasopressin

### Left Ventricular Failure and Dilated Cardiomyopathy

**Assessment:**
*   Echocardiography: LV function, structural disease, coronary origins
*   ECG: tachyarrhythmias, ischaemia/infarction
*   Bloods: end-organ function, troponin, lactate, SvO₂
*   Cardiomyopathy screen: genetics, metabolic, endocrine

**Medical management (Figure 4):**
*   Optimise preload, contractility, afterload
*   Diuretics, inotropes (adrenaline, dopamine), inodilators (milrinone), vasopressors
*   Mechanical support (ECMO, VAD) if medical management fails → bridge to recovery/transplant

### Post-cardiotomy Complications

**Airway:** Endobronchial intubation, airway oedema, vocal cord palsy, central airway compression

**Breathing:** Acute lung injury, pneumothorax, pleural effusion, chylothorax, atelectasis, pulmonary oedema, diaphragm palsy

**Circulation:** SIRS, LCOS, myocardial dysfunction, arrhythmias, coagulopathy/bleeding, tamponade, residual/iatrogenic lesions, thrombosis

**Disability:** Pain, stroke, anxiety, delirium/withdrawal

### Postoperative Bleeding (Table 3, Figure 5)

| Severity | Chest Drain Losses |
|----------|-------------------|
| Nil | 0 |
| Minimal | 1–2 mL/kg/hr |
| Moderate | 2–5 mL/kg/hr |
| High | 5–10 mL/kg/hr |
| Very severe (assume surgical) | >10 mL/kg/hr |

**Management:**
*   Resuscitation: warmed blood products (RBCs, platelets, FFP, cryoprecipitate)
*   Correct coagulopathy: tranexamic acid (antifibrinolytic), normothermia, ionised calcium, protamine (heparin reversal), acidosis correction
*   TEG® (Figure 5) guides product selection: ↑R time (factors) → FFP; ↓MA (platelets) → platelets; ↑LY30 (fibrinolysis) → tranexamic acid
*   rFVIIa only in extreme circumstances (thrombosis risk)
*   Consider surgical bleeding/tamponade → re-exploration

### Low Cardiac Output State (LCOS)

**Incidence:** ~25% post-cardiotomy, peak 6–12 hours post-op

**Causes:** Inflammatory response, myocardial ischaemia, reperfusion injury, residual lesions, hypovolaemia, tamponade, pulmonary hypertension, arrhythmias (JET)

**Clinical features:** Cool peripheries, weak pulses, tachycardia, hypotension, ↓urine output, ↓NIRS, ↑lactate, ↓SvO₂

**Oxygen delivery (DO₂):** $DO_2 = CO \times CaO_2$, where $CaO_2 = (1.34 \times Hb \times SaO_2) + (0.003 \times PaO_2)$

**Management strategies (Figure 6):**
*   Sedation/muscle relaxation (↓metabolic demand)
*   Optimise ventilation/oxygenation
*   Optimise preload: correct bleeding, fluid boluses
*   Optimise contractility: inotropes (adrenaline, dopamine), inodilators (milrinone), calcium
*   Optimise afterload: normothermia, inodilators (if high SVR), vasopressors (if low SVR)
*   Renal replacement therapy, steroids (inotrope-resistant shock), ECMO

### Junctional Ectopic Tachycardia (JET) (Figure 7)

**Incidence:** 5–15% post-cardiotomy; occurs 24–72 hours post-op, resolves in 7–10 days

**Risk factors:** Younger age, low weight, long CPB/cross-clamp times, DHCA, complex surgery, TOF/AVSD, genetic syndromes, electrolyte disturbances, acidosis, inotropes

**ECG features:** Non-sinus tachycardia, QRS similar to sinus, AV dissociation (ventricular rate > atrial rate), P waves lost in QRS, regular R-R intervals

**Management:**
*   Sedation/muscle relaxation
*   Correct electrolytes (Mg²⁺, K⁺, Ca²⁺), acidosis
*   Reduce temperature (aim 35°C)
*   Reduce inotropes (especially dopamine)
*   Antiarrhythmics: α-agonists (clonidine, dexmedetomidine), amiodarone, β-blockers (esmolol)
*   Overdrive pacing (AAI/DDD at 10–15 bpm above JET rate)
*   ECMO if refractory

### Renal Replacement Therapy (RRT)

**Incidence:** 2–7% post-cardiotomy; associated with increased mortality

**Indications:** Fluid overload, severe AKI, oliguria/anuria, profound acidosis, hyperkalaemia, acute-on-chronic renal failure

| Modality | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|----------|------------|---------------|
| **Peritoneal dialysis (PD)** | Safe, no vascular access, no anticoagulation, simple equipment | Limited clearance, haemodynamic instability with intermittent shifts, limited to neonates/infants |
| **CRRT (haemodialysis)** | Predetermined fluid removal, consistent clearance, continuous (less instability), works for all ages | Large venous catheter, anticoagulation required, complex |

### Chylothorax

**Diagnosis:** Pleural fluid triglycerides >1.1 mmol/L (if enterally fed), lymphocytes >80%, chylomicrons

**Causes:** Thoracic duct injury (arch surgery), elevated systemic venous pressures (Glenn/Fontan, SVC obstruction, RV failure), congenital lymphatic abnormalities

**Management:**
*   Chest drain
*   Treat reversible causes (e.g., SVC thrombosis)
*   Nutritional: continue normal enteral feed (breast milk) if tolerated; if losses increase → medium-chain triglyceride (MCT) feed (not absorbed via lymphatics); if persistent → TPN
*   Octreotide
*   Surgery: thoracic duct ligation, pleurodesis, pleurectomy (if recurrent)

### Postoperative Management by Procedure

| Procedure | Key Considerations |
|-----------|-------------------|
| **PA band** | Loose band: minimise L→R shunt, pulmonary oedema; tight band: ↑PVR, inotropes, possible recalibration |
| **BT shunt** | Anticoagulation (heparin → aspirin), balance circulations, rapid extubation; shunt dependency critical (failure → no pulmonary flow → rapid arrest) |
| **Arterial switch (TGA)** | Monitor coronary perfusion: Left CAPP = Ao diastolic BP – LAP; Right CAPP = Ao diastolic BP – CVP. Avoid diastolic hypotension, avoid large fluid boluses |
| **TAPVC** | Obstructed = surgical emergency; post-op: pulmonary hypertension, residual PV obstruction, pleural effusion/chylothorax |
| **ALCAPA** | High-risk (pre-op LV dysfunction, ischaemia); monitor for coronary ischaemia (ST changes, regional wall motion); low threshold for ECMO |
| **L→R shunt repair** | Low-pressure shunts (ASD) low risk; pressure-loading shunts (VSD, AVSD, truncus) require milrinone; pulmonary hypertension risk |
| **R→L shunt repair (TOF)** | Restrictive RV physiology: ↓preload tolerance, ↑CVP, poor end-organ perfusion; manage with minimal PEEP, fluids, vasopressors, milrinone, evacuate effusions |
| **Norwood (Stage I)** | Balance Qp:Qs (<1 post-op). Sano modification: systolic pulmonary flow (preserves diastolic pressure) → more stable haemodynamics; open chest common |
| **BCPS (Glenn)** | Passive SVC flow → passive PBF; head elevation (30°), permissive hypercapnia (↑cerebral flow → ↑SVC return), minimal ventilation pressures, early extubation |
| **TCPC (Fontan)** (Figure 8) | Passive flow from SVC and IVC; fenestration may maintain CO at expense of desaturation; manage as BCPS + avoid ↑PVR, maintain preload, consider fenestration closure if stable |

### Cardiac Arrest Post-cardiotomy (Table 4)

**Reversible causes (4Hs and 4Ts):**
*   Hypoxia, Hypovolaemia, Hypothermia, Hyper/hypokalaemia
*   Tension pneumothorax, Tamponade, Thromboembolic, Toxins

**Specific interventions:**
*   Activate ECMO team
*   Early chest opening → internal cardiac compressions
*   Inhaled nitric oxide (pulmonary hypertension)
*   Epicardial pacing (bradycardia)
*   Heparin bolus (blocked shunt)

**Tamponade:** Assume in all post-cardiotomy arrests (even without echo evidence); emergency chest re-opening may be necessary

### Outcomes (RACHS-1 Categories, Table 5)

| Category | Example Surgeries |
|----------|-------------------|
| 1 (lowest risk) | ASD repair, PAPVD, PDA, coarctation >30 days |
| 2 | VSD repair, TOF repair, Glenn, AP window, coarctation <30 days |
| 3 | Ross, valve replacement, ALCAPA repair, Fontan, PA band, arterial switch (only) |
| 4 | Arterial switch + VSD, TAPVD <30 days, Rastelli, truncus, hypoplastic arch |
| 5 | Truncus + interrupted arch, Ebstein's <30 days |
| 6 (highest risk) | Norwood |

30-day survival ~98% (UK/Ireland data); long-term morbidity: neurodevelopmental (autism, cognitive impairment, behavioural difficulties, motor delay, seizures)

### Positive Pressure Ventilation (PPV) Effects (Table 6)

| | Preload | Afterload |
|---|---|---|
| **Pulmonary ventricle** | ↓ (↓venous return, ↓RAP, ↓RVEDV) | ↑ (↑RVEDP, ↓pulmonary blood flow) |
| **Systemic ventricle** | ↓ (↓pulmonary venous return, ↓LAP, ↓LVEDV) | ↓ (↓LVEDP, ↓aortic transmural pressure) |

Extubation (return to negative pressure) has opposite effects: ↑systemic afterload, ↑pulmonary ventricle volume loading — high-risk period

### Pulmonary Hypertensive Crisis (Figure 9, Table 7)

**Pathophysiology:** Acute ↑PA pressure → ↑RV pressure → ↓LV preload (no shunt) or R→L shunt (if present) → ↓CO → bradycardia → arrest

**Management:**
*   ↓PVR: hyperventilation (↓PaCO₂), FiO₂ 1.0, iNO, milrinone, sedation, muscle relaxation, correct acidosis
*   ↑SVR: vasopressin, noradrenaline, inotropes (adrenaline)
*   Fluid bolus (↑RV preload)
*   Magnesium replacement
*   ECMO if refractory

### Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS) (Figure 11)

**Binary questions:**
*   Cardiac: Is ventricle contracting well? Empty/full? Pericardial effusion?
*   Respiratory: Pulmonary oedema? Pleural effusion? Pneumothorax? Consolidation?
*   Abdominal: Ascites?

**Advantages:** Immediate availability, real-time changes, repeatable, improves procedural safety

### Nutrition (Table 8)

**Effects of poor nutrition:** Impaired immunity, infection, poor wound healing, constipation/ileus, vitamin/mineral deficiency, hypoproteinaemia

**Effects of overfeeding:** Fluid overload, infection, diarrhoea, obesity

**High-risk for ischaemic colitis (feed cautiously):** Low systemic output lesions (HLHS, critical AS, interrupted arch, coarctation), prolonged CPB, ECMO, previous abdominal pathology, long-term poor nutrition (refeeding syndrome risk)

**Enteral feeding preferred:** Contraindications: NEC, ischaemic colitis, GI bleeding, mechanical obstruction. Multiple inotropes NOT an absolute contraindication

### End-of-Life Care

**Process:**
1.  MDT consensus that treatment no longer in child's best interest
2.  Family discussions (time, understanding)
3.  Palliative care involvement
4.  Consider organ donation, memory boxes, religious support
5.  Withdrawal: location (home/hospice/hospital), who present, symptom management
6.  Post-death: team support, debrief, documentation, family follow-up

---

## Chapter 9: Adult Congenital Cardiac Intensive Care

### Where Should ACHD Patients Be Managed?

AHA and ESC advise that ACHD patients are managed in **specialist centres** with specific resources and expertise. If transfer is not possible due to clinical status, expert advice should be sought until transfer is possible.

### Most Common Indications for ICU Admission

| Type | Indications |
|------|-------------|
| **Post-surgical** | Recovery post-cardiac surgery (commonest) |
| **Medical acute/emergency** | Arrhythmia/cardiac arrest, LV failure, endocarditis, RV failure, haemoptysis, syncope |

### Risk Factors for Increased Perioperative Mortality

- Complex congenital lesions (higher mortality than simple lesions despite similar APACHE II scores; Figure 1)
- Deranged thyroid function tests
- Elevated serum creatinine and bilirubin

![](_page_1_Figure_4.jpeg)

**Figure 1. Surgical scoring and risk stratification in ACHD patients comparing observed outcomes using Parsonnet, Logistic EuroSCORE, and Ontario systems. * = p<0.05.**

### Main Differences from Non-Congenital Cardiac Intensive Care

ACHD patients often have significantly different normal physiological status. Key differences:
- High incidence of RV dysfunction (associated with renal/hepatic impairment, GI dysfunction, prolonged respiratory support)
- Certain ICU trends may not be well tolerated: volume resuscitation, systemic oxygen delivery, sedation, vasoactive drugs, positive airway pressure ventilation

### Principles of Managing Critically Ill ACHD Patient

In addition to standard principles:
1. Know the underlying cardiopulmonary anatomy
2. Understand the patient's normal physiology
3. Appreciate how interventions may affect the circulation
4. Anticipate potential pitfalls in monitoring, investigations, and interventions

### Baseline Information Required on ICU Arrival

- Primary cardiac lesion(s)
- Surgical/catheter interventions undertaken
- Important residual haemodynamic lesions (static or dynamic)
- Optimal intra-operative and post-operative haemodynamics and ventilatory settings
- Post-operatively: whether surgeon achieved all goals, unexpected challenges

### Minimal Baseline Investigations

**Compare admission values with last well values for:**
- Haemoglobin
- Oxygen saturations
- Systemic and pulmonary pressures
- ECG (beware: massive atrial enlargement may cause atrial tachycardia mimicking sinus tachycardia)
- Cardiac function (most recent echo/CMR)

**Additional investigations:**
- CXR, routine labs, haematinics (if cyanotic), coagulation screen (citrate-adjusted if haematocrit >60), pregnancy test, blood cultures, serum/faecal alpha-1 antitrypsin (Fontan with ascites)

### Challenging Situations

**Blood pressure monitoring inaccuracies:**
- Previous/existing BT shunt → use contralateral arm; bilateral shunts → lower body readings may be more accurate
- Coarctation, previous femoral bypass, multiple catheterisations → lower limb pressure may underrepresent central pressure
- Radial line cannulation/surgical cutdown → may have ulnar-dominant or absent radial artery (cuff pressure usually accurate)

**Volume status:** Cyanotic and univentricular patients tolerate hypovolaemia poorly. CVP monitoring not possible in Fontan/TCPC.

**Cardiac output measurement:** Challenging in complex CHD. PA catheterisation not possible in tricuspid/pulmonary atresia, Fontan, or TCPC. Intracardiac/extracardiac shunts make PA catheter data unreliable.

### Pacing Differences

- Some circulations make transvenous pacing impossible (e.g., tricuspid atresia) → alternative access needed (Figure 2)
- Restrictive ventricular disease with limited stroke volume may require high pacing rates (up to 120 bpm)
- Pacing optimisation using echocardiography can be helpful for borderline cardiac output

![](_page_5_Picture_6.jpeg)

**Figure 2. Access for pacing in tricuspid atresia. A = atrial or proximal pulmonary vein; B = epicardial; C = transaortic access; TA = tricuspid atresia; AoV = aortic valve; RV = rudimentary right ventricle; LV = left ventricle; MV = mitral valve.**

### Respiratory Support Differences

- Craniofacial abnormalities may complicate intubation and make NIV impossible
- Tracheostomy may be complicated by collateral vessels or abnormal airway anatomy
- Associated congenital pulmonary disease (hypoplastic lung, severe V/Q mismatch)
- Pulmonary hypertension may or may not need treating — compare with previous investigations
- Consider phrenic nerve injury (previous cardiac surgery) and congenital musculoskeletal deformities when weaning

### Non-Cardiopulmonary Organ Dysfunction

| System | Considerations |
|--------|----------------|
| **GI** | Asplenia/polysplenia, GI/renal dysfunction |
| **Renal** | Cyanotic CHD → common renal dysfunction; poor tolerance of IV contrast (rapid K⁺ rise post-procedure) |
| **Hepatic** | Abnormal LFTs common (high venous pressure, low CO, or medication) |
| **Thyroid** | Dysfunction common; manage in context of critical illness |
| **Feeding** | Severe RV dysfunction → low enteral feeding rates to avoid ileus |

### Specific Postoperative Considerations

| Procedure | Key Considerations |
|-----------|-------------------|
| **Coarctation repair** | Rebound hypertension, mesenteric ischaemia, spinal neurological damage (rare). Role of CSF drainage unknown in ACHD |
| **Interventional ASD closure** | High incidence of arrhythmias; elderly patients may have restrictive LV and RV disease; if CO suboptimal despite pacing/inotropes/volume → consider fenestration (at expense of cyanosis) |
| **AVSD repair** | If inadequate CO post-op with increasing inotropes → exclude tamponade, LV/RV failure, arrhythmia, LVOT obstruction (echocardiography) |
| **Tetralogy of Fallot (re-do)** | Most have uncomplicated course; severe pre-op RV dysfunction managed as usual; acute lung injury possible with increased pulmonary blood flow |
| **Ebstein's anomaly** | Care: maintain sinus rhythm, avoid elevated venous pressure, avoid RV over-distension, measure filling pressures and CO, maintain normoxia/normocarbia, avoid aggressive ventilation. Worsening LFTs = inadequate CO with high venous pressure (not drug reaction). Consider fenestration or mechanical circulatory support if CO inadequate |

### Cyanotic Heart Disease — ICU Management

**Adaptive mechanisms:** Increased CO, increased haematocrit, rightward shift of oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve

**Key principles:**
- Do not normalise O₂ saturations to non-cyanotic levels — target patient's "well" saturations (unless intervention intended to restore normoxia)
- Haemostatic abnormalities correlate with erythrocytosis — usually do not need treating
- Use citrate bottles adjusted for haematocrit when measuring INR
- Intolerant of hypovolaemia — keep well hydrated
- Transfusion threshold unclear — some intensivists have lower threshold
- Routine venesection NOT indicated; iron deficiency common
- High suspicion for cerebral abscess or haemorrhage with abnormal neurology/headache/postictal
- Hyperviscosity → renal hypoperfusion → renal failure. Hydrate well before IV contrast; those with significant kidney injury at risk of marked hyperkalaemia post-contrast

### Failing Right Ventricle (Subpulmonary) — Management (Table 1)

**Optimise preload:**
- Diuretics (overfilling increases RV filling pressure, size, wall stress)
- Cautious vasoconstriction (noradrenaline) with volume resuscitation (excessive vasodilation may reduce RV preload, especially in restrictive RV)

**Reduce afterload:**
- Optimise airway pressures (balance against atelectasis/hypoxaemia)
- Manage pleural collections aggressively
- Treat bronchoconstriction with bronchodilators
- Elevated PVR → inhaled or systemic pulmonary vasodilators
- Aim for normoxia and normocarbia
- Early extubation recommended (positive pressure reduces venous return)

**Pulmonary hypertension treatment (if CO inadequate in context of subpulmonary RV failure):**
- Inhaled nitric oxide
- Nebulised prostacyclin
- Oral/IV sildenafil (caution: systemic effects)

**Mechanical circulatory support** if progressive/worsening haemodynamics

<details class="med-details"><summary>

#### TABLE 1: Improving Cardiac Output by Optimising Preload and Reducing Afterload</summary><div class="details-content">

| PRELOAD OPTIMISATION | AFTERLOAD REDUCTION |
|----------------------|---------------------|
| **Diuretics:** Overfilling increases RV filling pressure, size and wall stress, impairing RV function | **Airway pressures:** Optimising airway pressures (minimising peak, plateau and PEEP) balanced against basal atelectasis and hypoxaemia |
| **Pressors:** Excessive systemic vasodilatation may reduce RV preload, particularly in restrictive RV. Cautious vasoconstriction (with noradrenaline) may be indicated, together with volume resuscitation | **Pleural collections:** Manage aggressively in context of RV failure |
| **Feeding/nutrition:** Any increase in intra-abdominal pressure reduces venous return to right heart. Post-op status + borderline CO + high venous pressure → high risk of gut ischaemia. Introduce enteral feeding cautiously | **Airways:** Bronchoconstriction treated with bronchodilators |
| | **Pulmonary vascular resistance:** Evidence of elevated PVR → inhaled or systemic pulmonary vasodilators |
| | **Arterial blood gases:** Hypoxia and/or hypercarbia → pulmonary vasoconstriction. Aim for normoxia and normocarbia |
| | **Positive pressure ventilation:** Reduces venous return; early extubation recommended (balance against atelectasis and abnormal ABGs) |

</div></details>

### Failing Systemic Morphologically Right Ventricle

**Seen in:** ccTGA, TGA repaired with atrial switch

**Management:**
- Seek and treat reversible causes (arrhythmia, volume overload)
- Coronary arteries usually unobstructed; if ECG suggests ischaemia → coronary angiography
- IABP may be helpful in older patients (use appropriately sized balloon for aortic diameter)
- Role of levosimendan uncertain
- Multi-site pacing may help; even simple HR optimisation and AV delay modification may alter haemodynamics
- Early mechanical circulatory support if progressive/worsening

### Fontan Circulation — ICU Management

**Challenges:** Univentricular circulation + systemic venous hypertension

**Key principles:**
- Passive flow depends on adequate preload and avoidance of pulmonary hypertension
- Positive pressure ventilation significantly reduces CO → keep intrathoracic pressure low, drain pleural effusions, avoid pulmonary vasoconstrictors, treat bronchoconstriction
- Aim for early extubation
- Very poor tolerance of hypovolaemia
- High risk of atrial arrhythmias (can be lethal) and thrombi
- Some patients (e.g., TCPC) may not have transvenous access for pacing → external pacing available
- Always involve an expert

### Univentricular Heart (Non-Fontan) — ICU Management

- Nature of pulmonary connections must be considered when using inotropes/vasoactive agents
- Unprotected pulmonary circulation (with/without Eisenmenger) → minimise further increase in PA pressures
- Protected pulmonary circulation (PA stenosis/banding) → relative effects of pulmonary vs systemic constriction/dilatation may differ
- Absent pulmonary connection with systemic-pulmonary collaterals/shunts:
    - Small ↑PVR → significant ↓pulmonary blood flow → dramatic desaturation
    - ↑SVR and/or ↓PVR → ↑systemic-pulmonary shunting → ↓CO
- Oxygen administration may alter balance between pulmonary and systemic circulations
- Any vasoactive drug should be used with care — results may be unpredictable

### Systemic-Pulmonary Shunt — Specific Considerations

- Patients with central or BT shunt depend on systemic arterial pressure to maintain pulmonary blood flow
- Desaturation may require systemic vasoconstriction
- Aggressive ventilatory manoeuvres may reduce pulmonary blood flow
- Blood pressure measurement: previous classic/modified BT shunt → reduced arterial pressure in ipsilateral arm

---

## Chapter 10: Cardiopulmonary Bypass

### Aims of Cardiopulmonary Bypass (CPB)

CPB facilitates open heart surgery by:
*   Completely replacing heart and lung function
*   Providing a clear operating field
*   Maintaining O₂ delivery and CO₂ removal
*   Emptying the heart → minimising myocardial O₂ demand
*   Providing myocardial and end-organ protection: cardioplegia (stops cellular metabolism) + hypothermia (↓cellular O₂ demand)

### CPB Circuit Components (Figure 1)

| Component | Function |
|-----------|----------|
| **Venous line (blue)** | Drains blood from SVC/IVC (or RA/hepatic veins) |
| **Reservoir** | Hard-shell plastic container with filters and volume markings |
| **Pump** | Roller pump (flow set regardless of afterload) or centrifugal pump (flow dependent on preload/afterload) |
| **Oxygenator** | Microporous fibres; gas exchange across micropores |
| **Heat exchanger** | Incorporated into oxygenator; precise thermoregulation |
| **Arterial line (red)** | Returns blood to arterial circulation (antegrade or retrograde) |
| **Cardiotomy suction (green)** | Removes blood from operative field |

### Safety Features

*   **Level detector** (reservoir) — stops arterial pump if venous return drops (prevents air entrainment)
*   **Ultrasonic bubble alarm** (arterial line) — stops pump if bubbles detected
*   **One-way valves** (suckers) — prevent air being blown into patient
*   **Arterial line pressure monitors** — alarm if cannulae obstructed (prevents circuit rupture)
*   **Filters** (reservoir, oxygenator) — prevent gaseous/particulate microemboli
*   **In-line monitors** — Hct, K⁺, lactate, O₂
*   **Hand crank** — for electrical failure

### CPB Pumps

| Pump Type | Characteristics |
|-----------|-----------------|
| **Roller pump** (Figure 2) | Flow set regardless of afterload; requires adequate venous return; risk of spallation (tubing breakdown) |
| **Centrifugal pump** (Figure 3) | Non-occlusive; flow dependent on preload/afterload; backflow possible; more common in ECMO/VAD |

### Priming Constituents for Congenital Heart Surgery

*   **Crystalloid prime** (Hartmann's, Plasma-Lyte™) — maintains tonicity
*   **Packed red blood cells** — added in neonates/infants (Hct >24% on CPB associated with better outcomes; Hct >40 in single-ventricle neonates)
*   **Colloids** (gelatins, hetastarch, albumin) — theoretical oedema reduction (outcome benefit unproven)
*   **Mannitol** — osmotic diuretic, membrane stabiliser, free radical scavenger
*   **Sodium bicarbonate/THAM** — corrects base deficit
*   **FFP** — improves fibrinogen post-CPB (benefit inconclusive)
*   **Aprotinin** — for redo surgery (bleeding risk)

### Parameters Monitored During CPB

**Circuit parameters:**
