*   Reservoir volume, venous return, arterial flow
*   Line pressures (arterial, cardioplegia) — high pressures (300–350 mmHg) stop pump

**Fluid parameters:**
*   Total gas flow, FiO₂
*   PaO₂ (target 20–30 kPa) — hyperoxic bypass may increase myocardial injury in cyanotic patients
*   SvO₂ — indicator of systemic perfusion (complicated by collaterals)
*   PaCO₂ — CO₂ addition prevents respiratory alkalosis
*   Hct — higher Hct strategy in infants associated with better outcomes
*   pH, K⁺, lactate — acidosis/lactate suggests inadequate perfusion
*   Blood glucose — rises during CPB (inflammatory response)

**Patient parameters:**
*   Arterial pressure — adjusted by pump flow or vasoactive drugs
*   RAP — should be zero on CPB (optimal venous drainage)
*   Core/peripheral temperature — avoid large gradients (<10°C)
*   Cerebral NIRS — indication of cerebral perfusion (especially in paediatrics)

### Differences: Congenital vs Acquired Cardiac Disease

| Feature | Congenital | Acquired |
|---------|-----------|----------|
| Patient age range | Preterm neonates to adults | Mostly adults |
| Circuit size | Smaller circuits; greater haemodilution | Larger circuits |
| Repeat procedures | Common (multiple staged/redo surgeries) | Less common |
| Sternotomy risk | Increases with number of procedures; femoral cannulation may be needed | Lower risk |
| Systemic-pulmonary collaterals | Common (difficult to assess tissue O₂ delivery) | Rare |
| DHCA requirement | Greater | Less |

### Anticoagulation

*   **Heparin** — added to CPB prime + IV bolus
*   **Target ACT:** 400–480 seconds
*   **Neonates/infants:** Less predictable efficacy/duration (variable clotting factors, immature metabolism)
*   **HIT:** Rare but possibly under-recognised

### Arterial Cannulation (Figures 4, 5)

*   Cannula must be large enough for adequate flow but small enough to avoid vessel obstruction
*   **Restenotomy:** Alternative sites (femoral, subclavian) for CPB prior to sternotomy
*   **Arterial switch procedure:** Distal placement to avoid flow directed away from arterial branches
*   **Stage I HLHS:** Cannula in pulmonary artery (aorta small/absent)
*   **Complex arch reconstructions:** Two cannulae for upper and lower body perfusion

### Venous Cannulation

*   Blood drains by gravity (siphon effect)
*   Separate IVC and SVC cannulae for optimal drainage
*   **Paediatric challenges:** Smaller vessels, smaller tubing reduces gravity siphon → may require vacuum-assisted drainage
*   **Restenotomy:** Femoral route may require vacuum assistance
*   **Unusual anatomy:** Left/bilateral SVC, azygos/hemiazygos continuation → >2 venous cannulae

### Venting the Heart

**Purposes:** Clear operative field, prevent heart warming, avoid distension (↑wall tension, ↓subendocardial perfusion), de-air heart

**Sources of blood return:**
*   Thebesian/bronchial veins (normal)
*   Systemic-pulmonary shunts (collaterals, PDA)
*   Abnormal anatomy (persistent left SVC)
*   Surgically implanted shunts (BT, Potts)
*   Coronary sinus (cardioplegia)

**Vent sites:** Left ventricle (antegrade via RSPV/LA or retrograde via aortic valve), right ventricle (antegrade)

### Flow Rate Calculation

$$\text{Flow (LPM)} = \text{BSA} \times \text{CI}$$

**DuBois formula:**
$$BSA = \sqrt{\frac{\text{Height (cm)} \times \text{Weight (kg)}}{3600}}$$

**Cardiac index (CI):** Adults 2.4; Paediatrics 2.6–2.8 (higher metabolic rate)

### Gas Exchange (Figure 6)

*   Gases diffuse across micropores (concentration gradients)
*   Microporous fibres orientated perpendicular/counter-current to blood flow → ↑efficiency
*   **Fick's law:** ↑concentration gradient → ↑diffusion
    *   ↑O₂ transfer: ↑O₂ concentration in fibres (blender)
    *   ↑CO₂ removal: ↑gas flow rate ("sweep")

### Myocardial Protection

**Match O₂ supply with demand:**
*   Adequate aortic root pressure
*   Empty ventricle (minimal wall tension)
*   Optimal Hct (high for O₂ carriage)
*   Adequate flow through small coronary vessels (low viscosity)

**Reduce O₂ demand:**
*   Hypothermia (↓metabolic rate)
*   Cardioplegia (↑extracellular K⁺ → paralysis) (Figure 7)

**Cardioplegia types:**
*   Blood cardioplegia (older children/adults) — ratios 4:1 or 1:1 (blood:cardioplegia)
*   Crystalloid (neonates/infants) — avoids viscous blood at low temperatures

### Cardiac Arrest Indications

**Arrest required for:**
*   Complex surgery on still heart
*   Surgery on very young myocardium
*   Risk of systemic air (intracardiac shunt, left-sided access, single ventricle)

**Arrest not required for:**
*   Extracardiac shunt (BT shunt)
*   Right-sided abnormality without intracardiac communications

### Myocardial Arrest Principles

**High-concentration solutions (St. Thomas'):** Depolarisation
*   K⁺ (high concentration) — alters resting membrane potential; side effects: vasoconstriction, Ca²⁺ loading
*   Mg²⁺ — inhibits myosin phosphorylase, protects ATP

**Depleted solutions (Bretschneider):** Hyperpolarisation
*   Na⁺ depletion — eliminates Na⁺ differential
*   Ca²⁺ depletion — prevents contractile force
*   Lidocaine — Na⁺ channel block (vasodilation)
*   Adenosine — enhances recovery

**Delivery routes:**
*   Antegrade via aortic root (requires competent aortic valve)
*   Antegrade directly into coronary vessels (caution with small ostia) (Figure 8)
*   Retrograde via coronary sinus (uncommon in CHD)

**Hypothermia:**
*   Local (ice, cooling jacket) — ↓50% O₂ consumption per 10°C
*   Systemic — decreases heat delivered to heart

**Hypothermic fibrillation (<24°C):** Still field, myocardium perfused if systemic pressure >70 mmHg; useful for redo surgery (avoids coronary injury). Problems: impaired coronary flow (compression), incompetent aortic valve (distension).

### Complications of CPB

**Paediatric:**
*   Immature organs more vulnerable
*   Lung injury (high PVR + inflammation + ischaemia-reperfusion)
*   Neurological: CVA, seizures, intracranial haemorrhage, delayed choreoathetoid syndrome
*   DHCA-related: NEC, perforation, bowel ischaemia
*   Cyanosis/hypertrophy → more sensitive to ischaemia-reperfusion injury
*   Acute kidney injury (common)

**ACHD:**
*   Increased catastrophic bleeding (redo procedures)
*   Pre-existing coagulopathy (liver failure + dilution)
*   Concurrent renal abnormalities

### Myocardial Injury Causes

*   **Cross-clamp ischaemic energy depletion** — may be reversible; hypoxic hearts less tolerant
*   **Reperfusion injury** — Ca²⁺ overload, free radicals, complement activation, endothelial injury, oedema, ↓NO → vasospasm, PHT, RV failure, air embolism
*   **LV distension** (inadequate venting)
*   **Failed myocardial protection** — long intervals between cardioplegia, impaired delivery (AR, anomalous coronaries, absent coronary sinus, coronary sinusoids)

### Temperature Control (Figure 9)

*   CPB heater-cooler with closed water circuits
*   Maintain thermal gradient <10°C (prevents microbubble formation during rewarming)
*   Children: smaller core-peripheral gradients (ineffective temperature regulation); large gradients → rapid heat loss from core to periphery

### Cerebral Protection (Figure 10)

**Optimise O₂ delivery:**
*   Adequate Hb (children 90 g/L, adults 80 g/L, cyanotic 120 g/L)
*   Adequate arterial saturations
*   Cerebral blood flow (↑CPB flow or ↑CO₂ → vasodilation)

**Reduce O₂ demand:**
*   Hypothermia (ice packs useful in infants with unfused fontanelles)
*   Sedation/analgesia

**Monitoring:**
*   Internal jugular venous saturation (>95% → minimal extraction)
*   NIRS (continuous transcranial)

**Drugs (uncertain benefit):** Corticosteroids (↓inflammation but ↑infection risk), mannitol (reduces fluid overload but may damage intracellular neuronal volume)

**Acid-base management during hypothermia:**

| Strategy | Method | Advantages |
|----------|--------|------------|
| **Alpha stat** | Allow pCO₂ to drift (blood becomes alkalotic as patient cools) | Maintains cerebral autoregulation, normal protein function, intracellular neutrality |
| **pH stat** | Add CO₂ to maintain pCO₂ 5.3 kPa, pH 7.4 at patient temperature | ↑cerebral blood flow (better cooling, ↑embolic load), minimises left shift of ODC |

pH stat may be better with DHCA (cerebral vasodilation provides more O₂ during cooling); alpha stat used for rewarming.

### Indications for Deep Hypothermic Circulatory Arrest (DHCA)

*   Aortic arch surgery (coarctation with arch hypoplasia, interrupted arch, Norwood, PHACES complex, descending aortic surgery for spinal protection)
*   Complex neonatal/paediatric surgery with multiple systemic-pulmonary collaterals
*   Difficult obstructed TAPVC
*   Unifocalisation of MAPCAs

### Principles of DHCA (Table 1)

**Cooling:** 15–20°C (cerebral metabolism ↓6–7% per 1°C)

**Risks:**
*   Impaired microcirculation (↑viscosity, vasoconstriction) — minimise with haemodilution (Hct 20% at 20°C)
*   Neurological injury, reduced neurocognitive function
*   Coagulopathy, platelet dysfunction
*   SIRS (capillary leak, oedema, organ dysfunction)

**Safety (time- and temperature-dependent):**

<details class="med-details"><summary>

#### TABLE 1: Safe Duration of Deep Hypothermic Circulatory Arrest at Different Temperatures</summary><div class="details-content">

| Temperature (°C) | 'Safe' DHCA Time (minutes) |
|------------------|---------------------------|
| 36 | 1 |
| 32 | 5 |
| 28 | 10 |
| 24 | 20 |
| 20 | 30–40 |
| 16 | 45–60 |

</div></details>

**Selective cerebral perfusion:**
*   **Antegrade** (Figure 11): Cannulate right axillary/subclavian arteries → clamp brachiocephalic artery → flow to right common carotid; additional cannula in left common carotid. Flow 10–30 mL/kg/min, pressure 30–70 mmHg
*   **Retrograde** (Figure 12): Attach arterial line/shunt to SVC. Flow 250–400 mL/min, pressure 25–40 mmHg; deoxygenated blood in aortic arch indicates cerebral O₂ uptake

### Renal Protection

*   Kidneys at risk during low flow/pressure
*   **Haemofiltration techniques:**
    *   CUF (continuous ultrafiltration) — during CPB
    *   ZBUF (zero-balance ultrafiltration) — during rewarming
    *   MUF (modified ultrafiltration) — post-CPB (caution: hypotension)
*   **Goal-directed perfusion:** DO₂ target 270–280 mL/min/m² + high Hct → significantly reduces postoperative acute renal dysfunction

### Anticoagulation Reversal (Protamine)

**Protamine reactions:**

| Class | Type | Mechanism | Treatment |
|-------|------|-----------|-----------|
| **Class 1** | Hypotension | Histamine | Fluids, vasopressors |
| **Class 2a** | True anaphylaxis | IgE antibodies | Stop protamine, adrenaline, hydrocortisone, chlorphenamine, bronchodilators |
| **Class 2b/c** | Anaphylactoid (immediate/delayed) | Complement cascade | Usually less severe |
| **Class 3** | Catastrophic pulmonary vasoconstriction | Complement + thromboxane A₂ → PHT, RV failure, shock, bronchospasm | Stop protamine, adrenaline, milrinone, often reinstitute CPB (with heparin) |

### Blood Conservation Challenges

**Paediatric:**
*   Small blood volume
*   Higher Hct requirement during CPB
*   Increased cyanotic heart disease
*   Immature coagulation ± congenital factor deficiency
*   Increased platelet dysfunction
*   Profound hypothermia more common

**ACHD:**
*   Redo operations (pre-emptive femoral CPB → prolonged dissection → cumulative blood loss)
*   Abnormal coagulation
*   Difficult cross-matching (antibodies from previous transfusions)

**Conservation techniques:**
*   Retrograde/antegrade autologous priming
*   Miniature circuits with vacuum-assisted venous drainage
*   Whole blood from venous line given before protamine (after MUF)

### Strategies to Limit Blood Loss

**Antifibrinolytics:**
*   **Tranexamic acid:** 100 mg/kg bolus + 10 mg/kg/hr infusion
*   **Aprotinin:** Test dose 1 mL (10,000 KIU), loading 4 mL/kg (40,000 KIU/kg, max 2M KIU), pump prime 4 mL/kg, infusion 1–2 mL/kg/hr. BART trial showed increased death/stroke/renal impairment → temporary suspension; reinstituted 2012 (greater benefits than risks)

**Topical haemostatic agents:**
*   Fibrin sealants (Tisseel, Evicel)
*   Tissue adhesives (BioGlue®) — NOT for children (prevents tissue growth)
*   Gelatin matrices (Floseal, Surgiflo, Surgicel, Gelfoam)
*   Platelet-rich fibrin (Vivostat PRF®) — autologous

**Other:**
*   Viscoelastic tests (TEG®, ROTEM®) — guide transfusion
*   Filtration — concentrates platelets/clotting factors
*   Erythropoietin — pre-op for low Hb
*   Autologous blood donation — age >3 years, weight >15 kg

### CPB Management with Aortopulmonary Shunt

**Examples:** Ductus arteriosus, MAPCAs, surgical shunts (BT, central Gore-Tex®), multiple small aortopulmonary vessels

**Challenge:** Cessation of systemic ejection → excessive pulmonary run-off → systemic hypoperfusion

**Management:**
*   Shunt-dependent pulmonary circulations → control shortly after commencing bypass
*   Shunt-dependent systemic circulations → cooling period to preserve end-organ function
*   Ejection during cooling helps maintain systemic perfusion
*   May require elevated bypass flows and vasopressors
*   If shunt cannot be controlled: hypothermia reduces O₂ demand → temporary reduction of bypass flow
*   DHCA may be necessary for intracardiac repair (excessive left atrial return)
*   Once heart stops ejecting: adequate left atrial drainage mandatory (prevents lung congestion)

---

## Chapter 11: Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation

### Definitions and Types

*   **Extracorporeal life support (ECLS):** Any form of extracorporeal support to failing heart/lungs unresponsive to conventional treatments — includes ECMO, VAD, CPB.
*   **Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO):** ECLS technique draining venous blood, passing through oxygenator (O₂ added, CO₂ removed), returning oxygenated blood to patient.
*   **VV ECMO:** Venous drainage → oxygenator → venous return (pre-pulmonary). Respiratory support only; uses native cardiac output.
*   **VA ECMO:** Venous drainage → oxygenator → arterial return (systemic). Cardiac + respiratory support; provides high O₂ delivery, reduces preload but increases afterload.
*   **AV ECMO:** Uses patient's arterial pressure (or pump) to drive blood across oxygenator; partial respiratory support (CO₂ removal).
*   **ECPR (Extracorporeal CPR):** Rapid ECMO deployment during cardiac arrest when conventional CPR fails to achieve ROSC within expected time frame. Studies show superiority over conventional CPR for survival and neurological outcome.

### ECMO vs CPB (Table 1)

| Feature | CPB | ECMO |
|---------|-----|------|
| Ideal use | Cardiac operating theatre | Bedside |
| Portability | Large, poorly mobile | Small, highly mobile |
| Pump | Roller pump | Centrifugal pump |
| Priming volume | Large | Small |
| Arterial filter | Yes | No |
| Venous reservoir | Yes | No |
| Anticoagulation | High (ACT >400) | Low (ACT 150–220) |
| Duration | Few hours | Few days |

### Circuit Components (Figures 1–5)

*   **Arterial and venous cannulae**
*   **Centrifugal pump** (magnetically levitating impeller) — preload-dependent, afterload-sensitive (Figures 2, 6, 7)
*   **Membrane oxygenator** with heat exchanger (Figure 3)
*   **Air/O₂ blender** and sweep gas regulator (Figure 4)
*   **Bridge** between venous and arterial sides (Figure 5)
*   **Side ports (pigtails):** Heparin infusion, pressure monitoring, blood gas sampling, CVVH connection

### Key Phenomena

*   **Pump cavitation:** Negative pressure causes venous collapse → intermittent flow. First intervention: reduce pump rpm. Also correct hypovolaemia, reposition patient, check cannula position/patency.
*   **Recirculation (VV ECMO, Figure 8):** Oxygenated blood withdrawn through venous drainage cannula without reaching systemic circulation. Solutions: reposition cannulae, add extra drainage cannula, decrease ECMO flow.
*   **Harlequin syndrome (VA ECMO):** Residual native cardiac output with impaired gas exchange → upper body perfused by poorly oxygenated blood (native ejection), lower body by oxygenated blood (ECMO arterial cannula). Solutions: increase ECMO flow, increase ventilation, reposition arterial cannula proximally, add axillary cannula, left atrial decompression.

### Cannulation Strategies (Tables 2, 3; Figures 9–13)

| Strategy | Indications | Details |
|----------|-------------|---------|
| **Central** (median sternotomy) | Post-cardiotomy failure, ECPR | Arterial cannula in ascending aorta; venous cannula in RA; optional LV vent |
| **Neck** | Non-post-cardiotomy neonates/infants | VA: RCCA + RIJV; VV: double-lumen catheter in RIJV (Avalon Elite®, ParaGlide™) |
| **Femoral** | Larger patients | VA: femoral artery + vein (opposite sides); leg ischaemia risk mitigated by side graft or retrograde reperfusion catheter |

**Venous cannulae larger than arterial** (prevents venous collapse, accommodates higher venous distensibility).

### Priming Solution

1.  CO₂ flush (displaces atmospheric O₂/N₂)
2.  Crystalloid prime (balanced solution)
3.  Albumin (coats prosthetic surfaces, ↓platelet/fibrinogen adherence)
4.  Blood (prevents haemodilution; target Hct 30–40%)
5.  Heparin (100 units per unit of PRBCs)
6.  Buffer (sodium bicarbonate)

### Indications for Left Heart Vent in VA ECMO

*   Severe LV dysfunction with prolonged lack of ejection
*   Mitral regurgitation (volume overload)
*   Cyanotic patients (increased pulmonary return from collaterals)
*   Significant ventricular return without adequate interatrial shunting
*   Single ventricle circulations (deoxygenated blood may enter coronaries)
*   Severe ventricular arrhythmias
*   When percutaneous septal fenestration cannot be obtained

**Access routes:** Right upper pulmonary vein, Waterston's groove, left atrial roof, left atrial appendage, left ventricular apex (uncommon).

### Transition from CPB to ECMO

**Indications (after excluding residual surgical lesions):**
*   Severe RV/LV failure unable to separate from CPB
*   Rapid haemodynamic deterioration unresponsive to inotropes/vasopressors
*   Rapid respiratory deterioration unresponsive to conventional ventilation
*   Severe pulmonary hypertensive crisis unresponsive to iNO
*   Cardiac arrest post-chest closure unresponsive to reopening/CPR

**Transition methods:**
*   Complete wean from CPB → protamine → controlled ECMO transition (for predominantly respiratory failure)
*   Immediate transition (ACT ~200 sec) — for patients who cannot separate from CPB

**Note:** Heparin-free ECMO run for limited time acceptable to limit severe blood loss.

### Indications for ECMO in ICU

*   Post-cardiac surgery haemodynamic/respiratory support
*   Acute medical cardiac conditions (myocarditis, malignant arrhythmias, cardiac arrest, neonatal failure)
*   Acute medical non-cardiac conditions (sepsis)
*   Respiratory failure

**Two main scenarios:**
1.  **Cardiac arrest/CPR:** If no return of function after 5 minutes internal CPR → ECPR with central VA cannulation. For non-surgical patients or high bleeding risk → neck cannulation.
2.  **Progressive deterioration despite full support:** Controlled cannulation → VA ECMO (neck for non-surgical, central for surgical if acceptable bleeding risk).

### Exclusion Criteria

**Neonatal:**
*   Gestational age <34 weeks
*   Body weight <2 kg
*   Chronic lung disease >15 days on ventilator
*   Uncontrolled bleeding/coagulopathy
*   Intracranial haemorrhage Grade III
*   Non-correctable cardiac lesions
*   Non-cardiac congenital anomalies incompatible with life
*   Severe CNS trauma

**Paediatric (ELSO guidelines):**
*   Cardiac: unrepairable CHD, severe AR, unrepaired aortic dissection, severe peripheral vascular disease, prolonged arrest >60 min, repeated arrests, failed CPR with severe MOF, unwitnessed arrest, out-of-hospital arrest (some centres)
*   Respiratory: >7–10 days on ventilator, escalating settings (PEEP >15, mean airway pressure >25, PIP >45), severe anatomical lesions, fixed elevated PVR, chronic lung disease
*   Haematological: major haemorrhage, uncontrolled coagulopathy, immunosuppression, DIC
*   Neurological: severe CNS injury, infection, hypoxic damage, trauma, toxic insult
*   Metabolic: severe chronic organ dysfunction, severe metabolic failure, poor prognosis oncological diseases, chromosomal abnormalities
*   Infective: uncontrolled septic shock

### Imaging on ECMO

*   **CXR (Figure 14):** Cannula position, dislodgment, malposition, kinks, ETT position, lung pathology, pleural collections
*   **Echocardiography (Figure 15):** Cannula position, cardiac function, atrial communications, congenital defects, weaning progress
*   **CT:** Cardiac/respiratory/CNS assessment; CT angiography with brief flow reduction
*   **Angiography:** Anatomy visualisation, cardiac interventions
*   **Ultrasound/Doppler:** CNS, intra-abdominal organs, distal limb perfusion (femoral cannulation)

### Ventilation on ECMO

**Rest settings:** FiO₂ 30%, PEEP 3–5 cmH₂O, RR 5–10 rpm, TV 5–10 mL/kg.

**Note:** On VA ECMO with cardiac ejection, increase ventilation (increased pulmonary venous return for coronary perfusion).

### Anticoagulation Management

*   **Target ACT:** 220–250 sec (180–200 sec if bleeding)
*   **Loading:** Heparin 100 IU/kg pre-cannulation
*   **Infusion:** 0.5 mL/hr or 10 IU/kg/hr, titrated
*   **Monitoring:** q15 min ×2 hours, q30 min ×1 hour, then hourly
*   **Higher ACT required:** Clot/fibrin in circuit, suboptimal flows, during weaning
*   **Lower ACT:** Persistent bleeding (can stop heparin temporarily; heparin-coated circuits can run days without heparin)
*   **Bleeding management:** Platelets, FFP, cryoprecipitate; IV tranexamic acid; surgical exploration

### Weaning from ECMO

**Pre-weaning evaluation:**
*   Pulmonary: CXR, lung compliance (manual ventilation), consider iNO for elevated PVR
*   Cardiac: Serial echocardiography; "stress echo" (increase ACT to 250 sec, start ventilation, reduce flow by 25% decrements to stop for 30–60 sec) — assesses recovery and timing
*   Renal: Plan alternative RRT if needed
*   Fluid balance: Increase oncotic pressure, vasopressors
*   Metabolic: Worsening lactic acidosis, unstable glucose, worsening MOF = poor predictors
*   Neurological: Head US/MRI
*   Infective: Worsening sepsis = poor predictor

**Weaning process:**
*   Increase ACT to 300 sec
*   Reduce flow in 25% steps over 1–2 hours
*   Regular blood gases, monitor lactate
*   **Completion:** Clamp venous line → unclamp bridge → clamp arterial line → child isolated from circuit
*   Cannulae left in place, circuit runs via bridge (flush q15–20 min)

**Signs of early failure:**
*   Poor haemodynamics (↓systemic BP)
*   ↑RA and/or LA pressure
*   Poor ventricular function on echo
*   Arrhythmias
*   Poor peripheral perfusion (↑lactate, hypoxia, hypercarbia, acidosis)
*   Poor oxygenation, high end-tidal CO₂

**"One-way wean":** ECMO not reinstituted in early failure — senior MDT decision, involve parents.

**Decannulation:**
*   Central → delayed sternal closure
*   Neck → vessel reconstruction (ligation if patency not obtained)
*   Protamine to achieve ACT 100–120 sec

### Outcomes (Table 4 — ELSO 2018)

| Population | Total Runs | Survived ECLS | Survived to Discharge |
|------------|------------|---------------|----------------------|
| **Neonatal Pulmonary** | 30,934 | 84% | 73% |
| **Neonatal Cardiac** | 7,794 | 64% | 42% |
| **Neonatal ECPR** | 1,718 | 66% | 41% |
| **Paediatric Pulmonary** | 8,820 | 67% | 58% |
| **Paediatric Cardiac** | 10,462 | 68% | 52% |
| **Paediatric ECPR** | 3,946 | 57% | 42% |

**Post-cardiac surgery survival to discharge:** 40–50%

**Risk factors for mortality:**
*   Increased ECMO duration (beyond 7 days, +12% mortality per extra day)
*   Low weight, preterm birth, pre-operative NEC
*   Post-op complications: renal failure requiring RRT, neurological impairment, bleeding, NEC on ECMO
*   Longer CPB (>400 min) and DHCA use

### Complications

**Cannulation-related:**
*   Vessel damage/dissection, haemothorax, pneumothorax (Figure 16), haemopericardium/tamponade, cannula dislodgement/malposition, limb ischaemia (especially femoral)

**On-ECMO:**
*   Bleeding (minor/major)
*   Circuit thrombus, haemolysis
*   Air in circuit
*   Pump/console/motor failure
*   Oxygenator failure
*   Tubing rupture
*   Cerebral bleed (Figure 17), infarction, oedema
*   Infection
*   Accidental decannulation (Figure 18)
*   Renal/GI complications, general ICU complications

### Emergency Scenario Management

| Problem | Actions |
|---------|---------|
| **VV ECMO hypoxia** | Pump flow >⅔ CO; FiO₂ 100% (pO₂ >150); ↑pump flow; ↑ventilation; cool; muscle relaxants; maintain Hb; add second access line |
| **VV ECMO hypercarbia** | Pump flow >⅔ CO; ↑O₂ flow to oxygenator (×2 pump flow rate); ↑pump flow; ↑ventilation; cool; muscle relaxants |
| **VV ECMO recirculation** | Check cannula position; pre-membrane pO₂ <50; reposition cannula |
| **VA ECMO hypoxaemia** | Check right radial ABG; oxygenator return line pO₂ >150; ↑pump flow; ↑ventilation, PEEP, FiO₂; readjust/replace arterial line |
| **VA ECMO hypercarbia** | Pump flow >⅔ CO; O₂ flow to oxygenator ×2 pump flow rate; ↑pump flow; ↑ventilation; cool; muscle relaxant |
| **VA ECMO bleeding** | Prevention (meticulous haemostasis, monitoring); APTT 50–75 sec (heparin can be withheld); transfuse platelets, cryo, FFP, RBCs; IV tranexamic acid; surgical exploration |
| **Haemolysis** | Monitor Hb, LFTs, U&Es, clotting; replace volume; adjust pump flow; TOE check cannulae; circuit change if needed |
| **Unable to maintain flow** | Check cannula position/volume; check kinking; pre/post-oxygenator blood sample; echo + CXR; optimise fluid/preload/afterload/contractility; decrease pump speed then increase smoothly |
| **Pump failure** | Call for help; conventional ventilation; inotropes/vasoconstrictors; use back-up console; hand cranking; replace pump head |
| **Cardiac arrest (VV)** | Call for help; CPR; reversible causes; DC shock |
| **Cardiac arrest (VA)** | Call for help; DC shock (arrest has little effect on ECMO if flows maintained) |
| **Accidental decannulation** | Call for help; clamp circuit; stop pump; CPR; ventilate; volume + CV support; prepare for surgery |
| **Air in circuit** | Clamp arterial line; stop pump; head-down position; inotropes/ventilation/volume; examine entry points |
| **Circuit rupture** | Clamp circuit; stop pump; call for help; CV + respiratory support; replace volume; prepare for surgery/circuit change |
| **Thrombus in circuit** | Maintain optimal ACT; monitor circuit; clean/change affected component |

### Stopping and Recommencing ECMO

**Roller pump circuit:**
*   Stop: Clamp venous line above bridge → open bridge clamp → clamp arterial line above bridge → stop flow
*   Restart: Open arterial clamp → clamp bridge → open venous cannula → return to previous rpm

**Centrifugal pump circuit:**
*   Stop: Close post-oxygenator clamp → clamp venous line above bridge → open bridge → clamp arterial line → release post-oxygenator clamp → maintain flow through bridge
*   Restart: Close post-oxygenator clamp → clamp bridge → open arterial clamp → open venous clamp → increase rpm → remove post-oxygenator clamp → clear bridge

### General Parameter Guidelines (Paediatric)

| Parameter | Target |
|-----------|--------|
| ACT | 160–220 sec |
| PaO₂ (VA) | 60–80 mmHg |
| PaO₂ (VV) | 45–80 mmHg |
| Haemoglobin (VA) | 13–15 g/dL |
| Haemoglobin (VV) | 15 g/dL |
| Haematocrit (VA) | >40 |
| Haematocrit (VV) | >45 |
| pH | 7.35–7.45 |
| PaCO₂ | 35–45 mmHg |
| Platelets | >75 × 10⁹/L |
| Urine output | >2 mL/kg/hr |
| Heparin | 25–50 units/kg/hr (adjust for renal function, bleeding, haemofilter) |
| Venous O₂ saturation | 70–75% |
| VA ECMO flow | 80 mL/kg/min (80% of estimated CO) |
| VV ECMO flow | 80–120 mL/kg/min (adjust for recirculation) |

### Commencing ECMO — Steps

1.  Check circuit, cannula position, secure cannulae
2.  Open venous line clamp (above bridge)
3.  Close bridge clamp
4.  Open arterial line clamp (above bridge)
5.  Slowly increase blood flow
6.  Monitor cardiovascular response
7.  Wean ventilation support
8.  Wean inotropic support
9.  Re-evaluate for air bubbles
10. Check connections and ports
11. Verify console, transducers, electrical components operational
12. Perform flow, sweep, FiO₂, drug/fluid calculations
13. Check ACT/APTT, adjust heparin
14. Optimise clotting profile
15. Inform team of set parameters and alarms
16. Recheck cannula security
17. Request echo and CXR as appropriate

### Daily Management Principles

**General:**
*   Check cannulation site, cannulae, circuit (mechanical + electrical)
*   Patient head position: midline, up to 30°

**Cardiovascular:**
*   Maintain systemic perfusion and intravascular volume
*   Monitor urine output, CVP, perfusion signs, body weight
*   Echocardiography as required
*   Check circuit for clots and integrity
*   Regular ECG and blood tests

**Respiratory:**
*   Ventilation settings
*   ABGs and CXR
*   Pulmonary hygiene, flexible bronchoscopy as needed

**Renal:**
*   First 48 hours: high incidence of ATN and oliguria → thereafter urine output usually >2 mL/kg/hr
*   If renal function does not improve → add CRRT to circuit
*   Daily renal blood tests as indicated

**CNS:**
*   Avoid paralytic agents
*   Regular sedation holds, neurological examinations (pupils, reflexes, LOC, movements)
*   Head US or CT as required (CT requires transport on ECMO)

**Infection control:**
*   Aseptic technique for all procedures
*   Check all sites for infection
*   Cultures (blood, sputum, urine)
*   Antibiotics as indicated

**Fluids, electrolytes, nutrition:**
*   Regular monitoring of electrolytes (Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺, PO₄³⁻)
*   Close fluid balance monitoring, high-calorie intake, nutritional support

**Surgical team:** Daily checks of cannulation site, cannulae, circuit; assist with dressing changes; available for transport and invasive procedures.

---

## Chapter 12: Informed Consent in Congenital Cardiac Surgery

### The Role of Consent

*   **Autonomy:** The right of every citizen to influence their own destiny; consent is the expression of this right regarding physical contact.
*   **In healthcare:** Patients (or parents) must agree in advance before any physical intervention. Lack of consent can lead to professional disciplinary action, civil litigation, or criminal prosecution.

### Legal Definitions (English Law)

| Term | Definition |
|------|------------|
| **Child** | Someone who has not yet reached 18 years |
| **Young person** | Citizens of 16 and 17 years — still children legally, but with emerging autonomy (Mental Capacity Act 2005 applies) |
| **Looked after child** | Subject to a care order (LA shares parental responsibility) or accommodated by local authority without a care order |

### Parental Responsibility

*   **Mother:** Automatically has parental responsibility
*   **Father:** Automatically if married at birth registration; unmarried fathers get it automatically when registering birth (since 2003)
*   **Acquisition:** Can be acquired later by agreement, court application, or marriage to mother
*   **Adoption:** Passed to adoptive parents on legal adoption

### Who Can Provide Consent?

*   **Parent with parental responsibility** — must act in child's best interests
*   **Children under 16** — may provide independent consent if they pass the **Gillick test** (sufficient understanding and intelligence)
*   **Young people (16–17)** — presumed to have capacity (Family Law Reform Act 1969 + Mental Capacity Act 2005)
*   **Adults (≥18)** — presumed to have capacity; if lacking capacity, proceed under Mental Capacity Act 2005 (best interests)

### Gillick Test for Competence (Under 16)

Child must understand:
*   That a choice exists
*   The nature and purpose of the procedure
*   The risks and side effects
*   The alternatives to the procedure
*   And be able to: retain information, weigh it, arrive at a decision, be free from undue pressure

**Competence rests on intelligence, maturity, and experience — not age.**

### Gillick Competent Child — Refusal of Treatment

*   Courts have **resolutely denied** otherwise competent minors the right to choose death
*   Life-saving treatment can be authorised against the child's refusal (e.g., heart transplant, blood transfusion)

### Young People (16–17) — Consent and Refusal

*   **Consent:** Can provide independent consent; parental right provides a "safety net"
*   **Refusal (elective):** Abandon procedure until dispute resolved
*   **Refusal (emergency/life-saving):** Courts invariably override child's autonomy; treatment can be given against wishes
*   **Disagreement between young person and parents:** If young person consents and parents disagree, can rely on young person's consent (but understand basis for disagreement)

### Incapacitated Adults (≥18)

*   Presumption of capacity can be rebutted under Mental Capacity Act 2005
*   If lacking capacity: proceed in patient's **best interests** (consent not required but procedure lawful)
*   **Court of Protection consultation required for:** Organ donation, non-therapeutic sterilisation, foreseeable forceful restraint, innovative/experimental treatments with ethical dilemmas
*   **Unbefriended patients:** Independent Mental Capacity Advocate (IMCA) must be appointed
*   **Advanced decisions** and **Lasting Power of Attorney** must be respected

### Disclosure of Information (Montgomery v Lanarkshire, 2015)

**Duty:** Take reasonable care to ensure patient is aware of **any material risks** involved in recommended treatment, and of any reasonable alternatives.

**Test of materiality:** Would a reasonable person in the patient's position attach significance to the risk? Or is the doctor (or should be) aware that this particular patient would attach significance to it?

**Key principles:**
*   **Dialogue is mandatory** — consent requires discussion, not a soliloquy
*   **Therapeutic exception** (rare, highly restrictive): disclosure may be withheld if it would seriously harm the patient's health (requires second opinion)
*   **No numeric threshold** for risk disclosure — materiality depends on the patient, the risk, the benefits sought, and available alternatives

### Court's Approach to Disclosure Claims

*   English courts **do not rely on expert witnesses** to set the standard for disclosure
*   Judges put themselves in the shoes of the **'reasonable' patient** and ask what such a person would want to know

### When Doubts Remain About Elective Operation

*   Abandon the procedure, reconsider, pursue different course
*   Seek colleague's opinion, MDT discussion, formal second opinion

### Role of MDT

*   No legal provision for shared responsibility — identified consultant bears responsibility for patient's outcome
*   Surgeon should **never** perform an operation believed to be counter to patient's interests
*   The "consenting" surgeon carries all responsibility for choices made when selecting what information to disclose

### Disclosure to Pregnant Mother

*   Foetus has no legal personality until born — child protection laws do not apply
*   Mother bears responsibility for risks to her own health and unborn child
*   Reasonable steps must be taken to inform mother of risks she runs; if she requests information, doctor must provide it

### Standard Consent Forms (NHS)

| Form | Use |
|------|-----|
| **Standard consent form (Figure 2)** | Patients able to provide own consent (e.g., ACHD patients) |
| **Consent Form 2 (Figure 3)** | Parent or person with parental responsibility for child/young person |
| **Consent Form 4 (Figure 4)** | Adults who lack capacity (Mental Capacity Act 2005) |

---

## Chapter 13: Atrial Septal Defects

### Definition and Classification
*   **Atrial septal defect (ASD):** A congenital lesion resulting in communication and shunting of blood between the left and right atria.
*   **True ASDs (embryologically):** only defects in the region of the fossa ovalis (ostium secundum, PFO, vestibular defect) — 75–76%.
*   **Surgically referred to as ASDs:** ostium primum (15–20%, partial AVSD), sinus venosus (5–10%, most commonly at SVC junction), coronary sinus (1%, unroofed coronary sinus).
*   **Ostium secundum:** caused by abnormal reabsorption of the septum primum or incomplete overlap of the septum secundum with the septum primum.
*   **Ostium primum:** caused by incomplete fusion of the septum primum with the endocardial cushions.
*   **Sinus venosus:** caused by incomplete fusion of the embryological sinus venosus with the atrial septum.

### Epidemiology and Associations
*   Account for 7% of all congenital cardiac abnormalities (second only to bicuspid aortic valve).
*   Female to male ratio is 2:1. Increased incidence in Down's syndrome.
*   Ten times more likely to occur with other anomalies than as an isolated defect.

### Pathophysiology and Natural History
*   Left-to-right shunt (due to differences in ventricular compliance) → right-sided volume overload → enlargement of RA, RV, and pulmonary artery.
*   If uncorrected: reduced life expectancy, complications include atrial arrhythmias, RV dysfunction, pulmonary hypertension, stroke (paradoxical embolism), rarely Eisenmenger syndrome.

### Diagnosis
*   **ECG:** incomplete RBBB (rSR' in V1). Ostium secundum — right axis deviation; primum and sinus venosus — left axis deviation.
*   **Chest radiography:** cardiomegaly (RA + RV enlargement), increased pulmonary vascular markings.
*   **Echocardiography:** gold standard — defect type, shunt quantification (Doppler), haemodynamic consequences (RV dilatation), associated anomalies.
*   **Bubble test:** intravenous injection of agitated saline — bubbles in the left atrium indicate ASD/PFO.
*   **Catheterisation:** step-up in oxygen saturation at the RA level (Table 1). Qp:Qs >1.5 is haemodynamically significant.

### Shunt Calculation (Qp:Qs)
*   Using the Fick principle: 
    $Qp:Qs = \frac{(Ao\ sats - RA\ sats)}{(PV\ sats - PA\ sats)}$
*   RA sats = (3×SVC sats + 1×IVC sats)/4.
*   Clinically significant: Qp:Qs >1.5; indication for closure: Qp:Qs >2.

### Indications and Contraindications for Closure
*   **Indications:** Qp:Qs >2; atrial arrhythmia; reversible pulmonary hypertension; RV dysfunction; PFO with right-to-left shunt and recurrent paradoxical cerebral emboli.
*   **Contraindications:** asymptomatic patient with Qp:Qs <1.5; fixed pulmonary hypertension (PASP >60 mmHg, PVR >12 WU, shunt reversal).

### Closure Methods
*   **Percutaneous (device closure):** only ostium secundum defects, 5 mm rim from defect to valves/veins, defect <40 mm. Devices: AMPLATZER™, CardioSEAL®, HELEX®.
*   **Surgical:** all ASD types. Approaches: median sternotomy, mini-thoracotomy, thoracoscopic/robotic.

### Surgical Principles by Defect Type
*   **Ostium secundum:** right atriotomy, closure with autologous pericardium or direct suture.
*   **Ostium primum:** patch attached to the crest of the ventricular septum, avoiding AV node and bundle of His + concomitant left AV valve repair (closure of anterior cleft).
*   **Sinus venosus:** pericardial baffle to redirect anomalous pulmonary veins to the left atrium; Warden procedure (SVC detachment and reimplantation) may be used.
*   **Coronary sinus:** re-roofing of the coronary sinus + closure of the interatrial defect with a second patch.

### Risks and Outcomes
*   **Mortality:** children <1%; adults up to 10% (risk factors — coexistent malformations, pulmonary hypertension, atrial fibrillation).
*   **Complications:** AV block (ostium primum, coronary sinus), mitral regurgitation (ostium primum), systemic venous obstruction (sinus venosus), residual shunt.
*   **Long-term outcomes:** closure at age <25 years — survival comparable to age-matched population. Older age at repair and preoperative NYHA Class III/IV heart failure are independent predictors of late mortality.

---

## Chapter 14: Ventricular Septal Defects

### Definition and Classification
*   **Ventricular septal defect (VSD):** A congenital cardiac malformation in which there is a hole or pathway between the ventricular chambers (ISNPCHD).
*   **Five major types:** perimembranous (most common), inlet, muscular, subarterial, Gerbode defect (LV → RA).
*   **Perimembranous:** occupies the space of the membranous septum, adjacent to fibrous continuity between valves.
*   **Muscular VSD:** exclusively muscular borders, can occur in any part of the muscular septum.
*   **Subarterial (doubly committed juxta-arterial):** positioned directly beneath semilunar valves, often with fibrous continuity between aortic and pulmonary valves.
*   **Gerbode defect:** deficiency of the AV component of the membranous septum with shunting from LV to RA.

### Epidemiology and Associations
*   Isolated VSD is the **second most common congenital cardiac malformation** (after bicuspid aortic valve): ~2 per 1000 live births.
*   Accounts for ~1/5 of all forms of congenital heart disease.
*   >50% of patients with a congenitally malformed heart have a VSD (isolated or with other anomalies).
*   **Subarterial defects** are more common in patients of **Asian descent**.
*   Associated anomalies: ASD, PDA, coarctation of the aorta, interrupted aortic arch, AVSD, TOF, DORV, common arterial trunk, TGA, univentricular heart.

### Pathophysiology and Natural History
*   Left-to-right shunt; magnitude determined by defect size, PVR, outflow obstruction, ventricular compliance.
*   Large VSD → heart failure (dyspnoea, poor feeding, infections, failure to thrive).
*   **Spontaneous closure:** common in small VSDs (<5 mm), most frequent during first year of life (up to 5 years). Mechanisms: margin fibrosis, valve leaflet adherence, septal hypertrophy.
*   Aortic valve leaflet prolapse into VSD causes aortic insufficiency — indication for surgery.
*   Unrepaired large VSD → irreversible pulmonary vascular disease by 1–2 years → Eisenmenger syndrome (PVR > SVR, cyanosis, death by age 40).

### Diagnosis
*   **ECG:** may be normal; with significant shunt — LVH, RVH, or biventricular hypertrophy.
*   **Chest radiograph:** increased pulmonary vascularity, cardiomegaly (biventricular enlargement).
*   **Echocardiography:** gold standard — defect type, borders, shunt, volume overload, associated anomalies, valve involvement.
*   **Catheterisation:** for suspected irreversible PH, older children/adults with moderate-large VSD, Qp:Qs calculation (surgery if Qp:Qs >1.5–2:1).

### Medical Therapy
*   For mild/moderate heart failure (bridge to spontaneous closure or surgery).
*   For severe heart failure (bridge to surgery).
*   Agents: cardiac glycosides, diuretics, ACE inhibitors, nutritional support.

### Palliative Treatment — Pulmonary Artery Banding (PAB)
*   Rarely performed today. Indications: Swiss cheese septum, neonatal apical muscular VSD, contraindications to CPB, LV preparation for TGA/ccTGA.
*   Target distal PA pressure: 30–50% of systemic pressure (for biventricular heart).

### Indications for VSD Closure
*   **Emergent (0–3 months):** large VSD with severe, intractable heart failure.
*   **Elective (4–6 months):** large VSD with heart failure responsive to medical therapy.
*   **Older children:** Qp:Qs >2:1, ventricular dilatation (even if Qp:Qs <2:1), aortic valve prolapse (even without AI), prior endocarditis.
*   **All doubly committed and juxta-arterial VSDs** — regardless of shunt size (no spontaneous closure, risk of AI).
*   **Contraindications:** dominant right-to-left shunt, PVR >8 units/m² with no response to vasodilators.

### Transcatheter Device Closure
*   **Perimembranous VSD:** experimental — risk of heart block and valve damage; reserve for non-surgical candidates.
*   **Subarterial VSD:** experimental — risk of aortic and pulmonary valve damage.
*   **Muscular VSD:** safe, especially for difficult-to-access defects. Hybrid approach (RV anterior wall access) available.

### Surgical Closure
*   **Approaches:** transatrial (most common), transpulmonary, transaortic, RVOT, RV apical, left ventricular (rare).
*   **Perimembranous VSD:** right atriotomy, patch (autologous pericardium, xenopericardium, PTFE, Dacron). Sutures through septal tricuspid leaflet to protect conduction tissue.
*   **Subarterial VSD:** transpulmonary approach, patch sutured to pulmonary valve annulus.
*   **Muscular VSD:** approach depends on location. Swiss cheese septum — PAB or early repair with endocardialisation.
*   **Structures at risk:** AV node and His bundle, tricuspid valve leaflets, aortic valve leaflets.

### Outcomes
*   **Operative mortality:** <1% (STS data: 0.5% for patch closure).
*   **Complications:** arrhythmias (5–15% transient, <2% require pacemaker), respiratory failure (<3%), others (<2%).
*   **Long-term:** >97% excellent outcomes. Patients with aortic leaflet prolapse require lifelong follow-up.

---

## Chapter 15: Atrioventricular Septal Defects

### Definition and Classification
*   **Atrioventricular septal defect (AVSD):** A spectrum of endocardial cushion defects defined by variable absence of the atrioventricular septum. Accounts for **3% of all congenital heart defects**.
*   **Complete AVSD:** Primum ASD + inlet VSD + common AV valve (left and right components with bridging leaflets).
*   **Intermediate AVSD:** Primum ASD + inlet VSD but with two separate AV valve orifices.
*   **Transitional AVSD:** Primum ASD + restrictive VSD + commonly a cleft in the left AV valve.
*   **Partial AVSD:** Primum ASD + usually a cleft in the left AV valve (no VSD).
*   **Rastelli classification (complete AVSD):** Type A (75%, divided superior bridging leaflet with chordal attachments), Type B (rarest, straddling chords, unbalanced), Type C (25%, undivided leaflet, no chordal attachments, associated with TOF).

### Associated Anomalies
*   Tetralogy of Fallot, LVOTO, Down syndrome (70–80% of complete AVSD), double-orifice left AV valve, additional VSDs, PDA (10%), DORV, TGA.

### Pathophysiology and Natural History
*   Left-to-right shunting at atrial level (partial/intermediate) → pulmonary volume overload → right-sided chamber dilatation → AV valve insufficiency.
*   Complete AVSD → shunting at both atrial and ventricular levels → volume and pressure overload of pulmonary circulation + left-sided chamber dilatation → heart failure within first months of life.
*   Irreversible pulmonary vascular changes as early as **6 months**, accelerated in Down syndrome.
*   Untreated → Eisenmenger complex (right-to-left shunt, cyanosis).
*   80% of untreated complete AVSD survive into 2nd–3rd decade; survival beyond 4th decade is rare.

### Diagnosis
*   **ECG:** Left axis deviation (inferior displacement of bundle of His), RV hypertrophy, occasionally prolonged PR interval.
*   **Chest radiograph:** Cardiomegaly, increased pulmonary vascular markings.
*   **Echocardiography:** Diagnostic — evaluates ASD/VSD size, AV valve regurgitation, valve/ventricular balance, LVOTO, associated anomalies. 3D echo useful for regurgitation mechanism.
*   **Catheterisation:** Not routine pre-op except in older patients for PVR calculation. Post-op for residual lesions (LVOTO, VSD).
*   **MRI:** Post-operative assessment of residual LVOTO.

### Surgical Indications and Timing
*   Diagnosis of AVSD is an indication for surgical repair.
*   **Complete AVSD:** Safe repair as early as **2 months**; waiting beyond 8 months risks obstructive pulmonary vascular disease.
*   **Partial/transitional AVSD:** After **1 year** of age (pulmonary circulation is protected).

### Surgical Principles
*   Complete repair: close all shunts (ASD, VSD, PDA), obtain two competent and non-stenotic AV valves, treat LVOTO potential, avoid conduction system.
*   Coronary sinus can be left on right or left side of patch.
*   Pulmonary artery banding (PAB) rarely performed (tendency to develop left AV valve regurgitation).

### Surgical Techniques for Complete AVSD
*   **Double patch technique:** Separate patches for VSD (Dacron® or autologous pericardium) and ASD (autologous pericardium). Cleft closure after VSD repair but before ASD repair.
*   **Single patch technique:** One large autologous pericardial patch — sutured to crest of ventricular septum, divided AV valves resuspended on patch, cleft closed, remaining patch closes ASD.
*   **Modified single patch technique (Nunn procedure):** AV valve tissue sutured directly to crest of ventricular septum with pledgeted sutures passed through septum, valve tissue, and patch.

### Partial and Transitional AVSD Repair
*   **Partial:** Left AV valve cleft closure + ASD closure with autologous pericardium.
*   **Transitional:** Restrictive VSD closure (1–2 pledgeted sutures) + left AV valve cleft closure + ASD closure with autologous pericardium.

### Complications
*   **Heart block:** Minimised by suturing VSD patch on right side of crest at posteroinferior border; closing coronary sinus into left atrium may also reduce risk.
*   **Residual ASD/VSD:** Assess by intra-op echo and saturation run. Qp:Qs formula: $\frac{\text{Aortic sats} - \text{Mixed venous sats}}{\text{Pulmonary vein sats} - \text{Pulmonary artery sats}}$
*   **LVOTO:** Usually from accessory chordal attachments requiring division.
*   **AV valve regurgitation:** Reoperation in up to 15%. Risk factors: double-orifice left AV valve, absence of Down syndrome, significant pre-op left AV valve regurgitation.

### Management of Left AV Valve Dysfunction
*   **Regurgitation (more common):** Cleft reclosure, annuloplasty at commissures, leaflet extension with autologous pericardium. Annuloplasty rings often too large for small patients.
*   **Stenosis:** Partial takedown of cleft repair (risks regurgitation), papillary muscle splitting.
*   **Valve replacement:** Last resort (smallest mechanical prosthesis 15mm — risk of heart block, coronary injury, outgrowth).

### Outcomes
*   **Early mortality (complete AVSD):** 2–5% (single institution); STS database: **2.2%**.
*   **Most common reasons for reoperation:** Left AV valve dysfunction and LVOTO → lifelong follow-up required.
*   **Unbalanced AVSD with single-ventricle palliation:** Subject to Fontan complications (protein-losing enteropathy, plastic bronchitis, liver dysfunction, diminished ventricular function).

---

## Chapter 16: Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Connections

### Definition and Spectrum
*   **Anomalous pulmonary venous connections:** Congenital defects of the pulmonary veins including PAPVC, TAPVC, atresia of the common pulmonary vein, cor triatriatum, and abnormal absorption of the common pulmonary vein.
*   **Partial anomalous pulmonary venous connection (PAPVC):** One or more (but not all) pulmonary veins drain into the right atrium or systemic veins.
*   **Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC):** All four pulmonary veins drain into the right atrium or systemic veins. Subtypes: supracardiac (most common), cardiac, infracardiac, mixed.
*   **Scimitar syndrome:** Right pulmonary vein draining into the IVC (often with right lung hypoplasia and dextrocardia).
*   **Cor triatriatum:** Accessory atrial chamber receiving pulmonary veins, communicating with the left atrium through a membrane.
*   **Pulmonary vein stenosis:** Congenital or acquired (postoperative) narrowing of individual pulmonary veins.

### Embryology
*   Common pulmonary vein arises from left atrial outpouching at 27–29 days, bifurcates twice to form four pulmonary veins.
*   Lung buds drain to splanchnic venous system; mesodermal plexus meets developing pulmonary veins at week 5.
*   Common pulmonary vein gradually incorporated into left atrial wall → all four veins enter posterior LA separately.

### Epidemiology and Associations
*   **PAPVC:** Incidence 0.6–0.7%, more common in females, occurs in 10% of patients with ASD. Associated with ASD (90% — usually sinus venosus type), heterotaxia, Scimitar syndrome.
*   **TAPVC:** 1.5% of congenital heart defects; infracardiac type 3× more common in males. Associated with ASD, PFO, PDA.
*   **Cor triatriatum:** Associated with TOF, DORV, aortic coarctation, VSD/AVSD, asplenia/polysplenia.

### Pathophysiology
*   **PAPVC:** Qp:Qs proportional to number of anomalous veins. Right heart dilatation → arrhythmias, RV failure, pulmonary hypertension.
*   **TAPVC:** All pulmonary venous return to RA → RV volume overload. Obstruction or restrictive ASD → pulmonary hypertension, decreased cardiac index.
*   **Cor triatriatum:** Pulmonary venous obstruction → pulmonary venous hypertension, reduced CO (may be compensated by ASD).
*   **Pulmonary vein stenosis:** Severity depends on number of veins involved. Bilateral arteriopathy can occur even with unilateral stenosis.

### Natural History
*   Uncorrected TAPVC with obstruction → death within first month of life.
*   Three or four stenotic pulmonary veins → mortality >80% (pulmonary hypertensive crisis, infection, haemoptysis).
*   PAPVC prognosis depends on shunt magnitude and sequelae.

### Diagnosis
*   **ECG:** May be normal; right atrial enlargement (tall P waves), RV hypertrophy (rSR pattern, right axis deviation), arrhythmias (AF/flutter, SVT).
*   **CXR:** Cardiomegaly, prominent hilum, widened mediastinum, pulmonary oedema, Scimitar sign (curvilinear opacity to IVC).
*   **MRI:** Broken ring sign (PAPVC — breach of mediastinal fat ring around SVC). Defines anatomy and haemodynamics.
*   **Echocardiography:** Diagnostic — identifies anomalous connections, PV dimensions, cor triatriatum membrane, associated defects. Colour Doppler: PV stenosis suggested by monophasic flow or velocity >1.6 m/s.
*   **Catheterisation:** Not routine for PAPVC. For TAPVC/cor triatriatum/stenosis — defines anatomy, pressures, wedge pressures.

### Pulmonary Vein Stenosis Score (Table 1)
| Score | Echo 2D | Mean Doppler gradient | Operative diameter |
|-------|---------|----------------------|--------------------|
| 0 | No stenosis | <2 mmHg, biphasic flow | Normal |
| 1 | Mild–moderate | <5–6 mmHg | 3–4 mm |
| 2 | Severe (pinpoint ostium) | >7 mmHg | <2 mm |
| 3 | Occlusion/atresia | None | No lumen |

### Therapeutic Options
*   **Medical (TAPVC with obstruction):** Sedation, hyperventilation (100% O₂, pCO₂ <30 mmHg), PGE₁, bicarbonate, haematocrit optimisation.
*   **Percutaneous:** Atrial septostomy (restrictive PFO), stenting of obstructed vertical vein, balloon dilatation of cor triatriatum membrane (limited success).
*   **Surgery:** Definitive treatment.

### Indications for Surgery
*   **PAPVC:** All patients (low morbidity/mortality); or Qp:Qs ≥2, or entire lung drains anomalously.
*   **TAPVC with obstruction:** Medical emergency — immediate surgery after optimisation.
*   **TAPVC without obstruction:** Elective surgery at 2–4 months.
*   **Contraindications:** Fixed pulmonary hypertension (PAPVC); ASD closure not suitable if pulmonary pressure >⅔ systemic.

### Structures at Risk During Surgery
*   Conduction system, sinoatrial node and its artery, left phrenic nerve.

### Surgical Techniques — PAPVC
*   **Baffle repair (double or single patch):** Glutaraldehyde-treated autologous pericardial patch redirects anomalous veins to LA and closes ASD.
*   **Warden operation:** SVC transected and reanastomosed to right atrial appendage; baffle created from anomalous veins to ASD and LA.

### Surgical Techniques — TAPVC
*   **Supracardiac:** Vertical vein ligated; incision in pulmonary venous confluence anastomosed to posterior LA (large, unobstructed anastomosis).
*   **Cardiac:** Unroof coronary sinus (between CS and fossa ovalis) → large ASD; patch reconstructs atrial septum.
*   **Infracardiac:** Similar to supracardiac; vertical/Y-shaped LA incision; some surgeons leave vertical vein unligated as low-pressure relief.
*   **Mixed:** Combination of above; sutureless technique (pericardium around PVs anastomosed to LA) may reduce postoperative stenosis.

### Surgical Techniques — Pulmonary Vein Stenosis
*   **Sutureless technique (atrial marsupialisation):** Complete resection of stenotic segments; atrial wall anastomosed directly to pericardium.
*   **Atrial pulmonary venoplasty, pericardial patch venoplasty, anastomotic revision.**
*   **Pneumonectomy:** For unilateral stenosis with haemoptysis or recurrent infection (not for CHF).

### Complications
*   Bleeding, thromboembolism, atrial arrhythmias, congestive heart failure, pulmonary hypertension and RV dysfunction, recurrent pulmonary vein stenosis, AV block (pacemaker), death.

### Long-Term Outcomes
*   **TAPVC:** Hospital mortality 2–20% (risk factors: poor pre-op status, obstruction, high PVR, young age, small LV, single ventricle). Pulmonary venous obstruction in up to 15% (usually at anastomosis). Bilateral occlusion → poor prognosis.
*   **PAPVC:** Excellent long-term prognosis. Early ASD closure eliminates arrhythmia risk. Diffuse stenosis rare.
*   **Scimitar PAPVC:** Better prognosis than PV stenosis; restenosis 3–10%. Survival depends on associated lung hypoplasia.
*   **Pulmonary vein stenosis:** High mortality; few surgical options.

---

## Chapter 17: Ebstein's Anomaly

### Definition and Pathoanatomy
*   **Ebstein's anomaly (EA):** Malformation of the right heart characterised by failure of delamination of the tricuspid valve (TV) leaflets from the underlying right ventricular (RV) myocardium.
*   **Classic findings:** Apical displacement of functional TV hinge points (most prominent in septal leaflet, then posterior, then anterior); dilatation of 'atrialised' RV; anterior leaflet fenestrations/redundancy/tethering; right atrioventricular junction dilatation.
*   **Atrialised RV:** Portion of RV between anatomical AV junction and functional TV annulus; functionally associated with RA rather than RV; may account for >50% of RV volume in severe cases.

### Classification (Carpentier)
*   **Type A:** Adherent septal and posterior leaflets, normal RV
*   **Type B:** Atrialised RV, mobile anterior leaflet
*   **Type C:** Stenotic anterior leaflet
*   **Type D:** Extensive atrialisation of RV

### Associated Anomalies and Epidemiology
*   Interatrial communication (PFO or ASD) — **80–90%**
*   VSD, RVOT obstruction (functional/anatomical pulmonary atresia, pulmonic stenosis), PDA, ccTGA
*   Left heart abnormalities: mitral valve prolapse, bicuspid aortic valve, LV non-compaction
*   **Epidemiology:** 1% of all congenital heart lesions (~1 in 20,000 live births). Maternal lithium use associated with relative risk 1.2–7.7.

### Pathophysiology
*   RV contractile dysfunction (myopathy + enlargement) + severe tricuspid regurgitation → inefficient forward flow → progressive RA and RV dilatation → further TV annular dilatation → worsening TR.

### Natural History
*   **Neonatal:** Mortality approaches 100% for severely symptomatic neonates.
*   **Beyond neonatal period:** Cumulative survival at 1, 5, 10, 15, and 20 years: 89%, 81%, 76%, 53%, and 41% respectively.
*   **Predictors of cardiac-related death:** Cardiothoracic ratio ≥0.65, younger age at diagnosis, increased TV displacement severity, cyanosis, severe TR.

### Symptoms
*   **Neonates:** Severe cyanosis or heart failure, functional/anatomical pulmonary atresia.
*   **Children:** Early fatigue or mild cyanosis (depends on heart failure degree).
*   **Adults:** May be asymptomatic; frequently present with arrhythmia (AF, atrial flutter, ectopic atrial tachycardia).

### Neonatal Echocardiographic Predictors of Mortality
*   Gestational age <32 weeks (OR 8.6, p<0.001)
*   Pulmonary regurgitation (OR 2.9, p<0.001)
*   Pericardial effusion (OR 2.5, p=0.04)
*   Increased TV annulus diameter z-score (OR 1.3, p=0.001)

### Great Ormond Street Echocardiography (GOSE) Score
*   **Index = (RA + aRV) / (RV + LV + LA)** on apical four-chamber view in end-diastole.
*   **Score 1 (<0.5):** 0% mortality; **Score 2 (0.5–0.99):** 10% mortality; **Score 3 (1–1.49):** 44–100% mortality; **Score 4 (≥1.5):** 100% mortality.

### Diagnosis
*   **CXR:** Globular cardiomegaly (RA enlargement + RVOT displacement); normal/diminished pulmonary vascularity; massive 'wall-to-wall' heart in severely symptomatic neonates.
*   **ECG:** RBBB, right atrial enlargement, pre-excitation (LBBB pattern with dominant S waves in right precordial leads), low voltage in right precordial leads, SVT, prolonged PR interval.
*   **Echocardiography:** Apical displacement of septal/posterior leaflet ≥8mm/m² BSA; failure of anterior leaflet delamination; severe TR; possible right-to-left shunt if interatrial communication present; RV enlargement/dysfunction with paradoxical septal motion.
*   **MRI:** Defines AV junction and TV leaflet attachments; quantifies RV size and function (anatomical RV volume includes aRV; functional RV volume excludes aRV). Most adults have pre-op EDV 150–250mL/m².
*   **CT:** Useful for surgical re-entry planning and vessel assessment for peripheral CPB.

### Arrhythmias
*   **Atrial tachycardia (AF, flutter, ectopic atrial):** 25–65%
*   **Accessory pathways (WPW):** 10–45% (multiple pathways in 6–36%)
*   **AV nodal re-entrant tachycardia (AVNRT):** 10%

### Indications for Pre-operative Electrophysiological Study
*   Evidence of pre-excitation on ECG
*   History of SVT, wide-complex tachycardia, or syncope
*   Prior to operative intervention (routine in children, usually in adults)

### Surgical Approaches to Arrhythmia Management
*   **Paroxysmal AF/flutter:** Modified right-sided maze + cavotricuspid isthmus ablation or selective biatrial maze
*   **Continuous AF:** Biatrial maze
*   **Modalities:** Cryoablation, radiofrequency ablation, or traditional cut-and-sew

### Indications for Surgical Repair
*   **Neonatal period:** Inability to wean from ventilator, severe heart failure, persistent metabolic abnormalities, persistent cyanosis, prostaglandin dependence, GOSE score 3–4 with mild cyanosis, cardiothoracic ratio >80%.
*   **After neonatal period:** Symptoms or deteriorating exercise capacity, cyanosis, paradoxical embolus, progressive RV dilatation or dysfunction.
*   Early intervention considered if successful TV repair likely with low morbidity/mortality.

### Neonatal Medical Therapy
*   Prostaglandin E₁ (for functional pulmonary atresia — discontinue if PR develops)
*   Nitric oxide (reduce PVR)
*   Milrinone
*   Endotracheal intubation and ventilation
*   Cautious inotropes (epinephrine may exacerbate arrhythmias)

### Neonatal Surgical Options
*   **Single-ventricle (Starnes procedure):** TV patch closure (fenestrated), atrial septectomy, systemic-to-PA shunt (modified BT shunt).
*   **Biventricular repair:** TV repair, fenestrated ASD closure, reduction atrioplasty.
*   Early mortality when neonatal operation required: ~25%.

### Tricuspid Valve Repair Categories
*   **Monocusp repair:** Mobilisation of anterior leaflet to coapt with ventricular septum.
    *   Danielson method — TV remains in place in RV.
    *   Carpentier method — anterior leaflet mobilised, delaminated, reattached to anterior annulus + RV plication + ring annuloplasty.
*   **Cone repair:** Delamination and recruitment of all leaflet tissue → rotation → creation of 360° leaflet tissue → reanchoring at anatomical annulus.

### Principles of Cone Repair
*   Near anatomical repair with 360° of TV leaflet tissue
*   Neo-TV hinge point at true TV annulus (AV junction)
*   Selective plication of atrialised RV (eliminate dyskinetic areas)
*   Excision of redundant right atrium

### Adjunct Manoeuvres for TV Repair
*   TV annuloplasty band or reinforced annular plication
*   Anterior leaflet augmentation (elliptical patch for height; triangular patch for circumference) — autologous pericardium preferred
*   Neo-chordae (autologous or Gore-Tex®)
*   Modified Sebening stitch (approximates anterior papillary muscle to ventricular septum)

### Relative Contraindications to TV Repair
*   Older patients (>60 years)
*   Severe RV or TV annular dilatation
*   Pulmonary hypertension (uncommon)

### Surgical Approaches Outside Neonatal Period
*   Biventricular pathway
*   1.5-ventricle pathway (addition of bidirectional cavopulmonary shunt — BCPS) for severe RV dilatation/dysfunction

### Indications for Bidirectional Cavopulmonary Shunt (BCPS)
*   **RV offloading:** decreases RV volume by 35–45%.
*   **Specific indications:** RV EDV >250mL/m², RV EF <25%, cardiothoracic ratio >65%, small/D-shaped LV, post-repair RA pressure/LA pressure >1.5:1, post-repair low cardiac output, TV stenosis with mean gradient >8–10mmHg.
*   **Contraindications:** Mean PAP >20mmHg, PVR >4 Woods units, LVEDP/LA pressure >12mmHg, significant pulmonary artery hypoplasia.

### Atrial Septal Defect Subclosure/Non-closure
*   Intermediate option to bypass RV when function poor (less unloading than BCPS).
*   **Use:** Routinely in neonate/infant; selectively in child/young adult; rarely in adult (risk of paradoxical emboli).

### Tricuspid Valve Replacement
*   **Indications:** Older patients (>60 years), pulmonary hypertension, significant LV dysfunction.
*   **Prosthesis choice:** Bioprosthesis preferred (good durability in TV position, fewer thromboembolic complications).
*   **Technical note:** Suture line on atrial side of membranous septum and AV node (marked by vein of D). Tie sutures with heart beating to identify conduction injury.

### Perioperative Care
*   **Volume:** Tachycardia (HR 100–120, atrial pacing if needed), relative hypovolaemia (RA pressure <12mmHg), post-bypass ultrafiltration for patients ≤20kg.
*   **Ventilation:** Minimise PEEP and mean airway pressure; extubate when metabolic parameters normalise.
*   **Cardiac support:** Epinephrine + milrinone early (wean to avoid tachyarrhythmias); selective pulmonary vasodilators (24–48 hours).
*   **Arrhythmia prevention**

### Postoperative Discharge Medications
*   Beta-blocker and/or ACE inhibitor
*   Sildenafil for 6–8 weeks (severe RV dysfunction or high altitude residence)
*   Amiodarone for 2–3 months (transient arrhythmias) or 3–6 months (large RV plication)
*   Warfarin for 3–6 months → aspirin (biological TV replacement)

### Long-Term Outcomes
*   **Historical:** Late survival at 10 years — 93% after repair, 86% after replacement.
*   **Cone repair (recent data):** Overall survival 98% at 6 years; freedom from reoperation 98% at 6 years.

---

## Chapter 18: Tricuspid Atresia

### Definition and Epidemiology
*   **Tricuspid atresia:** Congenital absence or abnormality of the tricuspid valve resulting in no direct communication between the right atrium and right ventricle. Most patients have a dominant left ventricle and a rudimentary right ventricle.
*   **Prevalence:** 0.04–0.05 per 1000 live births; represents **1–3% of all congenital heart disease (CHD)**.

### Embryology and Classification
*   **Muscular type (75%):** Embryological abnormality arises early in gestation; a dimple or localised fibrous thickening in the floor of the right atrium.
*   **Membranous type (25%):** Abnormality occurs later in gestation; imperforated tricuspid valve.
*   **Classification by great artery relationship:**
    *   **Normally related great arteries (70%):** PA-IVS or VSD ± pulmonary stenosis.
    *   **Transposed great arteries (30%):** PA-VSD or VSD ± pulmonary stenosis.

### Associated Anomalies
*   VSD in 90% (40% close spontaneously or decrease in size → restrictive VSD causes subpulmonary or subaortic obstruction)
*   Coarctation of aorta or interrupted aortic arch (8%)
*   Persistent left-sided SVC, juxtaposition of atrial appendages, right aortic arch, transposition of great arteries

### Pathophysiology
*   **Normally related great arteries:** Obligatory right-to-left shunt across interatrial communication → systemic desaturation. Blood reaches PA via VSD and hypoplastic RV. Subpulmonary stenosis develops in ~50% (restrictive VSD or infundibular level).
*   **Transposed great arteries:** LV connected to PA → abundant pulmonary flow, minimal cyanosis but marked LV volume overload. Pulmonary valve normal in 2/3, stenotic/atretic in 1/3. Restrictive VSD causes subaortic stenosis.

### Natural History
*   Overall survival: **70% at 1 year, 55% at 5 years, 50% at 10 years**.
*   Restrictive VSD → intense hypoxia and death unless PDA or collaterals present. 90% die by 1 year without palliation.
*   Tricuspid atresia with transposed great arteries and subaortic stenosis (restrictive VSD) is an ominous combination.

### Diagnosis
*   **Symptoms:** Cyanosis (progressive), hypoxic spells, congestive heart failure (if transposed arteries).
*   **Signs:** Cyanosis ± clubbing, single S2, systolic thrill (if pulmonary stenosis), 3/6 holosystolic VSD murmur at lower LSB, hepatomegaly.
*   **ECG:** Left axis deviation, LVH, tall right atrial P wave, biatrial P wave.
*   **CXR:** Normal or slightly increased heart size, enlarged RA, flat inferior border (absence of RV), decreased pulmonary markings (most), plethoric fields with cardiomegaly (if volume overload).
*   **Echocardiography:** Absence of right AV connection with hyperechogenic shelf at TV location (diagnostic). RA enlarged; LA may be enlarged if pulmonary flow increased. Interatrial communication always present. RV hypoplastic, LV large and hypertrophied.
*   **Catheterisation:** Limited role. RA pressure slightly > LA pressure with prominent 'A' wave. PVR assessment required before BCPS or Fontan. Balloon atrial septostomy for restrictive interatrial communication.

### Management Principles
*   **Initial treatment depends on pulmonary blood flow:**
    *   **Low pulmonary flow (cyanotic):** Prostaglandin E₁ (maintain PDA)
    *   **High pulmonary flow:** Pulmonary artery banding
    *   **Restrictive interatrial communication:** Balloon atrial septostomy
    *   **Balanced circulation:** Monitoring only

### Surgical Strategy — Fontan Circulation
*   No surgical cure; palliation with **Fontan circulation** (diverts systemic venous return directly to pulmonary arteries).
*   **Staged approach:**
    *   **Neonatal/early infancy (if inadequate flow):** Modified Blalock-Taussig shunt
    *   **Neonatal/early infancy (if excessive flow):** Pulmonary artery banding
    *   **3–6 months:** Bidirectional cavopulmonary shunt (BCPS) — SVC anastomosed to PA
    *   **2–6 years:** Completion — total cavopulmonary connection (TCPC)

### TCPC Techniques
*   **Extracardiac TCPC with fenestration:** Gore-Tex® tube (16–24mm) anastomosed end-to-end to IVC and end-to-side to RPA.
*   **Lateral tunnel TCPC:** Gore-Tex® baffle connecting IVC and SVC inside RA; SVC anastomosed to PA.

### Traditional 'Ten Commandments' (Fontan)
1.  Age >4 years
2.  No pulmonary artery distortion from prior shunt
3.  No systemic or left AV valve leak
4.  Normal venous drainage
5.  Normal ventricular function (EF ≥60%)
6.  Normal heart rhythm
7.  Normal right atrial size
8.  Low PA pressure (≤15 mmHg)
9.  Low PVR (<4 units/m²)
10. Adequate PA size (PA:aorta ratio ≥0.75)

### Current Modifications to Criteria
*   Age progressively lower (but child should be crawling for musculoskeletal contribution to venous return)
*   Pre-operative abnormalities can be addressed before Fontan
*   Pre-operative sinus rhythm and normal RA size not prerequisites (but post-op arrhythmias impair prognosis)
*   Mean PAP >15 mmHg not an absolute threshold — consider in context
*   **PVR (<4 units/m²) remains the most important criterion**
*   **PA size assessed by McGoon ratio or Nakata index** (lower values increase risk)

### Pulmonary Artery Size Assessment
*   **McGoon ratio** = (pre-branching RPA diameter + LPA diameter) / descending aorta diameter at diaphragm
    *   Risk of Fontan failure higher if ratio <1.8
*   **Nakata index** = (pre-branching RPA CSA + LPA CSA) / BSA (m²)
    *   Lower index associated with worse outcomes
*   *Limitation:* Indices do not account for vascular compliance or PA distortion.

### Advantages of Venous-to-Pulmonary Shunt
*   Desaturated (systemic venous) blood shunted to lungs → greater efficiency in increasing arterial oxygen saturation
*   Reduces volume load on the heart (arterial shunt adds ventricular volume and workload)

### Fenestration
*   Allows right-to-left shunt through defect
*   Maintains cardiac output at expense of mild desaturation
*   Prevents undue elevation of systemic venous pressure → reduces capillary leakage
*   Improves long-term outcomes: functional capacity, liver function, reduction of portal hypertension complications (varices, malabsorption, protein-losing enteropathy)

### Complications
*   **Short-term:** Low cardiac output, persistent pleural effusion, chylous leak, thrombus formation, acute liver dysfunction.
*   **Long-term:** Hepatomegaly/ascites, supraventricular arrhythmias (freedom 90% at 5 years, 85% at 10 years), progressive desaturation (venous pathway obstruction, baffle leak, pulmonary AV fistula), protein-losing enteropathy, failing systemic ventricle (requiring transplant).

### Outcomes
*   **Short-term mortality:** ~1–2% (improved due to TCPC design, shorter bypass time, staged procedures, fenestration). Higher mortality in heterotaxy syndrome and hypoplastic left heart syndrome.
*   **Long-term survival:** 85–93% at 5 years, 83–91% at 10 years.
*   Heart transplant for failing Fontan may have adverse outcomes (difficult haemodynamic assessment, complex anatomy, multiple prior procedures, unique pathology).

### Fontan Conversion
*   For failing atriopulmonary connection (progressive RA dilatation → turbulent slow flow, thrombi, supraventricular arrhythmias)
*   Converts to TCPC (usually extracardiac Gore-Tex® conduit)
*   **Critical adjuncts:** Fenestration, atrial wall reduction plasty, removal of intracavitary thrombi, arrhythmia surgery (modified Maze), possible permanent pacemaker
*   Symptomatic improvement reported; impact on survival/transplantation unclear.

---

## Chapter 19: Hypoplastic Right Heart

### Definition and Spectrum
*   **Hypoplastic right heart (HRH):** Umbrella term for congenital cardiac defects with underdevelopment of the right heart where the majority of ventricular mass for single-ventricle circulation is morphologically left ventricle.
*   **Common feature:** Functionally univentricular circulation due to severe RV hypoplasia or inability to septate into two ventricles.
*   **Lesions included:**
    *   Tricuspid atresia (TA) with RV hypoplasia
    *   Double-inlet left ventricle (DILV) or straddling tricuspid valve (sTV)
    *   Pulmonary atresia/stenosis with intact ventricular septum (PA-IVS) with severe RV hypoplasia or RV-dependent coronary circulation
    *   Severe Ebstein's anomaly (EA) with hypoplastic RV or severe TR unsuitable for repair
    *   Unbalanced AVSD to the left (uAVSD)
    *   Congenitally corrected TGA (ccTGA) with severe RV hypoplasia

### Embryology
*   Underdevelopment related to **low flow through the right heart** during 7th–9th week of gestation.
*   **TA/DILV/sTV:** Limited blood entry to foetal RV → failure of cavity development. RV development dependent on VSD.
*   **PA-IVS with intact septum:** RV hypertension → maldevelopment of epicardial coronaries → sinusoids or coronary fistulae. RV-dependent coronary circulation → decompression causes ischaemia.
*   **Isomerism/heterotaxy:** Single common AV valve with RV hypoplasia → uAVSD to the left.
*   **ccTGA:** Discordant AV and VA connections → high predilection for heart block (spontaneous or post-intervention).

### Aetiology and Associations
*   Multifactorial, largely unknown. Up to 1/3 of univentricular patients have genetic abnormalities.
*   **Specific associations:** Transposition more common in boys; Ebstein's associated with maternal lithium; maternal viral infections and ACE inhibitors.
*   **Syndromes:** VACTERL, Apert, Noonan, Rubinstein-Taybi, Holt-Oram, Down, DiGeorge (22q11).
*   **Genetic mutations:** TBX5, NKX2-5, GATA4, NOTCH1.
*   **Extracardiac malformations:** Exomphalos, gastroschisis, diaphragmatic hernia, renal dysplasia, etc.

### Diagnosis
*   **Foetal ultrasound (2nd trimester):** Assess RV apex formation, wall thickness, TV inflow/regurgitation, valve Z-scores, sinusoids, forward flow through PV and ductus.
*   **Postnatal:** Echocardiography is sufficient for most lesions; MRI/CT for volume assessment. Angiography seldom required.
*   **CXR:** Small right heart shadow with decreased vascular markings (low flow) or cardiomegaly with plethora (high flow).
*   **Catheterisation:** Confirms anatomy, RV volume/function, coronary anatomy (PA-IVS), PA size, PVR, MAPCAs.

### Z-Score Classification (Table 1)
| TV Z-score | RV composition | Likely destination |
|------------|----------------|-------------------|
| > –2 | Tripartite | Biventricular repair |
| –2 to –5 | Tripartite ± bipartite | 1½-ventricle repair |
| < –5 | Bipartite | Univentricular (Fontan) |

### Symptoms
*   **Low pulmonary blood flow:** Cyanosis (deoxygenated Hb >5g/dL), clubbing with chronic hypoxia.
*   **High pulmonary blood flow:** Dyspnoea, tachypnoea, recurrent RTIs.
*   Some patients asymptomatic with incidental murmur or arrhythmia.

### Specific Lesions

**Double-Inlet Left Ventricle (DILV) and Straddling TV (sTV)**
*   Atrioventricular malalignment → valves shared between ventricles, overriding the septum.
*   Both AV valves open into LV (DILV) or predominantly into LV (sTV). Hypoplastic RV gives origin to aorta (if VA discordance).
*   Blood to aorta must pass through VSD → **bulboventricular foramen (BVF)**. Restrictive BVF → systemic outflow obstruction.
*   Treatment: Damus-Kaye-Stansel anastomosis + aortopulmonary shunt (Norwood-type) for restrictive BVF.

**Tricuspid Atresia (TA)**
*   Complete absence of right AV valve, unrestrictive ASD, variable RV hypoplasia.
*   Often associated with VA discordance, VSD, subpulmonary stenosis.
*   Definitive palliation: Staged Fontan (BCPS → TCPC). Rarely, if adequate RV cavity, atrioventricular valved conduit for biventricular repair.

**Ebstein's Anomaly (EA)**
*   Dysplasia of TV with apical displacement of posterior/septal leaflets → atrialised RV.
*   Redundant 'sail-like' anterior leaflet attached normally or rotated.
*   Associated with ASD and accessory AV conduction pathways (WPW).
*   Severe neonatal form with hypoplastic RV or severe TR → single-ventricle palliation.

**Pulmonary Atresia with Intact Ventricular Septum (PA-IVS)**
*   Severe PV stenosis/atresia, intact VSD, variable RV hypoplasia. Pulmonary arteries normal size. Lung blood supply solely via ductus (PDA-dependent).
*   RV hypertension → RV-dependent coronary circulation (widespread fistulae with proximal coronary stenosis) in ~1/3. Decompression of RV causes ischaemia.
*   TV dysplasia mirrors RV underdevelopment. Z-score <-3 or RV-dependent coronaries → univentricular strategy.

**Congenitally Corrected TGA (ccTGA) with Pulmonary Stenosis**
*   Discordant AV and VA connections. Frequently associated with PS, VSD, RV hypoplasia.
*   Heart block very common (spontaneous or post-intervention).
*   Severe desaturation as neonate → aortopulmonary shunt. Some achieve biventricular or 1½-ventricle repair.

**Unbalanced AVSD to the Left (uAVSD)**
*   ~10% of endocardial cushion defects. Common AV valve sits over one ventricle more than the other → contralateral ventricular hypoplasia.
*   Minority unbalanced to the left → hypoplastic RV. Pulmonary outflow obstruction from mild stenosis to atresia.
*   Often part of isomerism/heterotaxy with abnormal systemic/pulmonary venous drainage.

### Principles of Palliation
*   **Neonatal management:** Ensure adequate but not excessive pulmonary blood flow. Prostaglandin if ductal-dependent. Interventions: BAS, ductal stenting, balloon valvuloplasty, atrial septectomy, PA banding, aortopulmonary shunt, Damus-Kaye-Stansel.
*   **Staged Fontan pathway:**
    *   **Stage 1 (neonatal):** Balance circulation (shunt or band)
