    *   **Stage 2 (4–6 months):** Superior cavopulmonary connection (SCPC) — BDG or hemi-Fontan
    *   **Stage 3 (2–6 years):** Total cavopulmonary connection (TCPC) — extracardiac conduit or lateral tunnel

### Surgical Options
*   **Modified Blalock-Taussig (mBT) shunt:** Synthetic conduit from subclavian to PA. Easy construction/takedown; size limited by subclavian artery.
*   **PA banding:** ePTFE or nylon tape around mid-PA. Trusler's rule: 20 mm + 1 mm/kg. Target distal PA pressure with O₂ sat 80–85%.
*   **Bidirectional Glenn (BDG):** SVC disconnected from RA, end-to-side anastomosis to PA.
*   **Hemi-Fontan (HF):** SVC-RA junction incised and laid onto RPA with patch closure of junction.
*   **Extracardiac conduit (ECC):** IVC disconnected from RA, tube graft to underside of PA. Can be performed on warm beating heart.
*   **Lateral tunnel (LT):** IVC baffled within RA to PA.

### Medical Management of Single-Ventricle Circulation
*   Thromboprophylaxis (antiplatelet or anticoagulation)
*   Dietary modification or steroids for protein-losing enteropathy
*   Childhood and seasonal flu vaccines

### Complications
*   **Death:** From staged operations or inter-stage (neurological injury, myocardial dysfunction, sepsis). Earlier SCPC reduces inter-stage mortality.
*   **Arrhythmias:** Transient (respond to medication/pacing) or permanent heart block (requires pacemaker).
*   **Long-term:** Atrial arrhythmias (almost inevitable — sinus node dysfunction, heart block, junctional/atrial ectopic/re-entrant tachycardia), myocardial dysfunction (VAD or transplant).

### Structures at Risk During Surgery
*   **mBT shunt:** Recurrent laryngeal nerve, thoracic duct, phrenic nerve; innominate/subclavian/PA narrowing.
*   **PA banding:** PV incompetence (if too proximal); branch PA impingement (if too distal, especially right).
*   **Glenn/hemi-Fontan:** Right phrenic nerve, SA node/artery (transient or permanent junctional rhythm).
*   **Fontan:** Right phrenic nerve (during caval dissection).

### Outcomes
*   **Untreated:** Ductal-dependent circulations → death after ductal closure; pulmonary over-circulation → PVD and death; worsening cyanosis → mortality.
*   **Long-term survival ('perfect Fontan'):** 86% at 5 years, 74% at 10 years. Many survive into third decade.
*   **Determinants of outcome:** LV function, AV valve function, maintenance of sinus rhythm. Morphological RV as systemic ventricle not a risk factor.

---

## Chapter 20: Right Ventricular Outflow Tract

### Embryology
*   **Primitive heart tube:** Contains sinus venosus, primitive atrium, primitive ventricle, **bulbus cordis** and **truncus arteriosus**.
*   **Bulbus cordis and truncus arteriosus** give rise to left and right ventricular outflow tracts, pulmonary trunk, and aorta.
*   **5th week:** Bulbar ridges develop from mesenchymal cell proliferation (including neural crest migration).
*   **6th week:** Ridges fuse → spiral-shaped **aorticopulmonary septum** divides bulbus cordis and truncus arteriosus into aorta and pulmonary trunk. Spiralling causes pulmonary trunk to twist around aorta.
*   **Semilunar valves:** Develop from swellings of subendocardial tissue around aortic and pulmonary orifices; swellings hollow out to form thin-walled cusps.

### Pulmonary Stenosis with Intact Ventricular Septum (PS-IVS)
*   Rare congenital spectrum resulting in isolated narrowing of pulmonary valve and/or RVOT.
*   **Extreme form:** Pulmonary atresia with IVS (PA-IVS) — associated with hypoplastic right heart (HRH).
*   **Anatomical features:**
    *   **Pulmonary valve:** Raphe between leaflets, leaflet thickening/dysplasia, fused leaflets (sleeve-like or single orifice). Reduced annulus size typical in Noonan syndrome.
    *   **Pulmonary artery:** Post-stenotic dilatation beyond infancy.
    *   **RV:** Progressive hypertrophy; hypoplastic (spectrum: tripartite → absent trabecular part → inlet only).
    *   **Interatrial septum:** ASD always present; restrictive in 5–10%.
    *   **Coronary arteries:** Sinusoids (fistulae) between RV cavity and coronary arteries in PA-IVS. RV hypertension → fistulae perfused in systole, under-perfused in diastole → steal phenomenon.
    *   **Coronary circulation dependency:** RV pressure-dependent or entirely dependent if proximal discontinuity or peripheral stenoses. Decompression of RV → ischaemia/infarction.
    *   **Left ventricle:** Cavity size may be reduced.

### Classification of Pulmonary Stenosis (Table 1)
| Severity | RVOT gradient (mmHg) | RV:systemic pressure ratio | Indexed TV diameter (mm/cm²) | Indexed area (cm²/cm²) |
|----------|---------------------|----------------------------|------------------------------|------------------------|
| Mild | <40 | <0.5 | 8–10 | 0.75–1.0 |
| Moderate | 40–80 | 0.5–1 | 3–10 | 0.3–1.0 |
| Severe | >80 | >1 | <3 | <0.3 |

### Aetiology and Associations
*   **Genetic:** Noonan syndrome, Williams syndrome.
*   **Non-genetic:** Congenital rubella syndrome.
*   **Associated lesions:** PFO, VSD, TGA, DORV, Ebstein's anomaly.
*   **Epidemiology:** Isolated PS = 5.8% of all congenital defects; ~50% of malformed hearts include PS as a component.

### Pathophysiology
*   **Critical PS/PA:** Pulmonary circulation entirely ductus-dependent. Complete mixing at atrial level → desaturation, cyanosis.
*   **Sinusoids present:** Coronary circulation RV-dependent with variable myocardial ischaemia.
*   **TV Z-score ≤ –3:** Diminutive RV cavity → circulation comparable to tricuspid atresia.
*   **TV Z-score ≥0:** TR and large RV cavity.
*   **Atrial/ventricular shunting with right-to-left:** Cyanosis present; Qp:Qs <1.
*   **No shunting:** Normal arterial saturation; Qp:Qs =1.
*   **Moderate-severe PS (forward flow allowed):** Less critical; RV hypertensive with reduced compliance → increased RA pressure, right-to-left atrial shunting, variable cyanosis. Sinusoids rare.

### Natural History
*   Survival into adulthood: 40–50%. Survival beyond 7th decade reported.
*   Most common cause of death: Heart failure (~30%).
*   Untreated PA-IVS: Potentially lethal (myocardial ischaemia or severe cyanosis). Sudden death common.

### Symptoms and Signs
*   **Neonates with severe PS/PA:** Life-threatening cyanosis → requires PGE₁.
*   **Mild-moderate stenosis:** Poor feeding, slow weight gain, dyspnoea, reduced exercise tolerance.
*   **Murmur:** 2nd left intercostal space with thrill (does not radiate to carotids). Cyanosis on exertion.

### Diagnostic Features
*   **Physical examination (Table 2):** RV hypertrophy signs, systolic ejection click (unless severe), ejection systolic murmur + thrill.
*   **ECG (Table 3):** RV hypertrophy (right axis deviation, tall R in V1, right atrial enlargement in severe PS).
*   **CXR:** Depends on severity and age. Large cardiac shadow, prominent RA, RV enlargement (if not hypoplastic), mild post-stenotic dilatation, oligaemia when ductus constricts.
*   **Echocardiography (gold standard):** Level of stenosis, valve anatomy, RVOT anatomy, Doppler flow velocity (peak/mean gradient), pulmonary trunk/branch anatomy, Z-scores (PV, TV, RV), sinusoids presence/direction.
*   **Catheterisation:** Not routine unless discrepancy. Peak-to-peak gradient from RV to MPA on pull-back. Assess pulmonary vascular bed, RV sinusoids (RV injection), Qp:Qs and cardiac output (oxygen saturations).

### Therapeutic Options by Lesion Type
*   **PS-IVS:** Relieve RVOT obstruction (promote RV cavity growth); decompress RV (even if small).
*   **PA-IVS:** Avoid RV decompression (especially if RV-dependent coronary circulation); univentricular or 1½-ventricle repair.

### Decision-Making: Univentricular vs Biventricular Repair
*   **Mild RV hypoplasia:** TV and RV cavity ≥2/3 normal size; well-developed RVOT. TV Z-score: –1.5 to 0.
*   **Moderate RV hypoplasia:** TV and RV cavity ~1/2 normal size (1/3 to 2/3); outflow tract allows effective valvotomy. TV Z-score: –3 to –1.5.
*   **Severe RV hypoplasia:** TV and RV cavity ≤1/3 normal size; outflow tract severely hypoplastic/obliterated. TV Z-score: –4 to –6.

### Medical Treatment for Severe Cyanosis
*   Neutral thermal environment (minimise O₂ consumption)
*   Oxygen (possibly intubation)
*   **PGE₁** if duct-dependent circulation suspected
*   Catecholamines (dopamine, dobutamine) if needed

### Therapeutic Options — Severe PS with Well-Developed RV, Mild/No TR
*   Surgical pulmonary valvotomy (via pulmonary artery)
*   Balloon angioplasty (severe PV stenosis)
*   Laser/radiofrequency (membranous atresia) → create opening → balloon dilation to 6–8mm. If hypoxaemia persists, stent ductus arteriosus.
*   Modified Blalock-Taussig (BT) shunt if obstruction not adequately relieved.

### Therapeutic Options — PA with Hypoplastic RV and Tricuspid Stenosis
*   RV volume <35–40% unlikely to sustain cardiac output (but estimates unreliable).
*   **Inadequate criteria:** TV diameter <8mm (neonate), TV:MV diameter ratio <0.7, TV Z-score ≤ –3.
*   **Ventricular morphology:**
    *   Tripartite + PA connected to RV → open RVOT.
    *   Monopartite (inlet only), wide separation between cavity and PA → systemic-to-PA shunt + atrial septostomy (before BT shunt if fossa bulging into LA).
    *   Borderline + PA connected to ventricular mass → attempt RVOT opening with limited interatrial communication.

### Therapeutic Options — PA with Hypoplastic RV and Coronary Sinusoids
*   Very high mortality.
*   **Options:** Staged palliation to single-ventricle, heart transplant. Rare reports of sinusoid ligation/occlusion (if antegrade coronary perfusion demonstrated).
*   **Neonatal:** Systemic-to-PA shunt or prolonged PGE₁ (anaesthesia induction can be fatal due to uncontrolled ischaemia).
*   **3–4 months:** Cavopulmonary anastomosis.
*   **If TR and TV cannot be repaired:** RV exclusion (patch closure of TV + tube graft from aorta to RV cavity to maintain coronary perfusion via sinusoids).
*   LV failure risk increases with age; few survive beyond 3–4 years.

### Therapeutic Options — Older Infants and Children with PS
*   **Indications for balloon valvuloplasty:** RV-PA resting gradient >80 mmHg (infants) or >50 mmHg (children >1 year) with cyanosis due to right-to-left atrial shunting.
*   Rationale: Prevent RV infundibular hypertrophy and myocardial fibrosis.

### Indications for Transcatheter Balloon Angioplasty
*   Transpulmonary peak velocity >4 m/s (peak instantaneous gradient >60 mmHg)
*   Peak instantaneous gradient >50 mmHg (normal cardiac output)
*   Neonates with moderate-severe stenosis (especially duct-dependent circulation) — urgent
*   Adults with moderate-severe stenosis, exercise intolerance, pressure overload, risk of RV fibrosis

### Principles of Transcatheter Balloon Angioplasty
*   GA or IV sedation + local anaesthesia
*   Femoral venous access → curved end-hole or balloon-tipped catheter into RV
*   Balloon size: exceed PV diameter by 20–25%. Length: 20 mm (neonates/infants), 30 mm (children), 40 mm (adolescents/adults)
*   Inflate until annular indentation eliminated (further pressure not beneficial)
*   Post-procedure: record RV pressure and gradient

### Complications of Balloon Angioplasty
*   Arrhythmia 1%, bleeding 0.9%, femoral vein thrombosis 0.6%, hypoxia 0.4%, death 0.2%, TR 0.2%, femoral vein tears 0.2%, arterial thrombosis 0.2%, cardiac perforation 0.1%, respiratory arrest 0.1%

### Surgical Principles — Pulmonary Valvotomy
*   CPB, mild hypothermia, cardioplegic arrest
*   Vertical incision in proximal MPA into anterior sinus of Valsalva
*   Incise fused commissures, detach cusps from lateral attachments
*   Infundibular resection (if needed) through PV or RA
*   Excise hypertrophied muscular bands (parietal and septal extensions of infundibular septum)
*   **Risks:** Septal branches of LAD, medial papillary muscle of TV
*   Close PA directly or with enlargement patch
*   Close ASD
*   **Target:** RV/LV pressure ratio <0.75, RV-PA gradient <50 mmHg

### Surgical Principles — Transannular Patch
*   **Indications:** PV Z-score < –3, highly dysplastic/bulky valve requiring valvectomy
*   CPB, mild hypothermia, cardioplegic arrest
*   Open valve annulus, debulk RVOT, augment anterior surface of RVOT-PV-MPA complex with patch
*   Avoid LAD (emerges lateral to incision)

### Surgical Principles — Monocusp Valve
*   Creates competent, non-stenotic pathway → physiological RV mechanics
*   Incision over anterior RVOT, PV, MPA complex
*   Tailor monocusp from autologous pericardium, decellularised porcine pericardium, or other matrix (width ≈2× hypoplastic PV diameter)
*   Attach cusp with continuous 6-0 polypropylene suture
*   RVOT patch/hood from Gore-Tex® or bovine pericardium (continuous 5-0 polypropylene)

### Initial Surgical Management (Table 4)
*   **Mild PS (TV Z-score –1.5 to 0):** Valvotomy ± BT shunt (if cyanosis), RVOT patch (if subvalvular PS), PFO left open.
*   **Moderate-severe PS (TV Z-score –4 to –1.5):** RVOT patch, BT shunt, PFO left open.
*   **Critical PS/PA (TV Z-score –6 to –4 ± sinusoids):** BT shunt, atrial septostomy.

### Definitive Surgical Treatment (Table 5)
*   **Mild PS:** Close ASD, enlarge RV and RVOT ± transannular patch or pulmonary homograft; take down BT shunt.
*   **Moderate PS:** Close ASD, modified bidirectional cavopulmonary shunt (Glenn), enlarge RV and RVOT ± transannular patch or pulmonary homograft; take down BT shunt.
*   **Severe PS:** Fontan circulation; RA-to-RV or RA-to-PA communication; take down BT shunt.

### Pulmonary Valved Conduits for RVOT Reconstruction

**Homografts (pulmonary or aortic)**
*   Advantages: Wide size range, excellent handling, can anastomose bifurcating grafts directly to branch PAs
*   Disadvantages: Limited small sizes (neonates), limited shelf life (~2 years), high cost, immunological response (calcification, especially second homograft)

**Xenografts**
*   **Medtronic Freestyle™ (porcine aortic root, 19–29mm):** Excellent short-term durability; not suitable for neonates; handling difficult.
*   **Medtronic Contegra™ (bovine jugular vein, 12–22mm):** Freedom from reintervention 90% at 3 years, 66% at 7 years. Young age at implantation = risk factor for reintervention and distal conduit stenosis. Graft dilatation and decreased durability with elevated RV pressures or PH.

**Valved Conduits (bioprosthetic valves + synthetic conduits)**
*   Hancock® (porcine aortic valve in woven fabric conduit)
*   Carpentier-Edwards™ (porcine aortic bioprosthesis in woven polyester conduit)
*   BioIntegral No-React® BioPulmonic™ (porcine valve in porcine pericardium tube, No-React® treatment reduces calcific degeneration)
*   Advantages: Long shelf life, wide size range, rigid/semi-rigid annulus
*   Disadvantages: Size limitations (not for neonates/small children); not amenable to catheter-based dilatation

### Pulmonary Valve Replacement (PVR) Prostheses

**Stented Bioprostheses** (Carpentier-Edwards Perimount™, Magna Ease™, St Jude Trifecta™, Medtronic Mosaic™, Hancock™ II)
*   Advantages: Easy to implant, no lifelong anticoagulation, catheter interventions/reimplantation possible
*   Disadvantage: Degeneration by calcification (freedom from reintervention ~94% at 5 years, reduced in younger patients)

**Stentless Bioprostheses** (Edwards Prima Plus™, Medtronic Freestyle™, St Jude Toronto SVP®, LivaNova Pericarbon Freedom™)
*   Advantages: Excellent flow dynamics, no lifelong anticoagulation, catheter interventions possible
*   Disadvantages: Difficult handling, risk of external compression, reduced durability in younger patients

**Mechanical Valves** (St Jude Regent™, LivaNova Carbomedics®, On-X®, Medtronic Open Pivot™)
*   Rarely used in pulmonary position
*   Indications: Need for anticoagulation (e.g., left-sided mechanical valve), reduce reinterventions
*   Recent data: Freedom from reintervention 85% at 10 years (vs 80% reoperation rate for bioprosthesis at 10 years)
*   Advantages: Long-term durability, wide size range (smallest 16mm)
*   Disadvantages: Thromboembolism risk (low pressure, slow flow), lifelong anticoagulation, inefficient leaflet closure (low pulmonary diastolic pressure), no catheter interventions

### Newer Techniques

**Percutaneous Pulmonary Valve Implantation (PPVI)**
*   Non-surgical option for RVOT and pulmonary trunk dysfunction
*   Feasible, safe, effective for conduit stenosis and regurgitation; reduces number of lifetime operations
*   **Devices:** Medtronic Melody® (bovine jugular vein valve in CP stent), Edwards SAPIEN XT™ (bovine pericardial leaflets in cobalt stent)
*   **Technique:** Femoral venous access → delivery system to RVOT → identify landing zone (proximal to bifurcation, distal to muscular RVOT) → uncover valve → inflate inner then outer balloon → implant valve stent assembly
*   **Advantages:** Treats both stenosis and regurgitation, good availability (small valves for children), fully percutaneous, good stented valve performance
*   **Disadvantages:** Size limitation (dilated/hyperdynamic RVOT relative contraindication — SAPIEN XT™ available in larger sizes than Melody®), stent fractures, coronary artery compression, conduit/valve calcification (relative contraindication), migration/displacement

**Injectable Pulmonary Valve Implantation**
*   BioIntegral No-React® Injectable BioPulmonic Valve™ (porcine pulmonary valve in self-expandable stent covered by No-React®-treated pericardium; sizes 15–31mm)
*   Insertion in RVOT on beating heart without CPB (reduces damage to dilated, malfunctioning RV); requires full sternotomy (minimally invasive techniques used)
*   **Technique:** Median sternotomy → expose MPA, bifurcation, RVOT → purse-string sutures on distal RVOT → delivery trocar into MPA → inject valve into RVOT under digital control → secure with transmural sutures → assess with intra-op TOE
*   **Advantages:** Not limited by small catheter/vascular access, off-the-shelf availability, wide size range, less invasive than traditional PVR, good stented valve performance, fast recovery, easy reproducible technique, consistent RV reverse remodelling, can perform additional RVOT/PA procedures (reduction-plasty, branch PA enlargement) simultaneously
*   **Disadvantages:** Calcified RVOT may preclude implantation; no long-term follow-up data

---

## Chapter 21: Tetralogy of Fallot

### Definition and Anatomy
*   **Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF):** The most common cyanotic congenital heart disease. A conotruncal lesion with four recurrent anatomical features:
    *   Aortic override
    *   Ventricular septal defect (VSD)
    *   Right ventricular outflow tract (RVOT) obstruction
    *   Right ventricular hypertrophy (RVH)
*   All features attributable to **anterocephalad deviation of the infundibular septum** coupled with hypertrophy of the septoparietal trabeculations.

### Embryology
*   Develops during conotruncal development (5th–6th week).
*   Anterior deviation of infundibular septum → prevents alignment between primitive ventricular septum and infundibular septum → interventricular communication + aortic override.
*   Deviated infundibular septum + hypertrophied septoparietal trabeculations → RVOT obstruction.
*   RVH is a haemodynamic consequence of RVOT obstruction.

### Epidemiology and Aetiology
*   Prevalence: **0.21–0.36 per 1000 live births**.
*   Accounts for **7–10% of all congenital heart diseases**.
*   Mostly sporadic; familial cases reported (missense mutation in JAG1).
*   **22q11 deletion** seen in **12–20%** of patients.

### Phenotypic Spectrum
*   Wide spectrum depending on degree of infundibular septal deviation and RVOT obstruction.
*   Most patients have moderate to severe RVOT stenosis.
*   **Mild/no stenosis:** Eisenmenger defect.
*   **Extreme:** TOF with pulmonary atresia (TOF-PA).
*   Vast majority have **elongated RVOT** (funnel-like structure compounding stenosis).
*   Anatomical variations: VSD type, aortic-tricuspid relation, aortic-pulmonary relation, coronary artery anatomy.

### Associated Anomalies
*   **Pentalogy of Fallot:** PFO or ASD (most common).
*   Additional muscular VSDs, straddling tricuspid valve, AVSD-TOF, DORV-TOF, TOF-PA, right aortic arch (up to 15%).
*   **Coronary anomalies** (not uncommon): anomalous LAD from RCA (2–3%), accessory LAD from RCA (5–6%), single coronary artery.
*   Pulmonary artery branch anomalies (intrapericardial course variations).

### Pathophysiology Determinants
*   Degree of RVOT obstruction (fixed vs dynamic)
*   Size and position of VSD
*   Degree of RV hypertrophy
*   Associated lesions

**Fixed obstruction:** Independent of physiological/metabolic status (anatomical factors: pulmonary/suprapulmonary stenosis).
**Dynamic obstruction:** Varies with hypoxia, hydration, sympathetic activation (RVOT muscle contraction).

### Pathophysiology Scenarios by RVOT Obstruction Degree

| Degree | Shunt | Physiology | Presentation |
|--------|-------|------------|--------------|
| Minimal/mild | Large left-to-right | Equal RV and LV pressure; volume/pressure overload of lungs ("over-circulation") | "Pink tetralogy", CHF, failure to thrive |
| Moderate | Bidirectional | Well-balanced; right-to-left due to increased RVOT resistance | Mild cyanosis, mild symptoms, infrequent spells |
| Severe | Predominantly right-to-left | Critically reduced pulmonary flow; systemic flow normal until hypoxia causes bradycardia | Cyanosis, cyanotic spells, cardiorespiratory collapse |
| Extreme/PA | Duct-dependent | No antegrade pulmonary flow; saturations depend on ductus | Prostaglandin-dependent; critical at day 5–7 |

### Natural History (Untreated)
*   Survival: **~75% at 1 year, ~30% at 10 years**.
*   Major causes of death: hypoxic spells (62%), cerebrovascular events (30%).

### Symptoms
*   **Neonates (severe RVOT obstruction):** Significant cyanosis at birth (duct-dependent).
*   **Infants (mild/minimal obstruction):** CHF symptoms (poor feeding, poor weight gain, tachypnoea) — no cyanosis.
*   **Older patients:** Finger clubbing (longstanding cyanosis).
*   **Cyanotic spells:** Common in unrepaired patients >6 months; triggered by dehydration, fever, tachycardia, decreased SVR.

### Physical Signs
*   Cyanosis + systolic ejection murmur along left upper sternal border (moderate-severe TOF).
*   Second heart sound: typically **single**, normal intensity.
*   Continuous murmur if PDA patent.
*   Increased parasternal RV impulse ± thrill.
*   Growth usually normal (delayed if syndromic).
*   Minimal/mild obstruction: CHF signs (tachypnoea, hepatomegaly, poor weight gain).

### ECG
*   Rightward QRS axis
*   Various degrees of RV hypertrophy

### Chest Radiograph
*   Moderate-severe TOF: decreased pulmonary vascular markings, upturned apex, pulmonary concavity → **"boot-shaped heart"** (Figure 8).
*   Right aortic arch in up to 15%.
*   Acyanotic infants: normal or increased pulmonary blood flow (cardiomegaly, increased markings, congestion).

### Echocardiography (Gold Standard)
*   Complete segmental analysis required.
*   **Infundibular septal deviation:** Subcostal right anterior oblique and short-axis views (Figure 9).
*   **RVOT obstruction:** Nature and degree (subvalvular/infundibular, valvular, supravalvular); colour flow and pulse Doppler (Figure 10).
*   **Pulmonary artery anatomy:** Parasternal short-axis view (origin of LPA — acute angle) (Figure 11).
*   **VSD:** Type, position, shunt direction/degree (Figures 12, 13).
*   **Ductus arteriosus:** Patency (Figure 14).
*   **Coronary anomalies:** Parasternal short-axis view — anomalous LAD from RCA crossing RVOT (crucial for surgical planning) (Figure 15).
*   **MAPCAs:** May be visualised if large; require angiography/CT for delineation.

### Cardiac Catheterisation
*   Limited role for diagnosis (echocardiography sufficient).
*   RV injection confirms: aortic override degree, RVOT obstruction (Figure 16), VSD type/size (Figure 17), PA size/stenosis, RVH.
*   **Useful for:** MAPCA delineation, pre-operative and post-operative catheter-based interventions.

### Management Principles
*   **Surgical repair is the only definitive therapy.**
*   Medical treatment stabilises until surgery.
*   **PGE₁:** Neonates with duct-dependent pulmonary circulation (overdosing → apnoea, hypoxia).
*   **Beta-blockers (propranolol):** Older infants with progressive RVOT obstruction and cyanotic spells (oral or IV).
*   **Diuretics (furosemide, spironolactone):** Minimal RVOT obstruction with pulmonary over-circulation.

### Indications for Surgical Repair
*   Presence of TOF is an **absolute indication**.
*   Aim: definitive repair — close ASD/VSD, correct RVOT obstruction at all levels, re-establish biventricular circulation.

### Contraindications to Primary Repair
*   No absolute contraindications, but precluding conditions include:
    *   Extreme prematurity, low body weight (<2.5–2.8 kg)
    *   Intracranial haemorrhage
    *   Active systemic infection
    *   Extremely small branch PAs or MAPCAs (may need staged operations)

### Surgical Principles

**Historical evolution:**
1.  Initial: BTS (Blalock-Taussig shunt) for pulmonary blood flow.
2.  Then: Staged repair (BTS → repair at 1 year) with transannular patch (creating severe PR).
3.  Current: **Transatrial/transpulmonary approach** (minimises muscle injury, preserves pulmonary valve when possible — mild residual PS better than free PR long-term) (Figure 18).

**Current paradigm shift:**
*   From staged repair to **primary definitive repair** at any age without contraindication.
*   Initial palliation (BTS) now infrequent.

**Valve management:**
*   **Valve preservation:** Attempted even with moderate stenosis (Figure 19D).
*   **Transannular patch (TAP):** When valve cannot be preserved (severe PS/near atresia) (Figure 19C).
*   **Monocusp valve:** Mitigates free PR when TAP required (Figure 20).
*   Patients with TAP ± monocusp will require later pulmonary valve implantation.

**Ongoing controversies:**
*   Primary vs staged repair
*   Catheter-based palliation (RVOT/ductal stent) vs BTS
*   Transatrial transpulmonary vs transventricular approach
*   Indications, timing, modality of pulmonary valve implantation

### Treatment Algorithm (Figure 21)
*   **Asymptomatic:** Elective repair at **5–6 months**.
*   **Earlier repair:** O₂ saturations <80% or cyanotic spell.
*   **Neonates:** Primary repair unless contraindications (weight <2.5–2.8 kg, prematurity <36 weeks, pathological biventricular hypertrophy, intracranial haemorrhage, sepsis, significant end-organ dysfunction).
*   **If primary repair contraindicated:** Balloon dilatation or RVOT stent (if feasible).
*   **Post-stent patients:** Elective repair at **3–6 months** (or earlier if saturations <80%).
*   **Policy:** Avoid BTS in neonatal/early infantile period.

### Interventional Options

**RVOT stent placement (Figure 22):**
*   Excellent palliative option for critical cyanotic neonates/small infants not suitable for primary repair.
*   Alternative to BTS (Figure 23).

**Balloon dilatation:**
*   For isolated pulmonary valvular stenosis (temporary relief).

**PDA stent:**
*   Option to increase pulmonary blood flow if RVOT stenting insufficient.

**Percutaneous pulmonary valve implantation (PPVI):**
*   For patients with TAP requiring later valve implantation.
*   Transvenous approach via femoral vein.
*   **Contraindications:** Annulus/RVOT too wide, highly pulsatile RVOT, calcified narrow conduit, associated lesions requiring surgery (significant PA stenosis, TR, residual VSD).

### Complications of Surgical Repair
*   **Peri-operative mortality:** 0–1% (acyanotic), 1–3% (symptomatic neonates/infants).
*   **Complete heart block:** ~1% (requires permanent pacemaker).
*   **Low cardiac output state:** Up to 1/3 of patients (due to restrictive RV physiology).
*   **Residual lesions:** RVOT obstruction (Figure 24), pulmonary artery stenosis (Figure 25), tricuspid regurgitation — may require reintervention.

### Long-Term Outcomes
*   Survival: **~85% at 30 years**.
*   Risk of death: **~0.3% per year** (first 30 years). 40-year survival up to 90% (current era).
*   **Risk factors for late mortality:** Older age at operation, high post-repair RV/LV systolic pressure ratio.
*   **Transannular patch:** Mixed results on late survival; vast majority require later PVR.
*   **Common causes of death:** Cardiac-related sudden death, CHF, reoperation-related death.
*   **Functional status:** ~80% NYHA Class I, <10% Class III at 30-year follow-up (but exercise capacity markedly impaired).
*   **PVR risk:** Constant ~0.8% per year for 30 years.
*   **Reoperation (non-PVR):** 15–20% (residual ASD/VSD, TR, residual RVOT obstruction/aneurysm, branch PA stenosis) (Figure 26).

---

## Chapter 22: Pulmonary Atresia with Ventricular Septal Defect

### Definition and Epidemiology
*   **Pulmonary atresia with ventricular septal defect (PA-VSD):** A complex, cyanotic, highly heterogeneous malformation characterised by underdevelopment of the RVOT (subpulmonary infundibulum) with atresia of the pulmonary valve, a large VSD, and RV hypertrophy (Figure 1).
*   **Incidence:** ~2.5% of all congenital heart defects; ~10 per 100,000 live births. Slightly more prevalent in males.
*   **Distinction from PA-IVS:** In PA-VSD, pulmonary atresia occurs early (after conotruncal partitioning but before VSD closure); RV and TV are well-developed. In PA-IVS, atresia occurs later (after cardiac septation); RV and TV are hypoplastic with possible coronary sinusoids.

### Classification (by source of pulmonary blood flow)
*   **Type A:** Intrapericardial pulmonary arteries present; MAPCAs absent; pulmonary blood flow via patent ductus arteriosus (duct-dependent).
*   **Type B:** Both intrapericardial pulmonary arteries and MAPCAs present; flow from both ductus and MAPCAs.
*   **Type C:** Intrapericardial pulmonary arteries absent; pulmonary blood flow supplied only by MAPCAs.
*   Further categorised by presence/absence of confluent RPA/LPA and presence/absence of main pulmonary trunk.

### Major Aortopulmonary Collateral Arteries (MAPCAs)
*   Primitive intersegmental channels with muscular media that failed to involute during development.
*   Most common origin: descending thoracic aorta at carina level (also subclavian, internal thoracic, intercostal, abdominal aorta, rarely coronary artery).
*   Tortuous course; may pass anterior or posterior to oesophagus, tracheobronchial tree, and aorta.
*   Highly variable segmental distribution; associated with abnormal peripheral arborisation.

### Associated Anomalies and Syndromes
*   **Intracardiac:** DORV, TGA, complete AVSD.
*   **Extracardiac:** In 25% of patients.
*   **Syndromes:** 22q11.2 deletion (DiGeorge, velocardiofacial), VACTERL, Alagille, trisomy 21.

### Pathophysiology
*   Large VSD → complete mixing, cyanosis, equalisation of RV/LV pressures, compensatory RV hypertrophy.
*   **Type A (duct-dependent):** Ductus closure → hypoxia, cardiovascular collapse.
*   **Types B/C (MAPCA-dependent):** Abnormal, precarious flow; simultaneous double supply, over-circulation, and hypoperfusion of different lung segments; progressive stenosis or pulmonary vascular occlusive disease → lung segment attrition.

### Natural History
*   Unrepaired: poor prognosis; greatest attrition in infancy; survival into adult life rare.
*   Historical surgical outcomes: survival 60% at 1 year, 40% at 10 years, 6% at 35 years.
*   Long-term survivors have balanced MAPCA flow (enough to reduce hypoxia, restricted enough to limit pulmonary hypertension) but have functional limitation, polycythaemia, prone to infection and pulmonary haemorrhage.

### Symptoms
*   ~2/3 develop symptoms during infancy.
*   **Presentation modes:** Cyanosis (50%), heart failure (25%), incidental murmur with mild cyanosis/failure to thrive (25%).
*   **Type A neonates:** Acute cyanosis, hypoxia, acidosis, shock as ductus closes.
*   **Types B/C infants:** Increasing cyanosis (MAPCA stenosis or late ductal closure) or heart failure (fall in PVR with increased flow).
*   **Children:** Incidental murmur, mild cyanosis, heart failure, failure to thrive.
*   **Adults (rare):** Ubiquitously cyanotic with dyspnoea and impaired NYHA class.

### Clinical Signs
*   **Ductal closure (acute):** Shock, marked cyanosis unresponsive to O₂, tachypnoea, tachycardia, preserved peripheral pulses.
*   **Decreased pulmonary flow:** Progressive cyanosis → clubbing, polycythaemia.
*   **Increased pulmonary flow:** Heart failure signs (respiratory distress, tachypnoea, tachycardia, hepatomegaly, bounding pulse).
*   Single loud S2 (absent pulmonary component).
*   Continuous bruit (turbulent flow through MAPCAs or ductus).

### ECG
*   Right axis deviation (differentiates from tricuspid atresia — left superior axis).
*   RV hypertrophy (tall R wave >10 mm in V1; differentiates from PA-IVS where RV is diminutive) (Figure 5).
*   Left atrial enlargement and biventricular hypertrophy (increased pulmonary venous return).

### Radiology
*   **CXR:** Boot-shaped heart (small/absent LPA → left heart border concavity; RV hypertrophy → uplifted apex) (Figure 6); right aortic arch (25–50%); pulmonary vascular markings decreased or increased (reticular pattern with MAPCAs).
*   **CT/MRI:** Emerging alternatives to catheterisation — define RVOT, MPA, branch PAs and stenoses, ductus, MAPCAs (number, location, course); functional assessment (MRI) of % flow to each lung; 3D reconstruction for operative planning (Figure 7).

### Echocardiography
*   Key for intracardiac anatomy; limited for pulmonary circulation.
*   Demonstrates: PA with absent flow between RVOT and pulmonary trunk (Figure 8); variable hypoplasia of subpulmonary infundibulum and MPA.
*   **Information provided:**
    *   VSD type (large/unrestrictive, malalignment, perimembranous with outlet extension — most common)
    *   Anterior deviation of infundibular septum ± aortic override
    *   RV hypertrophy and cavity size
    *   Interatrial communication (secundum ASD, PFO in 50%)
    *   Right aortic arch and mirror branching
    *   Coronary anatomy (usually normal; rarely anomalous LAD from RCA crossing RVOT)
    *   Intrapericardial PA anatomy (presence/absence of branching, confluent/discontinuous, size/stenoses)
    *   Colour Doppler flow direction in central PAs: antegrade (from ductus) vs retrograde (from MAPCAs)

### Cardiac Catheterisation
*   Key for delineating pulmonary circulation (Figure 9).
*   **Intrapericardial PAs:** Presence of MPA, confluence ('seagull' appearance), vessel size/side disparity, number of lung segments supplied, distal stenoses. Retrograde pulmonary vein wedge injection may be needed for hypoplastic PAs.
*   **MAPCAs (Figure 10):** Number, size, origin, lung segments supplied, proximal stenoses. Elevated distal pressure (mean >20–25 mmHg) suggests pulmonary vascular occlusive disease.
*   Also delineates coronary anatomy, PVR, transpulmonary gradient. RV pressure invariably systemic (unrestrictive VSD).

### Medical Management
*   **Duct-dependent (Type A):** PGE₁ to maintain ductal patency until surgery.
*   **Acute shock after ductal closure:** Immediate resuscitation ± ventilation.
*   **Heart failure (excessive pulmonary flow):** Diuretics and ACE inhibitors.

### Principles of Surgical Repair
*   **Primary aim:** Improve on predicted natural history.
*   **Ideal outcome:** Septated biventricular circulation with low PVR and low RV pressures.
*   **Key goals:**
    *   Increase pulmonary blood flow, O₂ saturation, encourage PA growth (systemic-PA shunt, RV-PA conduit, aortopulmonary window)
    *   Unifocalisation of MAPCAs (connect as many lung segments as possible to central PAs) (Figure 11)
    *   Secure long-term RV-to-pulmonary tree source (valved conduit, preferably oversized)
    *   Close VSD (only if low PVR guaranteed; otherwise leave open to avoid RV failure)
*   **Controversy:** Different centres pursue radically different strategies; long-term outcome data awaited.

### Surgical Options by Type

**Type A (no MAPCAs) — sternotomy:**
*   **Single-stage complete repair:** Tetralogy-type repair ± PA interventions + RV-PA valved conduit + VSD closure (REV-type repair possible).
*   **Staged repair:** Initial systemic-PA shunt → later take-down + RV-PA conduit + VSD closure.

**Types B/C (with MAPCAs) — thoracotomy + sternotomy:**
*   **Single-stage complete repair (one session):** Unifocalisation of MAPCAs, central PA augmentation/reconstruction, RV-PA valved conduit, VSD closure (some measure intra-operative PVR to decide on VSD closure).
*   **Staged repair:** Initial shunt ± unifocalisation → later RV-PA conduit ± VSD closure.

### Key Anatomical Features for Surgical Decision-Making
*   Adequacy of pulmonary vascular tree, PVR, post-operative RV pressure
*   Size of intrapericardial PAs and extent of peripheral arborisation
*   Number, origin, course, and segments supplied by each MAPCA
*   Location of stenoses within PAs and collaterals
*   Post-stenotic pressure in collaterals (predicts subsequent PVR)

### Indices for Assessing Pulmonary Artery Adequacy
*   **McGoon ratio** = (Proximal RPA diameter + Proximal LPA diameter) / Descending aortic diameter above diaphragm
    *   >1.2 adequate for complete repair; <0.8 inadequate for VSD closure (may overestimate as descending aorta often smaller in PA-VSD)
*   **Nakata index** = (Distal RPA CSA + Distal LPA CSA) / BSA
    *   >150 mm²/m² adequate for complete repair without shunt; not useful with MAPCAs (does not account for unifocalisation)
*   **Total NeoPulmonary Artery Index (TNPAI)** = (Nakata index + CSA of significant MAPCAs) / BSA
    *   >200 mm²/m² correlates with low post-operative RV pressure
*   **Intra-operative functional assessment:** After unifocalisation and RV-PA conduit, perfuse via conduit; if PA pressure <25 mmHg → expected RV/LV <0.5 → may close VSD.
*   **Surrogate marker:** Recruitment of ~15 lung segments = acceptable post-operative PVR.

### Components of Complete Repair
*   **RV-PA conduit:** De-ringed Hancock® valve conduit, Medtronic Contegra® (bovine jugular vein), or cryopreserved homograft (Figure 12).
*   **VSD closure:** Patch (double velour Dacron®) via tricuspid valve or longitudinal right ventriculotomy. Leave open if pulmonary vascular bed inadequate (allows RV decompression and time for PVR to fall).
*   **Unifocalisation of MAPCAs:** Anastomose MAPCAs and intrapericardial PAs to form confluence receiving blood from single source (RV-PA conduit). Ligate MAPCAs at origin (usually descending aorta); extensively mobilise to maximise length. Via median sternotomy, lateral thoracotomy, or both.
*   **Other components:** Ligation of ductus, take-down of previous shunt, ASD fenestration, patch augmentation of PAs.
*   **If VSD not closed:** Surgical clips or bands may prevent pulmonary overflow (Figure 13); subsequently released by balloon angioplasty.
*   **Valvular atresia only:** Transannular patch repair.

### One-Stage Complete Repair Rationale
*   Early recruitment of as many lung segments as possible to low-pressure RV-PA flow → key determinant of long-term survival and function.
*   Early unifocalisation normalises cardiovascular physiology, preserves lung parenchyma (avoids pulmonary vascular occlusive disease from unrestricted systemic flow via MAPCAs).
*   Alleviates cyanosis and polycythaemia in infancy; may improve long-term cardiac function.
*   Avoids interval death from shunt complications (thrombosis, distal anastomosis stenosis).
*   **Type A (duct-dependent):** Good candidates for early repair with RV-PA conduit; no unifocalisation needed.
*   **Types B/C:** Complete repair at 3–9 months (after surgery tolerance improved, before significant occlusive disease or MAPCA stenoses). Low O₂ saturations (<70%) indicate earlier intervention.
*   Stanford group aims for one-stage complete repair during infancy in majority of patients.

### Staged Repair Rationale
*   Evolved from early surgical experience; divides complete repair into less ambitious, better-tolerated procedures.
*   Avoids neonatal right heart repair (prolonged ICU stay, increased morbidity).
*   **Stage 1:** Palliative shunt (modified BT shunt or central Melbourne shunt if confluent PAs). If central PAs diminutive → RV-PA conduit with patch augmentation (provides catheter access for inter-stage balloon angioplasty). If no mediastinal PAs → bilateral modified BT shunts via thoracotomies + unifocalisation of significant MAPCAs.
*   **Stage 2 (3–6 months):** Ligation of MAPCAs to segments with dual supply; transplant only if sole source, free of stenosis, and not hypertensive.
*   **Stage 3 (1–3 years):** Take-down of shunt + RV-PA conduit ± VSD closure.
*   **Melbourne group:** Multi-stage approach without unifocalisation; relies on inter-stage growth of pulmonary tree + patch augmentation as required.

### Complications
*   Congestive heart failure
*   Complete heart block
*   Infective endocarditis
*   Cerebral abscess (if residual VSD)
*   Delayed growth

### Outcomes
*   **Operative mortality for complete repair:** 2–6%.
*   **Single-stage unifocalisation possible:** Up to 75% of patients.
*   **Primary complete intracardiac repair achieved:** 50% of patients (delayed VSD closure in most of remainder).
*   **Inability to achieve adequate unifocalisation** → reduced survival.
*   **Intrapericardial PA morphology** not a risk factor for survival.
*   **Multidisciplinary approach** (surgeons + interventional cardiologists) essential.
*   **Early reintervention to PAs:** ~30%. Nearly all long-term survivors with RV-PA conduit will require replacement.
*   **Long-term survival after complete repair:** Up to 85% at 15 years (vs ~55% at 15 years for shunt palliation alone, with impaired functional status).
*   **Late outcomes:** Aortic regurgitation, ventricular arrhythmias (associated with higher incidence of sudden death).

### Catheter Reintervention Role
*   Pulmonary artery balloon dilatation or stenting (including surgically inaccessible distal stenoses) (Figure 14)
*   Coil occlusion of residual MAPCAs
*   Palliative dilatation or stenting of unrepaired MAPCAs
*   Dilatation or stenting of RV-PA conduit
*   Balloon dilatation to release restrictive surgical clips or PA bands
*   Pacemaker insertion

### Surgical Reintervention Role
*   Complete repair after shunt placement (following PA growth)
*   Closure of residual VSD
*   Replacement of RV-PA conduit (stenosis or outgrown) (Figure 15)
*   Pulmonary artery augmentation
*   Recruitment of additional MAPCAs (uncommon)
*   Heart-lung transplantation

---

## Chapter 23: Transposition of the great arteries

### Definition and Anatomy

*   **Transposition of the great arteries (TGA):** A congenital anomaly with discordant ventriculoarterial connections — the aorta arises from the right ventricle and the pulmonary trunk arises from the left ventricle. Atrioventricular connections are concordant.
*   **Coronary arteries in TGA:** Variable origin patterns. The Leiden classification numbers the aortic sinuses facing the pulmonary artery as sinus 1 (rightward) and sinus 2 (leftward). Surgically significant groups: "usual" (70%), loop patterns (25%), intramural/interarterial (5%).
*   **Intramural coronary artery (5%):** Proximal course within the aortic wall, often with a slit-like ostium near a commissure. Independent risk factor for increased mortality in arterial switch operation.

### Classification and Epidemiology

*   **Incidence:** 2% of all congenital heart defects. Male:female ratio approximately 2:1.
*   **Variants of TGA:** Intact ventricular septum (TGA-IVS) — 75%; ventricular septal defect (TGA-VSD) — 15%; pulmonary/subpulmonary stenosis (TGA-PS) — 6%; coarctation of the aorta (TGA-CoA) — 4%.

### Pathophysiology and Natural History

*   **Parallel circulations:** Systemic venous return → right heart → aorta (bypassing lungs). Pulmonary venous return → left heart → pulmonary artery. Survival requires shunts (PDA, ASD, VSD).
*   **Transposition physiology:** Cyanosis despite excessive pulmonary blood flow. Postnatal fall in pulmonary vascular resistance → increased left-to-right shunt through PDA. Ductal closure (day 2–4) with restrictive ASD → profound cyanosis.
*   **Natural history:** Without treatment, >90% die before 1 year of age. In TGA-IVS by 6 weeks — left ventricular involution (thin-walled, crescentic shape). With VSD — LV pressure maintained, but pulmonary vascular disease develops by 6 months.

### Diagnosis

*   **Chest radiography:** "Egg-on-its-side" or "egg-on-a-string" appearance — narrow superior mediastinum, enlarged cardiac silhouette, plethoric lung fields.
*   **Echocardiography:** Parallel great vessels (PA from LV, aorta from RV). Assessment of: PDA, ASD, VSD, vessel relationship (anteroposterior/side-by-side), coronary arteries, ventricular function, aortic arch.
*   **Additional imaging:** Catheterization — for haemodynamic instability to exclude coronary problems. CT/MRI — gold standard for follow-up (pulmonary artery stenosis, neoaortic root dilatation, atrial switch complications).

### Management and Preoperative Stabilization

*   **Prostaglandin infusion:** Maintains PDA patency to improve mixing.
*   **Balloon atrial septostomy (Rashkind):** When saturations <75% despite PDA. Balloon catheter passed from RA to LA, inflated, and pulled back to tear the fossa ovalis. Complications: arrhythmias, cardiac perforation, balloon fragment embolization.

### Surgical Treatment

*   **Atrial switch (Senning/Mustard):** Physiologic repair — venous return redirected within atria. RV remains systemic ventricle. Complications: baffle obstruction/leak, tricuspid regurgitation, systemic RV dysfunction, sinus node dysfunction, atrial arrhythmias. Currently almost never performed (except late presentation with LV involution or challenging coronary anatomy).
*   **Arterial switch (Jatene):** Anatomic repair — aorta and pulmonary artery transected, coronaries transferred to neoaortic root. Pulmonary bifurcation translocated anterior to aorta (Lecompte maneuver). Timing: TGA-IVS — by 2 weeks of age (optimally day 5–7); delay >6 weeks risks LV involution. TGA-VSD with unrestrictive VSD — less urgent.
*   **Challenging coronary patterns:** No absolute contraindications, but increased technical difficulty with 1LR,2Cx, single coronary artery, intramural arteries, side-by-side vessel orientation.

### Complications and Outcomes

*   **Early complications:** Low cardiac output syndrome (most often due to coronary ischemia). Management: emergency re-exploration of coronary transfers, ECMO.
*   **Arterial switch outcomes:** Early survival >96% (~99% in UK for simple TGA). Risk factors: weight <2.5kg, complex anatomy. Late survival >95% at 10 years and beyond.
*   **Long-term consequences:** 10% require reintervention within 10 years: coronary lesions (5%, often silent), pulmonary artery stenoses (catheter dilation/stenting), neoaortic root dilatation with regurgitation. Neuropsychological impairments: visual-spatial and fine-motor deficits, increased psychiatric disorders (ADHD, depression, anxiety).

---

## Chapter 24: Congenitally Corrected Transposition of the Great Arteries

### Definition and Anatomy
*   **ccTGA** is a complex congenital heart defect characterized by **both atrioventricular and ventriculo-arterial discordance**. Systemic venous blood returns to the morphologic right atrium → mitral valve → morphologic left ventricle (LV) → pulmonary artery. Pulmonary venous blood returns to the morphologic left atrium → tricuspid valve → morphologic right ventricle (RV) → aorta. This results in a "corrected" and physiologically normal circulation.
*   **Segmental anatomy:** In 90% of cases, situs solitus (S,L,L); in 10%, situs inversus with dextrocardia (I,D,D).
*   **Associated anomalies** (98%): VSD (75%), pulmonary stenosis/atresia (50%), tricuspid valve abnormalities (Ebsteinoid, 50%), spontaneous heart block.

### Pathophysiology
*   The primary concern is the **morphologic right ventricle as the systemic ventricle** and the **tricuspid valve as the systemic AV valve**, which fail over time.
*   Tricuspid regurgitation (TR) is worsened by **septal shift toward the LV**. This can be reversed with pulmonary artery banding.
*   **LVOTO** (pulmonary stenosis/atresia) → early cyanosis. Large VSD or significant TR → heart failure.
*   Spontaneous **complete heart block** occurs at ~2% per year after diagnosis.

### Diagnosis
*   **Chest X-ray:** Parallel great vessels, straight left heart border, absent pulmonary knuckle.
*   **ECG:** Reversal of normal Q-wave pattern — Q waves in right precordial leads, absent in left precordial leads. Complete heart block (including neonatal period).
*   **Echocardiography** is the mainstay. Assessment includes: sequential segmental analysis, AV valve anatomy/function (especially systemic), LVOTO, VSD size/location, straddling.

### Management Principles
*   **Surgical indications:** Symptomatic patients; asymptomatic patients with declining RV function and worsening TR.
*   **Physiologic repair** (traditional): RV remains systemic. VSD closure, TV repair/replacement, LV→PA conduit for LVOTO. Survival: 77% at 5 years, 48% at 20 years. Late death from systemic ventricular failure in 50%.
*   **Anatomic repair** (contemporary): LV becomes systemic.
    *   Double-switch (atrial switch + arterial switch) — no LVOTO.
    *   Atrial switch + Rastelli — VSD + LVOTO.
    *   Early mortality ~6%, 9-year survival >90%.
*   **LV training** (PA banding) for intact septum: achieve LV pressure ≥80% systemic.

### Neonatal Palliative Interventions
*   **Modified BT shunt** — for pulmonary atresia/severe stenosis.
*   **PA banding** — for large VSD with pulmonary overcirculation.
*   **Dual-chamber pacemaker** — for congenital complete heart block with poor ventricular function.

### Late Complications After Anatomic Repair
*   RV→PA conduit dysfunction, systemic or pulmonary venous pathway obstruction, coronary obstruction/kinking, heart block, neo-aortic insufficiency, systemic LV failure (up to 25%).

---

## Chapter 25: Double-Outlet Right Ventricle

### Definition and General Concepts
- **Double-outlet right ventricle (DORV)** is a ventriculo-arterial connection in which both great arteries arise **entirely or predominantly** from the morphologic right ventricle (RV).
- The **"50% rule"** : >50% of the cross-sectional area of each great artery must be supported by the RV. The key word is **"predominantly"** .
- DORV is **not a single phenotype** but a heterogeneous group united by one type of ventriculo-arterial connection.
- **Embryology:** Persistence of the early developmental phase when both outflow tracts are supported by the developing RV.

### Associated Anomalies
- **Common:** RVOT obstruction (most frequent), ASD, coarctation/hypoplastic arch, LVOTO, AVSD (Type C Rastelli), LV hypoplasia.
- **Rare:** Discordant AV connections, straddling valves, RV hypoplasia, isomerism-associated anomalies.

### Taussig-Bing Anomaly
- DORV with **subpulmonary VSD**, side-by-side great arteries, bilateral conuses.
- Spectrum from DORV to TGA depending on degree of pulmonary trunk overriding.
- Almost always requires **arterial switch operation**.

### Anatomical Classification (by VSD position)
| Type | VSD location |
|---|---|
| Subaortic | Beneath aortic valve |
| Subpulmonary (Taussig-Bing) | Beneath pulmonary valve |
| Doubly-committed (juxta-arterial) | Fibrous continuity between aortic and pulmonary leaflets |
| Non-committed | Remote (inlet or apical), not directed to either outflow |

### Key Anatomical Modifiers
- **Outlet septum (OS):** Displaced, becomes intracavitary RV structure.
- **Septomarginal trabeculation (SMT):** Constant muscle bundle from outlet to RV apex.
- **Ventriculo-infundibular fold (VIF):** Forms muscular infundibulum below pulmonary valve.
- **Conduction tissue:** Usually runs in posteroinferior border of VSD; safe in muscular defects.

### Pathophysiology (depends on VSD position)
- **Subaortic VSD (VSD-type):** Behaves like VSD → pulmonary over-circulation. With PS → tetralogy of Fallot-type.
- **Subpulmonary VSD (TGA-type):** LV blood → PA, RV/mixed blood → aorta → cyanosis + pulmonary over-circulation.
- **Streaming:** Directed flow from LV across VSD into a specific outflow tract.

### Diagnosis
- **CXR:** Cardiomegaly + plethoric lungs (VSD-type); normal heart + oligemic lungs (TOF-type).
- **Echocardiography** — mainstay. Systematic sequential segmental analysis: atrial situs, AV valves, ventricular morphology, VSD location/size/number, 50% rule, infundibular morphology, coronary anatomy, arch anatomy.
- **CT/MRI:** Intracardiac/extracardiac anatomy, coronary anomalies, 3D printing for complex cases.

### Neonatal Palliative Interventions
- **To restrict PBF (no PS):** Pulmonary artery banding. Trusler's law: `18–21 mm + weight (kg)`. Goal: peak velocity 3–4 m/s, distal PA pressure <50% systemic.
- **To augment PBF (PS/atresia):**
  - RV→PA shunt (5–6 mm Gore-Tex®)
  - Modified BT shunt (3–4 mm)
  - Ductus arteriosus stent
  - RVOT stent

### Surgical Repair by Type
| VSD type | Repair |
|---|---|
| Subaortic / doubly-committed | Intraventricular baffle (tunnel) from LV → aorta; RVOT augmentation if PS |
| Subpulmonary (Taussig-Bing) | Arterial switch + VSD closure; if side-by-side arteries → intraventricular tunnel; if subpulmonary stenosis → Rastelli or REV |
| Non-committed | Often functionally univentricular palliation |

### Outcomes
- **Biventricular repair:** Mortality <5% (simple) to 20–25% (complex). 30-day reintervention 10–15%. 5-year survival 85–90%.
- **Univentricular palliation:** 5-year survival ~70%, 10-year post-Fontan ~80%.
- **Common reoperations at 5 years:** LVOTO, RV→PA conduit replacement, redo AVSD.

### Burden in Practice
- Only 1% of congenital defects, but disproportionately complex. High reintervention rates, ongoing burden of reoperations for LVOT and RVOT obstruction.

---

## Chapter 26: Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome

### Definition and Anatomy
- **Hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS)** is a spectrum of cardiac defects in which the **left ventricle cannot support the systemic circulation**.
- True hypoplasia (proportionally small but structurally normal left heart) is rare. HLHS typically presents with:
  - Mitral valve: stenotic, dysplastic, or atretic
  - Left ventricle: slit-like (mitral atresia) or small/globular with endocardial fibroelastosis (mitral stenosis)
  - Aortic valve: atretic, dysplastic, or stenotic
  - Ascending aorta: thread-like (atresia) to small/near-normal (stenosis)
  - Isthmus: small/tapering, often with pre-ductal coarctation shelf
- **Coronary perfusion:** Retrograde from main PA through ductus and backward along aortic arch.
- **Borderline LV:** Smaller than expected but with patent valves; may sustain circulation only at rest.

### Embryology and Epidemiology
- Intact ventricular septum suggests trigger **after 8 weeks gestation** (closure of embryonic interventricular communication) → acquired foetal disease.
- Theory: Diversion of blood away from left heart via oval foramen reduces flow → eliminates growth stimulus.
- **Incidence:** ~1 per 5,000 live births; **4th most common cardiac defect**; most common univentricular lesion.
- **Male predominance:** 2:1.

### Subclassification (3 types)
| Type | Mitral valve | Aortic valve |
|---|---|---|
| Combined atresia | Atretic | Atretic |
| Mitral stenosis + aortic atresia | Stenotic | Atretic |
| Combined stenosis | Stenotic | Stenotic |

### Associated Anomalies
- Intact/highly restrictive atrial septum (~10%) → poor prognosis unless alternative decompression
- Tricuspid valve dysplasia
- Aberrant right subclavian artery
- Family history of left-sided defects (coarctation, bicuspid aortic valve)

### Pathophysiology
- **Parallel circulations:** RV stroke volume distributed by relative PVR and SVR.
- **At birth:** Ductal closure → sudden drop in systemic perfusion → lethal without treatment.
- **Coronary steal:** Falling PVR → increased PBF at expense of systemic flow → diastolic pressure drop → compromised coronary perfusion.
- **Restrictive IAS:** Pulmonary venous obstruction → lymphatic changes, elevated PVR → profound postnatal hypoxia.

### Natural History
- Intrauterine death: up to 10%
- Untreated: **70% die within 1 week** of birth; very few survive >1 month
- Survival depends on patent ductus and elevated PVR (inevitable pulmonary vascular disease)

### Clinical Presentation
- Born normal, saturations often >90%, no murmur
- Progressive tachycardia, tachypnoea, grunting
- Weak/absent femoral pulses, cool mottled peripheries, hepatomegaly
- Metabolic acidosis, rising lactate, widening AV oxygen gap

### Diagnosis
- **Foetal echocardiography:** 4-chamber view shows only 3 chambers (hypoplastic LV)
- **Postnatal echocardiography:** Assess ductal patency, RV function, TR, PV function, IAS restriction, MV anatomy, LV size, AoV/ascending aorta, arch/coarctation, retrograde flow
- **CT:** For arch anatomy when echo incomplete (no anaesthesia needed)
- **CXR:** Plethoric lungs, enlarged cardiac silhouette; intact septum → mimics obstructed TAPVC
- **Catheterisation:** Little role, significant risk

### Treatment Options
1. **Termination of pregnancy** (gestational age-dependent)
2. **Comfort care** (no active intervention)
3. **Primary heart transplantation** (limited by donor availability)
4. **Staged surgical palliation** (Norwood sequence → Fontan circulation)

### Stage I Palliation (Norwood Procedure)
- **Damus-Kaye-Stansel procedure:** Disconnect PA trunk, connect pulmonary valve/trunk to aorta → neo-aortic trunk
- **Arch repair:** Relieve coarctation/obstruction
- **Pulmonary blood flow source:**
  - Classic Norwood: Modified BT shunt (brachiocephalic artery)
  - Sano modification: RV→PA conduit (left of neo-aorta)
  - Birmingham modification: RV→PA conduit (right of neo-aorta)
- **Atrial septectomy:** Ensure unrestricted pulmonary venous return
- **Target saturations:** 75–80% (higher → pulmonary over-circulation)

### Stage II Palliation (BCPS) — age 3–6 months
- Disconnect previous shunt/conduit
- **Bidirectional cavopulmonary shunt (BCPS):** SVC → RPA
- Alternative: **Hemi-Fontan** (SVC not disconnected from RA)
- Additional procedures: PA stenosis relief, TV repair, relief of subpulmonary stenosis/coarctation/pulmonary vein stenosis

### Stage III Palliation (Fontan/TCPC) — age 3–5 years
- Connect IVC to pulmonary arteries → total cavopulmonary connection (TCPC)
- **Extracardiac TCPC:** Gore-Tex® tube from IVC to PA outside RA (no arrest, some without CPB)
- **Lateral tunnel Fontan:** Intra-atrial tunnel (requires arrest)
- **Intracardiac TCPC:** Tube through atrial cavity (for heterotaxy, dextrocardia, anomalous PV return)

### Hybrid Procedure
- **Alternative to Norwood** for low birth weight (<2.5 kg) or postnatal collapse
- Maintain ductal patency with **stent or PGE₁**
- Control PBF with **bilateral PA bands** (median sternotomy) or percutaneous flow limiters
- **Rashkind septostomy** for wide interatrial communication
- No cardiopulmonary bypass in neonatal period
- Stage I Norwood delayed 4–6 weeks, or stages I+II combined at 4–6 months

### Comparison of Stage I Techniques
| Feature | Classic Norwood | Sano Modification |
|---|---|---|
| PBF source | BT shunt (systemic artery) | RV→PA conduit |
| Diastolic steal | Yes (coronary steal) | No |
| Ventricular incision | No (only RA) | Yes (RV incision) |
| PA distortion | Less | More (left-sided; Birmingham modification improved) |
| Postoperative stability | Less stable | More stable |
| 1-year survival (trial 2005–2008) | Inferior | Superior (benefit lost by 3–5 years) |

### Indications and Contraindications
**Absolute indication:** Diagnosis alone (near-certain death without intervention)

**Relative contraindications (increased risk):**
- Extreme prematurity: <32 weeks (very high risk); 32–37 weeks (moderate risk)
- Low birth weight: 2.5–3 kg (caution); <2.5 kg (increasing risk)
- Poor RV function: Severe IAS restriction, moderate/severe TR (structural > functional), PV dysfunction, obstructed PV drainage
- Rhythm disturbances: Congenital AV block, absence of sinus rhythm
- Non-cardiac: Major neurological injury, syndromic abnormalities, NEC, diaphragmatic hernia, pulmonary lymphangiectasia, renal agenesis, tracheal stenosis

### Peri-operative Mortality
- **Stage I:** 10–15% (high-volume, high-quality programmes)
- **Inter-stage attrition:** Reduced by home monitoring
- **Stage II (BCPS):** 2–3%
- **Stage III (Fontan):** 2–4%
- **Survival at 1 year:** 70–75%
- **Longest survivors:** 35–40 years (very few)

### Morbidity
**Stage I:** All complications of neonatal cardiac surgery with CPB

**Stage II:**
- Prolonged pleural drainage / chylothorax
- Cyanosis (elevated PVR)
- Diaphragmatic palsy (requires plication)
- Pulmonary artery stenosis (requires intervention)
- Pulmonary artery thrombosis (especially with bilateral SVC)

**Stage III (Fontan):**
- Thrombosis of Fontan circuit (elevated PVR, slow flow)
- Paradoxical embolism (via patent fenestration)

### Long-term Prognosis
**Fontan complications (emerging from teenage years onward):**
- Arrhythmias (atrial fibrillation/flutter) — less common with modern tunnels
- Pulmonary arterial stenosis
- Tricuspid valve dysfunction
- Residual/emergent aortic arch obstruction
- Deteriorating cardiac function (diastolic dysfunction nearly universal)
- Protein-losing enteropathy, ascites, hepatic congestion/cirrhosis, plastic bronchitis

**Interventions:** TV replacement, dilatation/stenting of PA or arch stenosis

**Definitive therapy:** Heart transplantation (limited by donor shortage)

**Destination therapy:** Wearable VADs in selected single-ventricle patients

---

## Chapter 27: Congenital Abnormalities of the Mitral Valve

### Function and Anatomy
- **Mitral valve function:** Opens in diastole (LA → LV), closes in systole (prevents regurgitation).
- **Embryology:** Development begins 4th–5th week, complete by 15th week. Endocardial cushions fuse at 6th week → lateral cushion → posterior leaflet, superior cushion → anterior leaflet. Papillary muscles form by delamination (completes ~17 weeks).
- **Normal components:** Annulus, two leaflets (anterior semi-circular, posterior quadrangular with 3 scallops P1–P3), two papillary muscles (anterolateral, posteromedial), multiple chordae tendineae.

### Classification (Carpentier)
| Type | Leaflet motion | Aetiology |
|---|---|---|
| I | Normal | Posterior leaflet defect, cleft MV, annular dilatation |
| II | Excessive | Elongated/absent cords, elongated papillary muscle, excess tissue |
| III | Restricted | Lack of tissue, papillary muscle–commissure fusion, parachute MV, hammock MV |

### Specific Anomalies
**Leaflet:**
- Double-orifice MV (Types I and II) — associated with AVSD
- Imperforate leaflet (mitral atresia) — with aortic atresia (HLHS)
- Isolated cleft MV — anterior leaflet, failure of AV cushion fusion
- Straddling leaflets, posterior leaflet hypoplasia

**Papillary muscle/chordal:**
- Parachute MV — single papillary muscle, associated with Shone syndrome (ASD, VSD, coarctation, LVOT obstruction)
- Asymmetric dominant papillary muscles (Types I–III)
- Hammock MV (mitral arcade) — papillary muscles extend to leaflet edges

**Annular:**
- Supramitral/intramitral rings (band-type or membrane-type)
- Isolated annular dilatation (with LV dilatation or ALCAPA)

### Epidemiology and Associated Defects
- **Incidence:** 0.4% of CHD, 5/100,000 general population
- **Male predominance:** 1.5–2.2:1
- **Associated defects (96%):** PDA, VSD, coarctation, conotruncal abnormalities, Shone syndrome

### Pathophysiology
- **Stenosis:** ↑ LAP → LA dilatation/hypertrophy → pulmonary oedema → RV overload → pulmonary hypertension
- **Regurgitation:** ↑ LV and LA volumes → chamber dilatation/hypertrophy → elevated pulmonary vascular pressures
- Concomitant defects: ASD alleviates stenosis manifestations; VSD worsens them

### Clinical Presentation
**Symptoms:** Dyspnoea, poor feeding, failure to thrive, wheezing/recurrent infections, syncope/aphonia (from LA compression)
**Signs:** Normal peripheral exam, LV dilatation (palpable in MR), blowing pansystolic murmur (MR) or mid-diastolic murmur (MS), RV heave (if PH), tachycardia/poor perfusion (if failure)

### Diagnosis
- **ECG:** Sinus rhythm, LA enlargement, 1° AV block, RVH (PH), LV enlargement (MR)
- **CXR:** Increased bronchial angle (LA enlargement), pulmonary oedema, ventricular enlargement
- **TTE:** Primary modality — 2D for anatomy, Doppler for stenosis/regurgitation
- **TOE:** When TTE windows suboptimal; 3D echo for enhanced imaging
- **MRI:** Ventricular volumes/function (valve anatomy less detailed)
- **Catheterisation:** Rarely for isolated disease; useful for associated anomalies and haemodynamics
- **Fluoroscopy:** Assess implanted mechanical valves

### Mitral Valve Prolapse (MVP)
- **Definition:** Leaflet bows into LA (usually posterior leaflet)
- **Aetiology:** Myxomatous proliferation of spongy layer, acid mucopolysaccharide deposition
- **Incidence:** 2.4% general population; autosomal dominant in non-syndromal cases
- **Associated syndromes:** Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos, osteogenesis imperfecta, cutis laxa, pseudoxanthoma elasticum
- **Symptoms:** Dyspnoea, fatigue, dizziness, palpitations, non-exertional chest pain
- **Signs:** Late systolic murmur following mid-systolic click
- **ECG:** Inverted T waves (inferior/lateral), prolonged QTc, SVT/VT
- **Prognosis:** Usually benign; complications include endocarditis, heart failure, sudden death

### Indications for Surgery
**Stenosis:**
- Poorly tolerated symptoms or evidence of pulmonary hypertension
- Delay intervention in neonates/infants if possible (valve grows in first months)
- 5× greater mortality than MR → often requires earlier surgery
- Repair can be palliative until child is old enough for replacement

**Regurgitation:**
- Symptoms present or progressive
- Secondary effects: arrhythmia, progressive ventricular dilatation, pulmonary hypertension

### Non-Surgical Interventions
- **Medical:** Diuretics (stenosis and regurgitation), ACE inhibitors (MR)
- **Balloon angioplasty:** Limited experience in congenital MS (used in rheumatic disease)

### Surgical Repair — Stenosis
- **Exposure:** Vertical/transverse transatrial septal incision, or left atrial incision at interatrial groove (± SVC division)
- **Techniques:**
  - Papillary muscle splitting/fenestration (parachute MV)
  - Patch augmentation of posterior leaflet (detach from annulus, augment with pericardium)
  - Leaflet thinning (dissect fibroelastic layer)
  - Excision of supramitral/intramitral rings
- **Avoid:** Supra-annular valve replacement (worse immediate/long-term results)

### Surgical Repair — Regurgitation
- **Annuloplasty:** Pericardium (preferred in children), partial annuloplasty (allows growth), prosthetic ring (only in older children)
- **Chordal reconstruction:** PTFE
- **Cleft repair:** Running/interrupted sutures + annuloplasty if residual regurgitation
- **Elongated chordae:** Shortening or transfer
- **Elongated papillary muscles:** Shortening or reconstruction

### Replacement Options
| Type | Pros | Cons |
|---|---|---|
| **Bioprosthetic/homograft** | No anticoagulation | Accelerated degeneration (≈1 decade) |
| **Mechanical** | Durable, few thromboembolic events | Requires lifelong anticoagulation; reoperation for growth |
| **Melody valve** | Percutaneously dilatable as child grows | Originally for pulmonary position; adapted for MV |
| **Ross II** | No anticoagulation; potentially less structural deterioration | Does not grow; replaces two valves |

### Risks and Complications
**Common to all MV surgery:** Heart block (pacemaker), endocarditis, stroke

**Repair:** Residual stenosis/regurgitation (may progress, require reoperation)

**Replacement:**
- Prosthetic valve thrombosis, haemolysis, perivalvular leak, thromboembolism
- Anticoagulation complications (mechanical valves)
- Reoperation for growth and structural deterioration
- Patient-prosthesis mismatch (predictor of early/late death)

### Long-Term Outcomes
- **Stenosis vs regurgitation:** MS has worse prognosis; parachute MV is risk factor for worse outcome
- **Repair within first year of life:** Risk factor for persistent MV dysfunction
- **MR repair vs replacement:** Repair → lower rates of heart block, thromboembolism, endocarditis
- **10-year survival (MS):** 40% (medical), 56% (surgical)
- **At age 19 (after repair):** Freedom from MVR 82%, freedom from reintervention 91%

### Emerging Technologies
- **Melody valve:** Catheter-based, expandable as child grows
- **Tissue-engineered valves:** Potential for growth, avoiding anticoagulation and frequent replacements
- **Advanced imaging:** Better operative planning, earlier characterisation

---

## Chapter 28: Left Ventricular Outflow Tract Lesions

### Anatomy of the LVOT and Aortic Root
- **LVOT components:** Subaortic (anterior MV leaflet, membranous/muscular septum), aortic valve (three semilunar cusps, sinuses of Valsalva, coronary ostia), supravalvular (sinotubular junction to ascending aorta)
- **Aortic annulus:** No true anatomic structure; corresponds to lowest points of leaflet attachment (three-pointed crown) — "virtual basal ring"
- **Sinotubular junction (STJ):** Where commissure tips meet ascending aorta; normally narrower than mid-sinus level
- **Embryology:** Semilunar valves form 4th week; neural crest cells form endocardial cushions; spiral separation into aorta and PA

### Classification of LVOT Lesions by Sector
| Sector | Stenosis | Regurgitation | Dilatation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Subvalvular | Membrane, HOCM, tunnel, accessory tissue | — | — |
| Valvular | BAV, UAV, quadricuspid | BAV, connective tissue diseases | Root dilatation (Marfan, BAV aortopathy) |
| Supravalvular | Williams syndrome (hourglass/diffuse) | — | Post-stenotic |

### Subaortic LVOTO
- **Discrete subaortic membrane:** Not present at birth, develops later; fibrous ring 3–5 mm below aortic valve
- **Other causes:** HOCM, accessory MV tissue/chordae, SAM, posterior septal deviation (with VSD), tunnel LVOTO, AVSD morphology

### Valvular Aortic Stenosis (Most Common)
- **Bicuspid aortic valve (BAV):** 1–2% of population; male predominance 2:1; may function normally for decades
- **Unicuspid (UAV):** All three commissures fused → "windsock" or "keyhole" orifice
- **Quadricuspid:** Extremely rare (usually truncus arteriosus)

#### Anatomical Classification of Aortic Valve Cusp Lesions
| Feature | Quadricuspid | Tricuspid | Bicuspid | Unicuspid |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Number of cusps | 4 | 3 | 2 (1 full, 1–2 hypoplastic) | 1 |
| Number of commissures | 4 | 3 | 2 (1–2 full, 1 hypoplastic) | 1 |
| Number of raphes | 0 | 0 | 1–2 | 1 |

### Congenital Aortic Regurgitation
- Isolated congenital AR is rare
- **Causes:** BAV leaflet stretch, connective tissue diseases (Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos, Turner), infective endocarditis, aortoventricular tunnel, post-intervention complication

### Supravalvular Aortic Stenosis
- Rare at birth; appears in early childhood
- **Williams syndrome:** Gene defect; associated with PA branch stenosis, coronary ostial stenosis
- **Morphology:** Hourglass (discrete) or diffuse (extending to arch)
- **Coronary risk:** Ostial stenosis, kinking of LMCA, membrane extending into sinuses

### Associated Abnormalities
- **Shone complex:** Parachute MV, supravalvular mitral ring, subaortic stenosis, coarctation
- **Endocardial fibroelastosis (EFE):** Fibrous replacement of endocardium
- **HLHS:** Extreme end of LVOTO spectrum
- Post-repair: Subaortic membrane after coarctation/arch repair; LVOT obstruction after AVSD repair

### Aetiology and Pathophysiology
- **Flow theory:** Abnormal foetal blood flow inhibits left heart development
- **Progressive nature:** Foetal AS at 18–20 weeks may progress to aortic atresia/HLHS by term
- **Foetal circulation:** LVOTO well-tolerated (systemic circulation via ductus)
- **Postnatal:** Ductal closure → LV must support systemic circulation; severe obstruction → low cardiac output, acidosis, death

### Clinical Presentation
- **Severe neonatal AS (gradient >30 mmHg):** Low cardiac output, acidosis, poor perfusion, death unless ductus maintained
- **Moderate AS:** Poor feeding, sweating on feeds, FTT, tachycardia, tachypnoea, diminished femoral pulses, systolic murmur
- **Mild AS:** Asymptomatic, incidental murmur
- **AR:** Dyspnoea, orthopnoea, PND, wide pulse pressure, diastolic murmur, hyper-pulsatile arteries

### Natural History
- **Critical AS/HLHS with restrictive IAS:** Death within hours without treatment
- **Severe AS/HLHS:** High first-year mortality; survivors develop PVD
- **Mild AS/BAV:** Progressive stenosis over decades; LV hypertrophy → risk of lethal arrhythmias; late symptoms: dyspnoea, fatigue, angina, syncope (dangerous)

### Diagnostic Imaging
| Modality | Key Findings |
|---|---|
| **ECG** | LVH, strain pattern (late), ventricular ectopics |
| **CXR** | Often unremarkable; may show cardiomegaly, plethoric lungs (severe failure) |
| **Echocardiography** | Mainstay. Assess: level of obstruction, mechanism (membrane, valve morphology, septal deviation), severity (Doppler), AR, associated anomalies, LV function/hypertrophy/EFE |
| **MRI** | Anatomy, obstruction level, valve morphology, root size, regurgitant fraction, LV volumes/function |
| **CT** | Superior for infants (fast acquisition); 3D reconstruction, coronary anatomy, root sizing, arch anomalies |
| **Catheterisation** | Rarely diagnostic; used for balloon valvuloplasty (neonates), coronary assessment (Williams) |

### Indications for Intervention
- **Subvalvular (isolated):** Symptoms, peak gradient >50 mmHg, LVH on echo; CPET if symptoms vague
- **Neonatal valvular AS:** Gradient >30 mmHg (poor perfusion after ductal closure); intervention almost always indicated
- **Child/adult valvular AS:** Symptoms, peak gradient >50 mmHg, Doppler velocity >3.5 m/s
- **Aortic regurgitation:** Symptoms not controlled medically, progressive LVEDD increase, CPET limitation

### Treatment — Neonates and Infants
**"Resuscitate, Review, Repair, Recover, Rehabilitate"**
- **Resuscitate:** PGE₁ (reopen ductus), inotropes, ventilation, renal support
- **Review:** Correct acidosis, improve LV function
- **Repair options:** Balloon valvuloplasty (favoured) vs open valvotomy
- **Outcomes:** Comparable survival; balloon → greater gradient reduction but more AR; open → less AR but higher residual gradient
- **Recover/Rehabilitate:** All interventions non-definitive; reintervention inevitable

### Treatment — Children and Adults
- **Balloon valvuloplasty:** Minimal leaflet calcification, no more than mild AR
- **Open valvotomy:** First intervention option
- **Repair techniques:** Leaflet resuspension (Trusler, plication, free edge reinforcement), leaflet reconstruction (patch repair, augmentation), bicuspidisation/tricuspidisation (raphe resection, commissurotomy)
- **Replacement:** Ross/Ross-Konno (preferred in growing children — growth potential, no anticoagulation), homograft/bioprosthetic (no growth, limited durability), mechanical (durable but lifelong anticoagulation)
- **Avoid prosthetic replacement** until valve suitable for adult life can be implanted

### Supravalvular AS Repair
- **Anaesthesia risk:** Restricted coronary filling, possible proximal coronary stenoses
- **Techniques:** Transverse aortotomy, inspect coronaries, shave thickened media
- **Patch repair:** McGoon (one sinus), Doty inverted Y (two sinuses), Brom (three sinuses)
- **Diffuse disease:** Augment whole ascending aorta ± arch

### Severe Multi-Level LVOTO / HLHS
- Single-ventricle palliation (Norwood, hybrid, transplantation) or comfort care/termination
- If LV adequate and MV normal → attempt biventricular circulation
- **Tunnel LVOTO:** Ross-Konno procedure (resection + pulmonary autograft root replacement)

### Surgical Techniques
- **Approach:** Median sternotomy, CPB, cardioplegic arrest
- **Subvalvular:** Through aortic valve (or tricuspid/AVSD defect). Peel membrane (extends to MV and aortic leaflets). Myectomy confined to muscle between left and right commissures (avoid bundle of His). Remove accessory chordae (preserve MV function). EFE resection (indications unclear)
- **Prosthetic AVR:** Implant largest possible valve; annular enlargement (Nicks, Manougian, Konno) may be needed; protect coronaries and bundle of His

### Ross and Ross-Konno Operations
- Replace aortic valve with pulmonary autograft → growth potential, no anticoagulation
- Harvest pulmonary autograft with RVOT muscle tongue; incise between LVOT and RVOT across infundibular septum; implant autograft; reimplant coronary buttons
- Replace pulmonary valve with homograft (most common) or other valved conduit
- **Not suitable for:** BAV aortopathy, Marfan, other connective tissue diseases

### Bentall Operation (Root Replacement)
- Mainly ACHD population (Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos, Turner, BAV aortopathy)
- **Special challenges:** Post-arterial switch (10% develop root dilatation/AR), post-truncus/DORV/Rastelli (calcified VSD patch), post-Fontan (coronaries difficult to locate), situs inversus/isomerism (coronary anomalies)

### Valve-Sparing Root Replacement
| Feature | Yacoub Remodelling | David V Reimplantation |
|---|---|---|
| Annulus distensibility | + | – |
| Cusp motion | ++ | (+) |
| Valve stability | + | + |
| STJ stability | + | + |
| LV-aortic junction stability | – | + |
- **David:** Resect sinuses, harvest coronaries, sleeve Dacron® tube over commissures, anchor to annulus, reimplant coronaries
- **Yacoub:** Tailor graft to replace sinuses, suture to aortic wall, reimplant coronaries; annulus remains outside graft → risk of continued dilatation

### Personalised External Aortic Root Support (PEARS)
- For root dilatation with minimal/mild AR (Marfan, similar aortopathies)
- Polymer mesh jacket made from 3D CT model; placed around root and ascending aorta (beating heart, no CPB)
- Jacket can be made 20% smaller than aorta → abolishes/reduces mild AR
- **Outcomes:** >200 cases, 16 years, no rupture/dissection; operative mortality 1%

### Complications of LVOT Surgery
- Bleeding, heart block (bundle of His damage), residual/recurrent stenosis (membrane, tunnel)
- Postoperative low output (poor LV diastolic compliance, especially with EFE)
- AR (leaflet prolapse after commissural incision)
- Aneurysm (patch dilatation after supravalvular repair)
- Myocardial ischaemia (coronary distortion in supravalvular AS repair)

### Long-Term Results
| Lesion | Key Outcomes |
|---|---|
| **Neonatal AS — Balloon** | 70% survival at 10 years; 26% freedom from reintervention; 61% freedom from moderate AR |
| **Neonatal AS — Open valvotomy** | 70–90% survival at 10 years; 66–78% freedom from reintervention; up to 90% freedom from moderate AR (case-dependent) |
| **Ross in infancy** | Operative mortality <10% (older children 1–3%); 95% survival at 5 years; freedom from AR 90% (2y) → 70% (5y); freedom from reintervention 37% at 5 years (mostly RV-PA conduit) |
| **Supravalvular AS** | Operative mortality 2–3%; residual gradient 15–20% (diffuse disease); 80% survival at 10 years; freedom from late reoperation 90% (10y) → 80% (20y) |
| **Discrete subaortic stenosis** | Operative mortality <5%; reoperation 10–15%; late AR up to 20%; 10-year survival >90% |
| **Tunnel LVOTO** | Higher operative risk, worse survival and freedom from reoperation than discrete |

---

## Chapter 29: Truncus Arteriosus

### Definition and Anatomy
- **Truncus arteriosus (common arterial trunk):** Single great artery arises from ventricular mass, gives origin to coronary, systemic, and pulmonary circulations (Figure 1)
